madam president, in 1987 the australian rock band midnight oil had a worldwide hit with their song 'beds are burning', a denunciation of ecological disasters and a call for urgent action. the words, in connection with the sad news that affects us today, come to mind. how can we dance when our earth is turning? how do we sleep while our beds are burning? thirty years later, the tragic situation in australia foreshadows what the world will be like if the paris agreement is not strictly respected. what role for europe beyond solidarity? this year, europe is expected to conclude a free trade agreement with australia. it is therefore the time, if not the time, to make the sustainable development chapter binding on both sides. let's not forget that while australia ratified the paris agreement, several generations of climate-skeptical political leaders have supported the mining sector, which is the engine of the economy, but also and above all, responsible for the destruction of biodiversity, the denial of aboriginal rights and which has placed australia among the countries with the highest per capita co2 emissions. it is therefore time for the commission and commissioner hogan, in the context of the current trade negotiations, to do what is announced in the green deal for europe and to put in place ambitious climate and environmental diplomacy also through its trade policy.
madam president, who else knows this problem? over the years, we have all accumulated a bunch of different mobile chargers, and that’s not only because we switched brands. often newer models of the same brand also came with a different charger. what is the result? more than 51 000 tonnes of so—called e-waste per year, according to the commission estimates. the environment does not benefit from this. when we forget our charger, only people that have the same mobile phone can help us out so the consumer does not benefit from this either. only the industry does, some companies more than others, as we all know, because the larger part of the industry is moving towards usb c, but some stubborn pupils in the classroom keep on refusing. we cannot let the interests of private profit overrule the interests of our people and the planet. when what is good for the consumer coincides with what is good for the environment, we need to seize that opportunity. in conclusion, commissioner, please come forward with a sustainable solution as fast as possible. we have already lost too much time.
the gender pay gap remains a persistent challenge. by 2020, women still earn less than men, and the inequality is even more pronounced in retirement. i would like to make a few comments on the report by mr de gucht. women are more likely to have part-time jobs, which automatically lead to lower wages. this puts them in a vulnerable position and puts them at greater risk of poverty. the reduction of the gender pay gap therefore presupposes the equal distribution of care tasks and also presupposes equal participation in the labour market: equal pay for equal work. the full implementation of the marianne thyssen work-life balance directive will therefore be of crucial importance. secondly, segregation in the labour market must be eliminated, both vertically and horizontally. breaking gender stereotypes and empowering women towards stem and digital sectors is the message. reducing the wage gap will make women stronger and more resilient.

mr president, homelessness is unacceptable. recent estimates even suggest that 700,000 people are homeless. housing first offers a unique opportunity to provide a roof over the head for homeless people, often with complex needs. there are many projects in various member states and this approach has proven to work in practice. already, many homeless people have been guided to a stable housing situation. it also creates opportunities to overcome health, mental and financial problems, to re-enter the labour market or to access education. a policy on homelessness goes beyond housing. health must also come first. will the announced child guarantee also reach homeless children, commissioner? the recommendations of the social protection committee on tackling homelessness are clear: a multidisciplinary approach across different policy areas. we must work on these recommendations urgently. we're counting on an ambitious plan.

the honorable chairman. europe has decided to respond to the climate situation, to climate change, and this is our green deal. the green deal has a price, and today we see and have before us the fund for equitable development. i'm all for being fair and decent to people and helping them out when they're in trouble. we're saving the mining sector, first and foremost. in slovenia, where i come from, we still generate about a third of our electricity from coal or lignite, so it is right that we help these regions. however, these regions will not be the only ones to pay the price of the green deal, and it is therefore right to envisage very concretely, in the light of the forthcoming financial framework, other needs and measures related to both new energy infrastructure, the transformation of sectors such as the automotive sector, as well as the other steel sectors and the like. it is right that we should not forget other policies either, and i expect that, as part of the adoption of the multiannual financial framework, we will have the full cost of the green deal on the table and that we will also find quality resources to cover this bill.

i have to say, madam president, mr. vice president, i think we all agree that we need an energy transition, that we need fewer finite resources, that we need more renewable resources, and that we also need less carbon emissions. but i think the danger we face - and many of my colleagues here have told me this - is that we will end up as the european union that closed the coal mines, that depopulated the countryside and that ultimately contributed to the loss of jobs. and in this case, we can't tap, because a worker who has worked in the coal mine for decades will be relatively indifferent to the fact that we are creating new opportunities. it's not that easy for him. and that is why it is imperative that we take a forward-looking approach. we must promote biotechnology, we must promote renewable resources, and we must ensure that this just transition fund does not become a fund for shutdowns, for abandonment, for depopulation, but a fund for new technologies, for the future, for biotechnology and, ultimately, for rural landscapes.
madam president! the name of the just transformation fund itself suggests that the funds should be allocated efficiently, but also fairly. fairly, that is, in proportion to the social burden borne by regions that are moving away from coal and coal-fired power. and here the map of europe is very diverse. for historical reasons, it's rarely the fault of society, but more often the fault of those who rule. and that's why we have to be smart about the green berets: "yes, but". because europe is not only going to be green, it is also going to be competitive. because if europe is to lead the world, it must succeed, not fail. this requires a very strong financial backstop for many european regions. and that shield is just emerging. it's foggy out there. if the facts are convincing, then we can all say with one voice, "it's the weekend".

mr president, in my remote northern england region, in a proud community, generations of men and boys slaved underground, mining for coal, and worked in iron and steel foundries, forging component parts for railways, factory ships and a thousand other products that helped to build our national infrastructure and generate trade, so i know exactly what happens when, with one fell swoop, an industry disappears, with no thought as to what might replace it. in post-industrial britain, under thatcher’s government, the skilled ironmasters and steelworkers of county durham were offered little more than precarious low-paid work on a factory production line, putting potato crisps into packets eight hours a day, five days a week, for example. these men – strong, decent, proud – became broken beings, emasculated, their loss of dignity obvious in their dull eyes and their spreading girth. what a difference it would have made to these men, to my community, if we had had a just transition fund. decent work confers a sense of dignity, of self-worth.

in writing. the green transition will affect all segments of our lives, and one of the most challenging sectors is transport. road and public transport are an integral part of our daily lives, so it is not a small question how these areas will change in the coming decades. one of the biggest sufferers of air pollution is the population living in cities and urban areas. modernization and decarbonisation of public transport can go a long way towards solving this problem. the european union must help member states, both financially and otherwise, to provide public transport for citizens that is clean, easily accessible and affordable. another important issue is access to alternative fuels across the european union. the development and deployment of alternative, clean fuels infrastructure in the member states is a prerequisite for a fair green transition in road transport. we need to focus on the lagging, less developed regions so that the green transition does not lead to new fault lines between the less developed and the developed regions. finally, it is important to say that the green transition can only be fair if we do not allow it to happen at the expense of european citizens, taxpayers. the biggest polluters have to bear the brunt of the burden.
in writing. the proposal for a european minimum wage, coupled with a new social action plan, is an important step forward. one in ten workers is on the verge of poverty. that figure has been rising for ten years. to regain the confidence of citizens, europe must be more than a market. never before have so many people been at work in europe: 241 million people. yet too many employees struggle to make ends meet. low wages and the wide gap between eu countries, from €1.62 per hour in bulgaria to €11.97 per hour in luxembourg, are pushing millions of working europeans into poverty, but also generating resentment about wage competition and social dumping within the european market. a european minimum wage framework should put the social partners at the centre and take account of the situation in each member state. this means that the same minimum wage should not apply to every worker in the eu. the european semester should be an annual process, with the social partners, towards more decent wages and less inequality within europe. europe is killing two birds with one stone: strengthening social consultation, while respecting national wage formation and traditions.

madam president, please. the main objective of our foreign security policy must be to guarantee the security of our citizens. this requires the eradication of terrorism, the effective protection of our external borders, the cessation of illegal migration, the prevention of new waves of migration and the avoidance of regional conflicts. all of this is necessary to preserve peace and stability. the implementation of a common european defence system is welcome in the common security policy. but in the meantime, strengthening our defence capabilities requires efforts by member states. that's why my country is currently undergoing a massive military development program, and by 2024, we will be spending two percent of our gdp on defense. the role of the western balkans and the trust of its peoples is crucial for the union, and enlargement must be taken seriously, with accession negotiations with albania and north macedonia opening as soon as possible. i must say, however, that i am not in favour of moving to qualified majority voting, since this would deprive the smaller member states in particular of the opportunity to express their sovereign national interests on an equal footing in matters of importance to them.
mr. chairman, i would like to congratulate the rapporteurs on a job well done. the report on the implementation of the common foreign and security policy was adopted by consensus. the role of the european union is highlighted not only in promoting peace and security but also as a responsible actor in a deteriorating global environment. as regards the report on the implementation of the common security and defence policy, i hope that we will adopt that report in a similar way in the future. i support the continuation of work on the development of european strategic capacities. in the face of growing instability, as the first weeks of this year have shown, the european union must act faster, stronger and more constructively, in the spirit of multilateralism, to promote the core european values of peace, cooperation and progress. the response to the many challenges is strategic autonomy, with an emphasis on the need for the union to better coordinate existing and continue to develop its own security and defence capabilities. i believe that more emphasis should be placed on the western balkans as a priority of the common foreign and security and defence policy. a stable neighbourhood is the key to a stable european union. activity, cooperation and a european perspective are the messages we need to send to our partners in the south east. i am confident that our new high representative will also be committed to a strong europe in the multilateral world, because the challenges are many and our citizens expect decisive and timely action, of course in line with our principles.
mr president, i hear a lot of expensive words and fine principles here, but mostly a lot of talk of the devil. the greatest threats to our security are uncontrolled mass immigration and islamization and the increasing number of terrorist attacks that result from this mass immigration. the eu's security, defence and foreign policy policies to date have not reduced these threats, on the contrary. we therefore propose not to undermine the sovereignty of the member states and not to impose coercive policies from the eu. there must of course be cooperation on material and so on. there should be an exchange of information between member states. all of which is effective in combating, as i said, the big threats: the european union is not a party to the conflict. so our recommendations are clear: the european union must not expand further, stop rapprochement with turkey and work more closely with partners such as russia and the united states.

madam president, the hotbeds of instability are multiplying around us. this new reality highlights the need for a single foreign and defence and security policy. unfortunately, we are far from that goal, with the responsibility of powerful states, and this has very serious consequences today. you have inadvertently allowed matters of the utmost importance to the security of the peoples of europe to be handled by trump, putin and erdoğan. some of you dismiss this view, because you simply do not border countries that do not respect international law. greece, for example, spends a very significant part of its budget on equipment, reducing funding for the welfare state, health and education. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european union is able to take on the burden of protecting its borders against common threats. it's time to do what you haven't done before. it's about justice and solidarity. the union must become strategically autonomous and develop its own defence structures. what seems like a utopia today is a one-way street for the future.

madam president, mobility policies are crucial to the quality of life of people, to the functioning of the economy and to the reduction of human impacts on the environment. in the perspective of economic, social and territorial cohesion, account must necessarily be taken of mobility needs at local, regional and national level. the european union has been placing an increasing focus on so-called transeuropean networks under pressure from the major economies that dominate the single market, while reducing the resources needed for what is still to be done, and a lot, at the level of local, regional and national mobility, especially in areas such as rail or metro in cities. the so-called european green deal, despite much publicity, fails blatantly on the question of mobility. there is no sustainable mobility without a strong commitment to public transport, without the massive shift of users from individual to collective public transport. well, not once does the so-called european green deal mention public transport. we hope that the forthcoming mobility strategy will correct these distortions.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner vălean, europe certainly needs to be at the forefront of a transport paradigm that is able to reconcile efficiency and environmental sustainability. but in catalonia, when we think about the future of transport, the first thing that comes to mind is the mediterranean corridor. the social and economic actors in both catalonia and valencia have never ceased to insist on and work on the need for this connection, which is highly strategic for europe. it has been included in the ten-t for ten years, but it has not yet been completed. we have seen the spanish state put a brake on investment for some time now; we have not had a comprehensive master plan for the decade 2020-2030, from france to algeciras; we have not yet known the date of the new connection to the port of barcelona, which has been planned for more than twelve years, the new terminal of the old llobregat canal, and so on. the commission's speeches are always welcome and important, but regulations are useless if they are not enforced. they have no real effect unless they are followed by good implementation by the member states. we hope that the european commission will be really firm in this respect.
i have to say, madam president, thank you very much for joining us today, commissioner. we're talking about the future of mobility. i would like to address a matter of current concern to the citizens of europe, and on which i have received countless questions from my constituents in recent months. that's the theme of the environmental plaques. we see many cities in europe, in the european union, introducing their own systems. we have this in strasbourg, for example. if you, like me, drive to strasbourg week, you have to apply for a plaque. if you drive here to brussels, like me, you had to register here, and we have that in countless cities across the european union. we, the european people's party, have asked the commission on several occasions in recent years - your predecessors asked us - whether this is a) in accordance with european rules and b) whether this patchwork is in the spirit of our european union. many citizens fear that if they drive into cities, they will be hit with heavy fines if they do not register. registration is not always easy either. and here is my request: in the light of all the far-sightedness we are hearing about the various issues here today, all of which are important, that we also take these current issues into account and that you, as the new commissioner, - i have met you in such a way that you really want to work for the good of the citizens - take up this issue and give us feedback and check it.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the green deal cannot and must not be a mere political advertisement. we are fully aware of the climate challenges we face and there is an urgent need to correct the course of community policies quickly and effectively. i call on the commission to take a more ambitious and determined approach, starting with eurovignette, which is blocked by national logic that is at odds with the long-term vision of the green deal. we all know that transport is responsible for a quarter of the union's emissions. specific investments are needed in rail, air and sea transport. in addition, the commission's current position on intermodal transport is still not sufficient. parliament has already reiterated that a greater economic effort is needed to achieve the 2030 targets. i am also very concerned that punishing the biggest polluters could turn into an unfair taxation that falls solely on the final consumer, instead of being an incentive to develop less impactful technologies. once again, the investment required to connect europe's peripheral areas, such as southern italy, appears to be insufficient. we therefore ask that, as parliament has already proposed, there be an increase of 6 billion in the cef mechanism.
madam president, welcome commissioner, efficient, sustainable and clean mobility of people and goods is one of the keys to the competitiveness of our economy. it is an essential tool in the fight against climate change and guarantees freedom of movement. we are leaders in many transportation technologies, and to maintain that position requires us to innovate, to create the world's first integrated mobility system. this requires a commitment to intensive digitalisation, industrial innovation to decarbonise vehicles and connect them to each other, to infrastructure and to users, and to the training of new professionals. we have to evolve in the management of transport modes to start talking about mobility. new business models are going to emerge that will allow users, in a single operation, to solve their needs from the first to the last mile. a transformation that requires, moreover, a change in which we are all protagonists and which starts at the ground level. that is why i insist that it should be a bottom-up effort and that it should rely on the institutions closest to the citizenry to make progress. and of course, the commitments and agreements which are key to this development must be fulfilled: the transeuropean transport networks, the single sky, the galileo network, the railway package; we are committed to the green pact, but it will be only pretty words if the member states continue to block the foundations of this transformation. you must be the star to demand that they do so.
madam president, luxembourg is the first european country where public transport will be free from 1 march. access to practical and efficient travel alternatives is essential to motivate citizens to change their mode of transport. access for all and affordable transport are essential, but other investments are also needed. we need to ensure that the infrastructure is there to make it easy to switch from train to bicycle, from train to electric car or from train to tram. equally necessary is the introduction of reduced fares across europe for low-carbon transport such as the train, especially for students, and the fair taxation of polluting means of transport. finally, we must also strengthen the intra-european railway lines and night trains. this strategy must be an integral part of the green deal. this is a concrete area in which europe must improve the quality of life of its citizens.
madam president, madam commissioner, thank you for the presentation. let me tell you that sustainability is built on three pillars. first, of course, the ecological issue, which was widely discussed in your speech. we must all support the green deal for europe and ensure that in the future goods will be transported more by rail than by road, but also that there is a tax on kerosene. personally, i am entirely in favour of fair taxation and that we can favour certain modes of transport. secondly, sustainability also means the economy, jobs, families and businesses that depend directly on the transport sector. yes, we must ensure that this particular attention to enterprises and employment is included in the strategy to be pursued with the help of digital technologies. finally, there is the social or democratic aspect. yes, transport is a democratic issue for many of our regions: those that are landlocked, those that are neglected, those where citizens cannot move because there is a lack of means of transport, whether public or private. this is a democratic challenge for europe: commissioner, you have a very important role to play in this area, in ensuring that every citizen, in the future, has mobility solutions close to home, close to home, close to work. at that time, yes, faith in europe will grow thanks to the solutions that will be provided by you, the commission and all the actors in the field of mobility. it is therefore also a democratic issue, beyond the ecological and economic issues. madam president, i met david at a demonstration outside here on social security and he told me that in his sector, with certain companies, say a romanian company, temporarily sending a worker to belgium, in this case, social security contributions are paid at the rate of the country of origin, i.e. romania. and this is a problem for it because in a logic of competition, this company has a competitive advantage over belgian companies, in particular, and this becomes an excuse for governments, such as the belgian government, to impose reductions in social contributions on belgian companies in the name of competitiveness. the workers are the turkeys in the hole, because they lose in gross wages and they lose in social security, so a very clear question for the european commission: can we please impose the highest social contribution rate, the most favourable to workers and social security; and when? because the workers need it, thank you.

rapporteur. – madam president, it will be better with the micro, but i have nearly lost my voice so i will try to be clear. i have to urge you, dear colleagues, not to postpone either the debate or the vote. there has already been a proposal in the committee to postpone. it was rejected with a clear majority. the committee chose a compromise: first, to monitor more the implementation of the two agreements, which is also the practice of the international labour organisation (ilo); and, second, to ask for more engagement from the vietnamese authorities. well, they delivered on the implementation of the ratification of the ilo convention. they delivered on – and this is a precedent – a joint committee of our parliament and the national assembly to monitor the implementation of the two agreements. they delivered – and this is also a precedent – on the involvement of our parliament in the human rights dialogue between vietnam and the european union. lastly, they delivered on the independence of the organisations taking part in the domestic advisory groups. so it’s also a question of credibility alongside all the other arguments, of course.
author. – mr president, the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco) formulated a couple of questions regarding automated decision—making processes in anticipation of the european commission’s initiatives on artificial intelligence (ai), which will be presented in parliament next week. for our committee, it is of utmost importance that in any future initiative on the matter, we do guarantee not only the free movement of ai-enabled goods and services, but also the trust of the consumer in these new goods and services because the applications are numerous and encompass virtually all sectors of the internal market. we are witnessing rapid development of ai technology. consumers are being confronted on a daily basis with systems that use automated decision making, such as virtual assistance and chat—bots on websites, and as is the case with all technological advancements, this provides us with opportunities as well as challenges. ai and automated decision making offers great potential in terms of innovative and higher quality products and services, but at the same time, various challenges need to be addressed in order to realise this potential. first and foremost, services and goods using ai and automated decision making create the risk of consumers being misled or discriminated against, for example, in relation to differentiated pricing. will consumers be aware of the fact that the price displayed on their screen was adapted to their estimated purchasing power? the same goes for professional services. if important decisions are automated and thus carried out without sufficient human oversight by highly skilled professionals, are consumers aware that decisions affecting their professional, financial or personal lives are in fact not made by humans, and if they feel wronged, will they be able to demand a human review? will a human, ultimately, be responsible for final decisions or will they be able to reverse them? so my first question to you is: how is the commission planning to ensure that consumers are protected from unfair or discriminatory commercial practices or from potential risks entailed by ai—driven professional services? secondly, we risk that the existing eu product safety and liability frameworks do not adequately cover new ai—enabled products and services. do concepts such as defective products and the legal provisions built around these adequately cover situations in which harm is caused by products operating under automated decision making? as is the case with other products and services, we should ensure that businesses and consumers are protected from harm and receive compensation if it occurs. this is important in order to ensure secured free movement throughout the single market. therefore, our second question is: what initiatives may we expect from the commission side to ensure that the eu safety and liability frameworks are fit for purpose? in that respect, we should ensure that market—surveillance authorities and other competent authorities possess adequate means and powers to act. this is especially important when competent authorities, businesses and consumers do not have access to clear information on how the decision was taken. how will the commission ensure greater transparency in this respect? lastly, talking about biased or unlawfully obtained data sets, the imco committee stresses the importance of respecting regulations such as gdpr when it comes to the collection of data, but also looking at non-personal data use, barriers and requirements. this is something we can and should address at this stage of the design. we should build in these safeguards – privacy by design. so i want to ask: how is the commission planning to ensure that only high quality and unbiased data set are used in automated decision—making processes? dear commissioner, as i said at the beginning, ai offers great potential in many aspects of our daily lives and many fields of the internal market, yet it confronts us with many new and unknown risks. we should address these risks in an adequate and timely manner because the trust of the consumer will be crucial for the acceptance of these new technologies in our society and economy. i thank you in advance for your answers on these very topical and societally important questions and i, and with me the whole imco committee, look forward to the presentation of your plans next week.
mr president, commissioner, i think we need to change the rules urgently. today you are presenting a list of a few dozen investments in polluting fossil gas. billions of euros in energy that, in the end, can kill, because it is poison for the environment, poison for our planet, poison for our air. you're proposing that we invest our money, taxpayers' money, in poison, rather than taking that money and putting it into renewables, into clean energy. i don't understand that. there's a second aspect that bothers me. last week i spoke to your colleague from the commission, i asked him the question and what did he say? first, he told me not to take what he wrote too seriously. then he told me you were going to raise the taxes. that is unacceptable to me. we're gonna sell poison to people and we're gonna tax them on that poison. they'll pay twice. please, stop this, change the rules.

madam president, i would like to talk about financial services, which i believe are crucial for future relations with the uk. we know that an fta can have only a very small chapter on financial services, addressing only the right of establishment and prudential carve-out mechanism, and eu financial services are about the single market, and it is clear now that it will be an equivalence mechanism that will provide the main framework for the relationship. the commission has just a few months to prepare all those unilateral equivalence decisions – around forty, i understand – and the financial industry should be fully aware of how little time they have to adjust to the future situation. efforts have been made recently, also in this house, to enhance the equivalence framework as the main regulatory tool for financial services in the future relationship with the uk. as equivalence is a unilateral discretionary policy tool, in order to make it deliver, we will need constructive cooperation with uk regulators and supervisors to protect the financial stability and integrity of our financial markets, protect investors and consumers, as well as ensuring lpf. but all of us here in this house must also be aware that for an ambitious, beneficial and successful future relationship with the uk in the area of financial services, it is our duty also to complete as soon as possible the banking union and build a genuine capital market union. the eu will preserve regulatory and supervisory autonomy, but so will the uk, and we just heard from the former governor of the bank of england that the uk will not be a rule-taker. so let me emphasise that, while we should maintain close and ambitions relations with the uk in financial services, we should also spare no effort to reduce potential systemic risk.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, chief negotiator barnier, after one of the darkest pages in the history of the union, we have the duty today to do everything possible to guarantee our citizens the results of 45 years of european integration, safeguarding our relations with the united kingdom and basing them on the principle of social and environmental equity and fair competition. we need an ambitious agreement, which commits the united kingdom to maintaining our production and food safety standards and avoids the risk that access to the single market for overseas products will be a trojan horse for products which would jeopardise the health of our consumers and the economic sustainability of our farms. future relations cannot be without a shared cultural heritage, which also includes quality agri-food production. that is why all our ladies will have to be guaranteed the same level of protection as before. these are the principles on which we are based on our conviction that after brexit there will be no vacuum, but an ever stronger and more sustainable europe, ready to defend its citizens.
madam president, it is with mixed feelings that i make this first contribution to the proceedings of this chamber because, naturally, it has been difficult to see the departure of the uk for any irish person, given the very close political, social and cultural affinity that we have with the uk. i’d much rather be here in very different circumstances, but it is fitting that i should speak, for my first contribution, on brexit, because essentially it is one of the reasons i put my candidacy forward a year ago and, ironically, one of the reasons that i’ve ended up here in the difficult circumstances. we’re at a delicate stage in proceedings, and there is no example in history where negotiating parties are seeking to impose trade barriers. so it will be difficult. it is amazing to continue to hear uk government officials expressing astonishment that the eu would exercise power, leverage and influence in trade negotiations, given the fact, on the one hand, that the uk were one of the architects of this system and that they have benefited from it so much. so i would call upon mr barnier to continue to speak about the uk as a trading partner and not a trading competitor, and not to imagine that the uk has to be punished in order for the eu to thrive.

madam president, the framework for the future relationship between the united kingdom and the european union will inevitably depend on the nature of the forces negotiating it. what we have on both sides are forces committed to the liberalization and commodification of public services, the attack on the social functions of the state, the limitation of state intervention in the economy, the deregulation of social and labour matters, a dangerous escalation of militarism within nato and an aggressive and interventionist foreign policy. we oppose and fight this path firmly. we see no reason why mutually beneficial cooperation links with the united kingdom should not be maintained and even strengthened, beyond the economic field, in fields such as science, education and culture, among others. a framework of loyal cooperation, respecting the sovereignty of each country and the rights of migrant communities, in which the people win, and not a competitive framework that exploits big capital at the expense of the people. it is not acceptable, under any circumstances, that the states should be prevented from constructing this framework based on bilateral relations with the united kingdom, nor is it acceptable that the european union should be given the exclusive right to negotiate. in this way we always know which interests will be served and to the detriment of which interests as well.
