in writing. the european commission has suggested that a more effective way for the eu to tackle money laundering and terrorist financing would be to significantly strengthen regulation at eu level and to bring supervisory functions to eu level. i am concerned about the transfer of supervisory functions to new eu-level institutions, as the current situation does not suggest that this would necessarily be the solution. instead, the aim is to strengthen national authorities and to increase cooperation between them. national authorities are able to meet the challenges; they are in close contact with banks, insurance companies, lawyers on a daily basis and have detailed and up-to-date knowledge of local conditions. the efficiency of a central supervisory body should not be equal to that of the member states' institutions. any further action should be based on an assessment of the current situation and take account of the practical functioning of the eu regulatory elements already adopted. moreover, we have no experience of how the newly introduced legislation works in practice. the further development of the eu's anti-money laundering system is of course key. however, consideration should be given to approaches that take into account the specificities of the member states and continue to provide the necessary flexibility for the member states.
the agenda includes the debate on the council and commission statements on the humanitarian situation of refugees at the eu's external borders (2020/2523 ((rsp)). i would remind you that it is possible to submit requests for assistance under the 'catch-the-eye' procedure and 'blue card' applications using both the 'standard registration' system and the electronic system, which i would urge you to try. the instructions are available at the entrance to our hemicycle.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, it is the second time under our presidency that we have the possibility to discuss in the plenary the humanitarian situation of refugees at our external borders. this shows the importance parliament attaches to this issue and we fully support it. our presidency is closely monitoring events on all migratory routes and will continue to do so throughout this semester. in 2019, the total number of illegal border crossings at the eu external borders was estimated at 139 000 which is a 6% decrease compared to 2018 and the lowest level in six years. however, while the central and western mediterranean routes saw a decrease of 40% and 50% respectively compared to 2018, arrivals on the eastern mediterranean route have increased by 50%. the situation on the greek islands is, in particular, a source of concern as we all noted during our last debate in the plenary on 29 january. the number of people present on the aegean islands reached a peak of over 40 000 migrants. it is the first time since the implementation of the eu-turkey statement that we have reached such a high number. the greek government is taking concrete measures to improve the situation. it has announced a new national strategy designed to protect minors who have arrived and who are resident in greece and it aims to increase the number of transfers to the mainland. in 2019, more than 35 000 migrants have been transferred and these transfers have continued at a fast pace since the beginning of 2020. arrivals from the eastern mediterranean route have direct impact on the flow of migrants in south east europe. in 2019, there were over 14 000 illegal border crossings on the western balkan route, which represents 140% increase compared to 2018. since the beginning of 2020, the pressure on this route has decreased, due also to winter conditions. nevertheless, this winter the pressure is higher when compared to the previous winter. our presidency will continue to devote much attention to the migratory situation and increasing migration management capacities in the entire western balkan region, with a particular focus on bosnia and herzegovina in order to ensure adequate protection of migrant population where, according to unhcr, more than 8 000 migrants and refugees are currently present, mainly in the north west. the situation in the country remains challenging, but in the last weeks, there have been some positive developments regarding the increase of adequate reception capacities. on 3 february, the croatian interior minister božinović, held a meeting with the new bosnian minister of security, mr radončić. the ministers agreed to strengthen the cooperation in several key areas: implementation of accelerated readmission procedures, more intense implementation of mixed border patrols and support of the republic of croatia in strengthening the bosnia-herzegovina security forces in border control. the ministers also agreed to strengthen the cooperation in combating smuggling networks, which was also a topic of a ministerial debate at the informal meeting of eu interior ministers in zagreb on 24 january 2020. minister radončić commented that bosnia-herzegovina lacks over one thousand border guard for better border control, as well as the 93% of eu funds approved to bosnia and herzegovina last year were intended for humanitarian treatment of migrants but only 7% to strengthen security forces. the eu will continue to support bosnia and herzegovina in its efforts to guarantee the dignity and safety of all migrants, especially the most vulnerable ones, while at the same time seeking a more permanent and comprehensive solution. we will also continue to support work with all partners in the western balkans. the overreaching goal of our efforts is to ensure that the migration flows at our borders are well managed and migrants’ fundamental rights are always respected. we expect the recent measures taken by the greek government to bring positive change. the new asylum legislation which has recently entered into force in greece should speed up the streamline of both asylum and return procedures. the return rate has been very low for far too long. returns are instrumental in managing the migratory flows. the plan of the greek government to return 10 000 migrants by the end of 2020 is ambitious but it can be achieved, also with necessary eu support. effective returns also require the good cooperation of turkey. to ensure that the situation is well managed, it is also important that our western balkan partners enhance their migration management capacities, also in the area of return and particularly assisted voluntary return, conclude readmission agreements with third countries and further align their visa policies. we are ready to assist them in these tasks by all means at our disposal. on the central mediterranean route, conditions for migrants in libya have deteriorated due to security issues arising from the internal conflict in the country. to alleviate the suffering of migrants and detained refugees in libya, the eu individual member states have funded unhcr and international organisation for migration (iom) programmes via eu trust fund for africa. iom is funded to carry out voluntary returns to countries of origin and to provide humanitarian emergency assistance and has done so at an impressive level. unhcr is funded to evacuate persons of concern to either niger or rwanda in view to a settlement in the eu or other receiving countries. these relevant actions require our further support, especially in view of ongoing conflicts in libya. to conclude, i would like to stress once again that the humanitarian situation at our external borders is a cause of great concern and we have to work closely together with our neighbouring countries and international organisations to improve it. let me also express here our appreciation for the work of all humanitarian partners. i will let the commission further elaborate on the comprehensive assistance the eu provides at our external borders to improve the situation.
member of the commission. – mr president, thank you minister and honourable members for this important debate. the humanitarian situation of refugees at our external borders is of great importance and of concern to us all. the eu promotes the respect of the rights of refugees, asylum seekers and migrants, both in the eu and outside of our borders in our neighbourhood. any form of violence against refugees and migrants is, needless to say, unacceptable. respect for fundamental rights and the rule of law must be ensured without exception. member states must fully comply with these principles when implementing the eu acquis on border management, asylum and return. pushbacks are not acceptable. access to asylum procedures and respect for the principle of non-refoulement, in line with the eu charter, must always be guaranteed. member states must have in place the mechanisms needed to prevent pushbacks in the first place. equally, they need to ensure that they have appropriate mechanisms to guarantee a prompt reply and investigate any alleged pushbacks. but challenges at external borders are many, and the commission is in constant contact with member states and provides them with support, including financial support for training the national border guards to act in conformity with fundamental rights requirements. the commission is also closely monitoring the situation at external borders and is in contact with member states regarding allegations of non-compliance with eu law at the external borders. as guardian of the treaty, the commission will take the necessary action where necessary. moreover, we now have in place a new european border and coast guard agency with a significantly reinforced mandate and an increased focus on the imperious need to respect fundamental rights. it is for this reason that a specific fundamental rights strategy has been drawn up by the new european border and coast guard agency. a fundamental rights officer was appointed to contribute to the agency’s fundamental rights strategy and monitor compliance with fundamental rights. fundamental rights monitors will also constantly assess the fundamental rights compliance of frontex operational activities. they will be deployed as part of the joint operations and they will be the eyes and ears of the fundamental rights officer. the agency must recruit 40 monitors by 5 december of this year. the new agency has also set up a complaints mechanism. this mechanism allows any person who is directly affected by the actions of staff involved in the activities of the agency and who considers himself or herself to have been the subject of a breach of his or her fundamental rights due to those actions, or to any party representing such a person, to submit a complaint in writing to the agency. what happens at our external borders affects europe. this is why the eu has always supported and also always stood at the side of our partners at the external borders as well as at the side of refugees and migrants in those countries. the eu is globally a key actor in providing assistance to refugees worldwide, especially in neighbouring countries, and in supporting third countries in developing their asylum system and their capacities to fight against smuggling. for instance, the eu supports turkey in its efforts to host more than 4 million refugees. the eur 6 billion facility for refugees in turkey has been fully mobilised and clearly produces tangible effects. over 1.7 million vulnerable refugees benefit from the emergency social safety net, the largest eu humanitarian project ever. the eu is also supporting more than 500 000 refugees’ children to regularly attend school. our western balkan partners have proven to be reliable and strong partners of the eu in managing migration and countering security challenges. however, reception capacities, in particular in serbia and bosnia and herzegovina, are stretched to the maximum, and migrants are often living in extremely difficult conditions. further capacity assistance is needed to help these countries to pursue their efforts to manage migration, addressing the issue of secondary movements in the region and treating migrants in a humane manner. to support all these actions, the eu provides financial and technical support to the western balkans through the regional programmes funded under the instrument for pre-accession so that they increase their capacity to build their national migration management system. in addition, the eu has recently provided eur 135 million in additional assistance to serbia, north macedonia and bosnia and herzegovina through the instrument for pre-accession and eur 32.6 million through the eu humanitarian aid. in all these actions inside and outside the eu, the protection of the most vulnerable, especially of children and unaccompanied minors, remains a priority for the commission. the commission is doing all it can to help member states to address the situation at our external borders. the dignity and safety of all affected persons needs to be ensured and protected. a lot has been achieved, but more remains to be done. we need to find a common understanding on how we collectively shape and manage the realities of migration. we must unite around our common values and humanitarian responsibilities and seek to make our society more cohesive and integrated. i count on your continued support and that of the council as well.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is well known that the western balkans route is one of the main migration routes to europe, making it also the busiest. unfortunately, frontline member states such as croatia, greece and others always carry a disproportionate burden, as due to their geographical location they are always the member state of first entry. the evidence is what is happening on the border between croatia and bosnia and herzegovina, as well as on the greek islands, where the flow of migrants is not diminishing. paradoxically, while asylum seekers can apply for international protection at official border posts, their numbers are minimal. instead, they use unregulated border crossings, often even using smuggling networks, with the sole aim of reaching western europe. at the end of 2018, following increased migratory pressure, the european commission allocated eur 6,8 million to croatia to support the management of the european union's external borders. this has been done in full compliance with the european union rules ensuring that all measures applied at the external borders of the union are proportionate and fully comply with the fundamental rights and the legislation of the european union on asylum. since the beginning of 2018, eur 36 million has also been provided to bosnia and herzegovina to address the immediate needs of migrants and asylum seekers. the protection of the external borders can only be achieved in accordance with the law and international conventions, and with respect for the fundamental rights and dignity of migrants. the member states at the external border and the european institutions are guarding our borders in good faith and applying these rules.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, the unprecedented peak in refugee arrivals in 2015 exposed the gaps in the eu’s policies at our borders, but it is now 2020 and the member states have had five years to find solutions. and yet, in many places, the situation of refugees at our external borders, instead of improving, continues to deteriorate. we still stand by and watch pregnant women and children losing their lives in the mediterranean sea. we still accept thousands of people, including unaccompanied minors, living in camps for years in inhumane conditions, waiting for their asylum procedure to advance. and we still consent to people being forced to return to unsafe countries. detention centres, illegal push-backs and floating sea fences are not the solutions we are calling for. i hope the croatian presidency will take note of this debate and our concerns on behalf of the european council. and let’s not forget that countries surrounding the eu are still taking in the vast majority of refugees. but also let’s not forget that countries who are our neighbours sometimes have their own citizens fleeing from the country, so we must address human rights with countries such as turkey and morocco. in this parliament we often talk about the european values. they include respect for human dignity and human rights. that also applies to refugees seeking protection.
president. - mr president, for the last few years we have been overwhelmed by unwanted migration. but this is just the beginning. africa's population will go from one to four billion this century. hundreds of millions will come to europe. that's why we have to guard the borders. not out of hatred for those outside our borders, but out of love for our own people. left-wing activists and rogue lawyers are abusing human rights to encourage and propagandize migration. they want to be able to enforce non-citizen rights in our home countries. they want to make migration a human right that can be enforced, with the cooperation of judges who are outsiders, and who, by the way, no one has ever voted for. the european convention on human rights, for example, was once intended to protect citizens of european countries from tyranny. today, however, it has evolved into a tool for forcing an open-border policy down the throats of our people. we need to solve the migration crisis at our borders. we do that by sending economic fortune seekers back without pardon. we need to take in genuine refugees in their region of origin, and above all, we need to adapt human rights treaties to the realities of 2020, so that they can no longer be exploited by the migration lobby.
madam president, no single country or organisation can stop this coronavirus outbreak alone. the eu should closely collaborate with member states, the who, the chinese government and other international partners, including taiwan. the international community should support all efforts to end this outbreak, but human rights should not be a casualty of the coronavirus. several international rights groups have signalled that the chinese government has been withholding information, suppressing freedom of expression and denying access to food and medicines. on the other hand, elsewhere in the world there is an increase in anti-chinese or anti-asian xenophobia and discrimination. international law is very clear that during a time of public health emergency, any restrictions on human rights should be based on legality, necessity, proportionality and grounded in evidence. i count on the eu to protect our health and to protect human rights, within the eu and elsewhere.
mr president, commissioner, you are well aware that the majority of this house are committed to the sustainable development goals and, of course, to our commitments to the paris summit. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. but i do not think that we can focus on two big programmes, the farm-to-table strategy or the green deal, which are big programmes and generate great expectations, on the fact that agriculture is causing difficulties and that big changes have to be made to it, without recognising what has already been done. i mean, we can't just focus on phytosanitary elimination. it is an important element, but we must give farmers the possibility of innovation, biological control and other elements. they must be given an alternative to this situation. without complicity and without commitment to agriculture it is impossible for this programme, this strategy to succeed. so with everyone's commitment, let's get it done. not with your back to the farmers.
mr president, commissioner, you are in a difficult position. believe me, farmers are watching this debate because they know that the strategy is about them and that the strategy must be for them. they expect us to support them. because they know that the impacts of climate change are primarily on them. they know that it's not the green deal, but it's the climate change. if there is no frost in the czech republic today, it means that diseases and pests are multiplying and a lower harvest can be expected. this is not the impact of the green deal, but it is the impact of climate change that we have to fight, but we have to support our farmers in doing so. they also bear the costs and risks of cheaper imports from third countries. then there's the challenge of these food chains, which my colleague here mentioned, because most people buy from the food chains, and they have far more power. so please, i have a question for you: how do you advocate for farmers to be better positioned in the food chain? that's a lot of worry. we need to support active farmers who live in rural areas, who care for the soil, forests and biodiversity. please support active farmers.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the food chain is responsible for between 21% and 37% of total co2 emissions. a system designed in this way is no longer sustainable, even in the light of the challenge we face: producing more with less, and providing healthy, quality food for all. let us not underestimate the political moment we are living in. the citizens are on our side and they want transparency and security, but legislation alone is not enough. a radical change of the whole system and of our way of life is needed. the holistic approach is key. we need more synergy between the different policies, health and agriculture first and foremost, which must complement each other and not clash. finally, we are all witnessing a growing awareness on the part of consumers of the value of food, in terms of health and the processes that contribute to bringing it to our table. we must therefore devise a uniform front-of-pack labelling system which informs the consumer without influencing him and which is based on sound scientific justification.
mr president, commissioner, i should like to begin by saying that i have great confidence in european agricultural production and that we can be proud of everything that is produced here. after all i heard this morning, it is important to be able to say it again and to say thank you to our farmer friends. the strategy of fork to fork, for me, is perhaps a reconciliation between consumers and farmers. three elements seem to me to be essential. first, that there should be an active participation of the agricultural world in this strategy, that it should be a party to this strategy, as well as consumers. secondly, there must be new guarantees of financing and investment to enable this transition. farmers do not have to be the adjustment variable in european budgets, nor the adjustment value in the green pact; they need to be supported in their investment and transition. and finally, thirdly, the essential element is protection: protection of consumers, protection of farmers, protection of the environment. it can only be achieved if controls are strengthened and if equivalence is established for products imported into our territory, to put an end to certain injustices and misunderstandings on the part of consumers. i wish you luck with that strategy.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission has already made a number of proposals. in the eu, around 500 people die on our roads every week. that's two full planes going down. we would never accept so many deaths in aviation. why do we accept them in traffic? the cost of road accidents is estimated at 280 billion euros a year. add to this the human and social tragedies. i therefore welcome the european commission's pledges to reduce by half the number of road deaths in that period, in particular, as part of the next eu road safety policy. this medium-term goal will be the key to achieving the vision of zero deaths by 2050. technological progress and artificial intelligence could significantly improve road safety, as 94% of accidents involve human error. however, an automated road sector brings new risks and challenges that need to be addressed, such as new infrastructure for driverless vehicles, ethical issues related to liability and data protection, and environmental and health issues. while pedestrians and cyclists are by far the least likely to cause harm to other road users, they are the most at risk road users and account for almost one third of all deaths. to increase road safety and reduce road deaths, we need a new urban planning system that prioritises pedestrians, cyclists and public transport. we need an eu strategy for safe and sustainable mobility. as you said yourself, commissioner, and i am glad to hear it, you have a strategy. and with strategy comes action. i hope you will keep your word and make road safety a top priority for you in the years to come.
on behalf of the ecr group. - madam president! - i have to go. improving the safety of road users, whether drivers, pedestrians or cyclists, is a major challenge for both the member states and the european commission. looking at the statistics, it is easy to see that the number of road deaths is closely linked to geography, with the least developed regions being the worst off. nearly 55 percent of all road deaths occur on rural roads. there is no better justification for continuing the ambitious cohesion policy that allows local authorities to invest in safe infrastructure and public transport. technology and advanced safety systems in vehicles also provide a great deal of support to drivers. the next step will certainly be fully autonomous vehicles, but that's the future. and today we need to encourage drivers, especially young ones, to drive responsibly. it is worthwhile to involve, for example, insurance companies that have the tools to effectively reward safe driving. i am convinced that the european commission can count on us in all its efforts to promote road safety and to implement the strategy to eliminate road fatalities by 2050.
madam president, commissioner, thank you for your statement. the strategy adopted last june aims to halve the number of deaths and serious injuries on our roads in europe. it is a wonderful human goal, and we all know the unfortunate cases of families who have been destroyed by the reality of road safety. to achieve this, i think we have to take a whole range of options. the european commission had envisaged, among other things, and i hope that it will, a review of the reality of driving licences; the commission is also proposing to review crossborder cooperation, particularly in the case of driving licences being withdrawn. the commission has also proposed a revision of the recommendations on limiting alcohol, drugs and even speed. these are at least three areas which i think are important and which i hope you will work on in order to take concrete measures, beyond a target, to improve the overall road safety situation in europe. if we look closely, we see that in recent years there has been a kind of stagnation in the road safety figures, and we can regret it. after many years of improvement, this stagnation cannot continue. it cannot continue, since there is a very strong objective to reduce the number of deaths and injuries. i would also ask you to go further, particularly in the correlations of good practice by member state. we need to be able to ask the right questions together. of course, infrastructure and vehicles are not the same in all member states, but we recognize that some measures must also be implemented in some member states to improve the reality of road safety and the figures. there are too many differences, and very significant differences, in road safety between the different member states. i very much hope, madam commissioner, that we can work more on these figures and that you too can, that we can make recommendations together, member state by member state, so that good practice is disseminated everywhere and the lot of road safety can be improved throughout europe.
madam president, i first want to start by expressing my empathy for the communities and the families affected by the virus, and also for the healthcare providers that have been helping them. indeed, we should not panic. this virus may not be as deadly as we think or as other viruses, but it will have indirect effects on health systems if we do not take the measures that are needed – and that is something that i want to explain in my intervention today. i think you have all seen the curves of how an epidemic usually takes place. well, we have to take all the measures possible to flatten that curve so that people do not infect other people. usually people infected with this virus infect two to three other people, as we have learned already from the epidemiology of the virus. we have to bring these numbers down so that, in total, people in the population do not get infected because, and let’s not forget this, there is no immunity in the population. so let’s not compare this with influenza. this has nothing to do with influenza where there is a high background immunity. this is a really different virus and the real danger is that the health systems, the healthcare systems are being overloaded. i think we’ve all read the newspapers. today we’ve seen what is happening in the northern part of italy. hospitals where people do not get beds, where intensive care beds are lacking because they can’t put the patients where they need to be. indeed, we really have to do everything possible to contain that virus. i want to reinforce the call of the commissioner that maybe we have to step up our measures to make sure that this virus is contained. i’m not going to talk about the social and economic consequences. we all know these, but if we don’t do what is necessary to contain the virus and to look at the health aspects – also the indirect effects of this epidemic – the social and economic consequences will be much worse than they would be otherwise. so this should be our first priority also in this house.
mr president, public health is a matter for the member states. crisis management is the same. i hope that all the member states will do their duty properly. article 168 gives the commission a mandate to promote coordination. this is a cross-border threat and much has already been done by the commission, for which i am grateful. but i ask for even more coordination between member states, particularly on prevention, results of various treatments, disinfection, how to deal with mass gatherings - that is a very different approach - how to deal with quarantine and so on. secondly, i note that we are dependent on third countries for disinfectants, medicines and so on. i urge that policies be developed to ensure that in the future our half a billion inhabitants will not be dependent on medicines and disinfectants from abroad.
madam president, commissioner, every crisis sometimes reveals the worst and the best. the best thing is to mobilize the health care workers, the health workers, the nurses, the doctors, etc. the worst is sometimes selfish national reflexes like france and germany, which ban the export of masks. every crisis also reveals our weaknesses, as françois-xavier pointed out, and i agree with him. of course, our reliance on medical care, particularly from china and india, shows european citizens that europe is not protecting its citizens at the heart of european health. the economic crisis and the health crisis should not be separated, since this health and economic crisis will have consequences for health. the economic crisis, too, we saw in 2008-2009: budgets that have seen significant cuts in a whole series of member states, people getting worse health care. we must mobilize to ensure that everyone in europe can get proper health care and meet this challenge also through this economic crisis. we must do everything we can to bring about these coherent policies, this necessary solidarity between the member states, to have a europe which protects, to have an industrial strategy, that is true, but also a sound energy strategy. this whole crisis, i hope we can draw the consequences and also the lessons. that's the most important thing. i would like to conclude by saying that i hope that one day we will be able to mobilize in such a significant way for road safety, since, if there have been 500 deaths from coronavirus in europe, at the same time, 2,500 people have died on our roads.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). we have fortunately promised that support and must show more than ever solidarity with the countries on europe's external borders. but make no mistake, guarding the borders is not going to solve the problem. we need to ensure that the emergency measures taken in 2016 and outsourced in part to turkey, which hosts 3.7 million migrants, have been of course temporary and partial. we must absolutely go for a common european policy on asylum and migration. the european parliament has taken its responsibility. but, commissioner, how long must we wait before we finally have a breakthrough with the other institutions?
in writing. the situation on the greek-turkish border is proof enough that erdogan understands neither dialogue nor international law. for years, some have tried to convince us that turkey is a reliable ally. but reality once again belies them. the european union's previous defensive stance has unfortunately had these results. we must finally go on the offensive and open the debate immediately on: freezing the customs union with turkey and the abolition of duty-free imports of turkish products into the eu. turkey is not going to comply otherwise. in conclusion, i would like to thank the men and women of the greek armed forces and the greek police, as well as the citizens of our borders, who are bearing all the burden at this moment, because the greek borders are the european borders. and we will defend them with all our might.

with today's extraordinary sitting i declare resumed the session of the european parliament adjourned on tuesday 10 march 2020.
in view of the gravity of the situation and the significant increase in cases of covid-19 in the european union, the conference of presidents decided on thursday 19 march, on a proposal from the president, to further amend the calendar of parliament's activities in 2020 for reasons of force majeure and as a measure of safety and protection of public health. in view of the extraordinary part-session to be held today, thursday 26 march, the partsession previously scheduled for 1 and 2 april, 22 and 23 april and 3 and 4 june in brussels is cancelled. the meeting, originally scheduled for 11 to 14 may in strasbourg, will take place in brussels on 13 and 14 may. the next meeting, originally scheduled for 15 to 18 june in strasbourg, will take place in brussels on 17 and 18 june. the next meeting, originally scheduled for 6 to 9 july in strasbourg, will take place in brussels on 8 and 9 july. the situation will of course be reviewed by the conference of presidents in the light of the evolution of the covid-19 pandemic. the revised provisional calendar is available on the plenary's website.
the first item on the agenda is the vote on requests for the application of the urgency procedure made by the commission under rule 163 of the rules of procedure. we 're voting on three demands: allocation of slots at community airports: common rules; specific measures to mobilise investment in the health systems of the member states and in other sectors of their economies in response to the covid-19 outbreak (coronavirus response investment initiative); financial assistance to member states and countries negotiating accession to the european union that are severely affected by a serious public health emergency. before the vote, only the person who made the request, a counter-speaker and the chairman or rapporteur of the committee responsible may be heard. does anyone in the chamber wish to speak? (parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the information on the voting was distributed to all members by e-mail on monday. two test votes have already been held on monday and yesterday to enable all members to become familiar with the procedure. the vote on the urgent procedure will take place from 10 a.m. to 11 a.m. all votes today will be by roll call. meps will be able to verify their votes and the results of the vote itself in the document which will be published today on the plenary's website. i would remind the house of the instructions for the vote: meps will now receive the ballot paper for the urgent procedure under rule 163 from the e-mail box 'plenaryvote@europarl.europa.eu'. members are invited to: printing the ballot paper; write their name and indicate their vote (for, against or abstention) on the ballot (there will be three votes on the same ballot, one for each proposal put to the vote); sign the solemn declaration on the ballot paper; scan or photograph the card; send the scan or photograph of the signed form to the e-mail address plenaryvote@europarl.europa.eu. if members have not been able to obtain a printer, they are invited to ask their local assistant to print out the ballot paper and hand it to them so that members can sign and send it. only if this is not possible may members hand-copy the ballot paper in clear and legible form, including the solemn declaration at the bottom of the ballot paper, clearly indicate their name and sign it. only ballots sent from the official address of the member's parliament and bearing the member's name and the vote in legible form with his signature shall be considered as cast. only ballots received in the e-mail box before the close of voting (11 a.m.) will be taken into account. the members present in the chamber shall vote by distance, in the same way as the other members. the vote is open. i invite you to vote by the deadline of 11:00 a.m. the results of the votes on the requests for urgent procedure will be announced at 12.30 p.m. the explanations of vote may be submitted in writing. exceptionally, voting statements of up to 400 words will be accepted.
