president-in-office of the council. – madam president, on behalf of the croatian presidency of the council, it is my honour to contribute to parliament’s discussion today on vaccines and therapeutics in the context of the covid—19 pandemic. this pandemic constitutes an unprecedented challenge for europe and the world. it has brought huge human losses and suffering, as well as social and economic damage to millions of people. it has put our healthcare systems under exceptional pressure. it has wide-ranging implications for all of us in every part of the european union. as we gradually start lifting confinement measures, we will have to learn to live with the coronavirus under strict conditions and constant monitoring. only the development, production and deployment of effective and safe vaccines and treatments will hopefully provide us with a lasting solution to this global health crisis. vaccines and treatments will be crucial for europe, but they will be even more critical for our most vulnerable partners with fragile health systems such as africa. our ultimate and common goal, therefore, is to accelerate the development, manufacture and delivery of vaccines and treatments for everyone, everywhere at an affordable price in the shortest possible time. the eu and its member states have embarked on a worldwide race to find a safe and effective vaccine and treatments to counter the spread of the coronavirus. regarding activities at eu level, already on 10 march at their first meeting after the covid—19 pandemic reached europe, eu leaders committed to supporting research as part of our common response to the outbreak. the european parliament has also raised issues relating to the development of vaccines and treatments in its resolution on eu coordinated action to combat the covid—19 pandemic adopted on 17 april. on april 7, the croatian presidency organised an informal videoconference where ministers responsible for research and innovation showed their determination to coordinate their actions and welcomed ten actions for immediate response to the crisis. on 7 may, in an informal videoconference organised by the commission, many member states supported the idea of preparing a covid—19 vaccination immunisation plan. to accelerate the coronavirus vaccine, the commission has launched a dedicated covid—19 data portal that enables researchers to upload, access and analyse research data. currently, there are more than 90 vaccines in development around the globe, but only eight are so far subject to clinical trials. three candidates for vaccines are receiving support from eu funding through the research programme horizon 2020. member states are also investing in several candidates. moving to treatments, it is encouraging to note that there are so far more than 40 developers of potential treatments for covid—19 who have contacted the european medicines agency and the member states for scientific advice. most of the treatments proposed are medicines currently authorised for other diseases. clinical trials are currently ongoing to determine their efficiency for the treatment of covid—19 patients. the eu has allocated resources via horizon 2020 to discovery, an eu—wide clinical trial to test treatments involving more than 3 000 european patients. the eu has already committed over eur 500 million to different research and innovation initiatives, including approximately eur 48 million to 18 projects on vaccines and treatments via horizon 2020 research programmes, and eur 72 million in public funds for a therapeutics and diagnostics innovative medicine initiative with an additional eur 45 million in expected contributions from the industry. additionally, the eu has redirected eur 150 million from smes and start—ups for innovative solutions to tackle the outbreak via the european innovation council (eic) accelerator programme and up to eur 80 million to curevac, a european vaccine developer, by a common commission aid initiative. in order to respond to the urgency, the european medicines agency will speed up its regulatory procedures so that marketing authorisation of safe, effective and high-quality covid—19 related medicines and vaccines can be guaranteed without unnecessary delay. turning now to activities at international level, on 24 april, the who launched global cooperation for the accelerated development, production and equitable access to diagnostics, therapeutics and vaccines. in response to this call, on 4 may, the european union, several member states and other g20 countries co—hosted the international coronavirus global response pledging conference. the initiative has raised eur 7.4 billion, including eur 4 billion from the eu and its member states. this is a very promising and important first step. there are a number of key challenges. once new therapies are identified, the challenge will be to scale up production and distribution to make them available to patients throughout the world at an affordable price. as regards vaccines, the first challenge is the patient’s safety and to produce a vaccine in extremely high quantities, since demand is global. at the european level, already in december 2018 the council adopted a recommendation on strengthened cooperation against vaccine—preventable diseases, which has the double aim of reducing inequalities between member states and increasing security of vaccine supply. eu instruments such as joint procurement could be used in that spirit to help ensure fair distribution and solidarity among all member states. in order to tackle other important issues, such as securing sufficient and autonomous production capacity in the european union, fair licensing and fair intellectual property rights conditions, it is necessary to involve all concerned actors, including industry and the public sector. in this context, i find it very encouraging to see that several manufacturers have agreed to pool their resources. public authorities, pharmaceutical companies and multilateral organisations must work together to reach the common goal of making vaccines and treatments available to as many as possible. we will defeat the covid—19 crisis and we will do it with a shared spirit of european and global solidarity and responsibility.
member of the commission. – madam president, from the very first day of this crisis for us in the commission, one consideration was evident: no country in the european union or worldwide can battle this pandemic alone. we need to take bold steps and we need to take them together. containment measures have proved efficient so far and have allowed us to diminish the spread of the virus. but they will not eradicate it. for this, we need further action on three areas: first, early diagnosis; second, targeted treatments for the ill; and, third, a vaccine that will ensure immunisation. we will also need all three to be widely accessible and affordable because, as long as the virus exists in the world, no one can be safe. this is why the european union is taking the lead in organising a coronavirus global response pledging initiative, which took place on 4 may. the aim was precisely to lead the world effort to develop diagnostics, vaccines and therapeutics against the virus as soon as possible, not only to make it possible to get to these objectives, but also to ensure that they are accessible on a fair and equitable basis once they are discovered. i am delighted to have this opportunity, on behalf of the commission, to discuss these issues with you in a bit more detail this afternoon. first, vaccines. on vaccines, there is no doubt that this is the best way to truly exit the pandemic and avoid a resurgence of the disease. however, developing a vaccine is a complicated process that depends on close coordination, financial support and unity. each of these considerations was evident during the pledging event of 4 may that the european union led at global level. first, we managed to attain our overall objective with an ambitious level of funding raised. we mobilised eur 7.4 billion (usd 8 billion) in pledges, of which eur 5 billion came from team europe. the second – and equally important – aspect of this pledging conference was that we sent, as the european union, a resounding message of unity and solidarity across the world, and we also proved that, in today’s turbulent times, the european union remains a force for good for the planet. this was only the beginning. the pledging marathon remains open until 23 may, so i take this opportunity to encourage all of you to engage in helping to find new donors, public or private, and to join forces in this unique worldwide effort. here comes the big question: where are we on the vaccines? as the croatian presidency has just indicated, the who has identified 8 vaccine candidates in clinical evaluation and 94 candidates in pre—clinical evaluation. three candidates are receiving support from the commission through our horizon 2020 programme, and member states are also investing in several candidates. we need to focus efforts on finding the most promising solutions. that’s why the commission has proposed that an eu—wide network for clinical trials be set up. such a network will ensure a critical mass of data from patients, help to identify the most promising vaccine candidates or therapeutics, and swiftly move them into the most appropriate clinical trials. once developed, of course, a vaccine would need to pass a rigorous approval process, and we in the commission will work very closely with the european medicines agency to ensure that this process moves as swiftly and as quickly as possible without, however, compromising on safety and efficacy. producing the vaccine is only half of the battle ahead of us. we should also ensure fair, widespread and equitable access to the vaccine as early as possible, not only to all europeans, but also to the rest of the world. there are many tools that we could use to achieve this – from public—private partnerships to increased manufacturing capacity, to advance purchase agreements to ensure production. the commission is currently in intensive discussions with both our member states and vaccine producers to identify clearly how we can best support member states in the purchase and deployment of vaccines. all options are on the table and we are not excluding any of them. last week, health ministers discussed the possibility of a uniform covid-19 immunisation strategy throughout the european union. this would also help us to maximise access to the vaccine when it becomes available and i look forward to hearing your thoughts on these approaches this afternoon. let me now turn to therapeutics. the commission has reacted efficiently to support member states in this regard. i don’t want to beautify the situation. we all know that the early stage of the crisis was not an example of optimal coordination. many measures were taken rapidly under pressure at national level without the necessary coordination. but, once this first stage went through and our member states saw the added value of cooperation and the interdependence that binds them together as the european union, with the help of the commission, we reacted efficiently to support them. let me recall some of these actions. since day one, we have coordinated closely with all important actors – the european medicines agency, the european centre for disease prevention and control (ecdc), the national agencies, industry – to avoid or tackle bottlenecks, to better anticipate future shortages and to assist in the adaptation of production. in parallel, we have issued concrete guidance on the rational supply of medicines, regulatory flexibility and clinical trials to avoid unavailability of treatments. we have activated the resceu stockpile of medical equipment, including therapeutics and vaccines, and we have established a covid clearing house mechanism that facilitates the matching of the needs for medical equipment with available supplies. thankfully, the situation is now beginning to stabilise in the eu, but we must also continue working on the supply side. with this in mind, we are supporting a dedicated industry cooperation project to increase the supply of critical intensive care unit medicines. parallel to that, the european medicines agency and the commission have put in place a mechanism to support the rapid development and authorisation of new covid-19 medicines and vaccines and, as we speak, we are currently preparing a joint procurement for such intensive care unit (icu) medicines. let me conclude by saying that today we are, of course, discussing the short term, which is saving lives and winning the battle with the virus on the medical side, but i would like to finish with a few words about the medium and long term. the pandemic has revealed and amplified important issues with our supply chain for medicinal products. i’m very aware of how dependent we are on important pharmaceutical products or ingredients. so we need to come together quickly and identify solutions to the three emerging weaknesses from the crisis: how to strengthen our coordination mechanisms and supply chains, how to boost our preparedness, and how to improve our society’s overall strategic resilience. the commission is preparing a new pharmaceutical strategy that will take account of the lessons learned during the pandemic and make the eu’s pharmaceutical system future proof and crisis resistant. yesterday, president von der leyen had the opportunity to give you a bit more detail about our very intensive work on the recovery initiative, which is to be expected in the next few days. i would say that part of building this resilience for cohesive and resistant european societies passed from a very rigorous health programme that would bridge this asymmetry between what europeans expect from europe in the area of public health and what europe has in terms of legal competence to face up to this challenge. commissioner kyriakides and i will look forward to working very closely with this house on this endeavour. but first, i am keen to hear your thoughts on the challenges and solutions and i am very willing to take them on board and report back to you at my earliest convenience. (applause)
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. we all long for normalcy. it's not just the economy that's suffering from the lockdown, but a lot of people personally. but the coronavirus is still among us, and it's still very dangerous. so we have to be careful, to be careful that there is not a second wave. and we also need to use digital solutions. i was a little annoyed and disappointed that commissioner reynders only listed the concerns this morning. we need to be on the offensive. and we have to look at italy, spain, france and belgium, the countries that have suffered the most from the crisis. strangely, there's hardly any complaints about the lockdown. in my country, germany, it seems that there is much to complain about, even though we have fared much better. i call on my fellow citizens: 'stop mourning me and show solidarity with those who have been hit much harder!' of course, the only final solution is a drug or a vaccine. here, personally, after many conversations with experts, i'm optimistic that we'll have a drug and/or a vaccine much faster than under normal circumstances. the conference held by the european commission last monday was a clear sign of cooperation and dialogue. but unfortunately we also need a plan b. because the united states did not participate in the conference at governmental level, and therefore we should continue to rely on dialogue, but also be prepared, for example, to issue compulsory licences in a coordinated manner in europe or to use trade policy instruments. when donald trump threatened tariffs on european cars, jean-claude juncker drove to the white house and issued a counter-threat. and the health of our citizens must be at least as important to us as the automobile industry. ursula von der leyen says: "we must speak the language of power". that is also true of this subject.

madam president, governments, companies, scientific committees, everyone is racing to develop a vaccine against covid-19 as quickly as possible. but the important thing is not only to do everything now to speed up this development, but also to ask whether there are some things that could have allowed us to shorten the research time considerably. for example, previous research for vaccines against sars and mers was stopped during the first phase. if phase 2 and 3 had been completed, some of the work to discover the covid-19 vaccine would have been done today and we would have saved a lot of time. is there any useful research on future health threats that is not being done today for lack of commercial interest, because in that case the duty of public institutions is to provide the means and resources so that this research can be carried out successfully. let us learn from this health crisis, anticipate it, and do everything we can to protect the health of the citizens we represent.

member of the commission. – madam president, thank you for this very useful debate. i identified two families of issues from your statements. one – let’s call it the quest for resilience – and the other one is what happens once we have the vaccine? how do we make sure that we avoid the problems that we saw in the early stage about access to the distribution of personal protective equipment? let me start on the quest for resilience. there is no doubt whatsoever that europe has to draw the lessons of this pandemic. it’s evident, it’s written on the wall. this is something that society will impose on all policy-making at all levels. as far as the quest for resilience at the eu level is concerned, let me repeat what i said earlier. we have to bridge this asymmetry of expectations between what europeans want from europe in areas where europe has nothing in terms of legal competence. this is a major issue. this contaminates public opinion and projects europe in a negative light. i really hope that this will be, together with my colleagues, one of our main lines of action in the months and years to come. also on the quest for resilience, we have to make sure that the recovery initiative sheds light and reinforces a self-standing health problem that is able to perform not only under regular, normal circumstances, but is also designed to face up to major sanitary crises and pandemics. this is also a major challenge in front of us. finally, many of you also raised the issues of production, back to the eu, pricing, strategical autonomy, the place of europe in the world and geopolitics. when it comes to pharmaceuticals and all these questions, i want to assure that they will be addressed in our pharmaceutical strategy communication, which i will be presenting to you, together with commissioner kyriakides, later this year. let me now come to – and finish –the other cluster of issues of concern to you, which is what happens once we have the vaccine, how we make sure that access is fair, equitable and widespread distribution is available to all europeans and the rest of the world. i think there are three options for us, a three—point strategy that will allow us to act effectively in this respect. first, we need to ensure immediate access to vaccine supplies. for this, we do have instruments to be deployed, namely we can create an eu stockpile of vaccines, serving as an immediate response through the european union civil protection mechanism, we can organise an eu joint procurement scheme, which will be secure purchase commitments in a stable framework, and the commission could also procure directly, on behalf of member states, vaccines making use of the emergency support instrument. this was about immediate actions. second, regarding secure production, we are already in close contact with industry and we are exploring together with them concrete measures such as public-private partnerships to increase manufacturing capacity or to advance purchase agreements to ensure production. thirdly, delivery. in the beginning we might need some prioritisation. this is precisely the objective of the european immunisation strategy that i was referring to in my opening statement. such an immunisation strategy could set priority strands for certain segments of the population, but we will also need to ensure access worldwide because, as long as the virus exists, no one is safe. this is again precisely the purpose of our global leadership in the framework of the pledging conference that should not end on 23 may, when we have this critical mass of financing to speed up the production of vaccines. but we will also have a role, after 23 may, to make sure that the rest of the world and especially those parts of the world that have more vulnerable health systems, like africa or latin america, will not be left out of this development.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, honourable members, i wish to thank you for this important debate. we have one common goal around the world and that is to defeat the virus. as the commissioner said, no country is able to defeat the covid-19 pandemic alone. there is only one way to defeat it, namely with a combination of prevention through vaccination and treatment of people who have fallen ill. unfortunately, we still have to wait for treatments and vaccines to be found. the european union has mobilised important resources and it is fully involved in a coordinated global effort to develop, produce and employ vaccines and treatments for all. a positive dynamic has developed with the launching of the coronavirus global response pledging effort. the european union is fully committed to support member states’ efforts within and outside the union. we know that we can now count on the cooperation of all eu institutions in this endeavour. in particular, we hope we can coordinate with the european parliament. until an efficient, accessible, affordable vaccine is developed, it is important that we continue respecting the epidemiological measures to protect all our loved ones.
in writing. – covid-19 has proven one more time that vaccination represents one of the most powerful and cost-effective public health measure. the entire world is currently waiting for the development and deployment of a vaccine anti covid-19, in order to return to the daily routine before the pandemic. we have at the moment more than 100 covid19 vaccine development projects worldwide, 8 of them are already in clinical trial phase, of which 3 are supported by horizon 2020. once developed, it is important to ensure swift authorisation by our trusted partner, the european medicine agency and work in close collaboration with european pharma sector to provide the necessary production capacity. with great concern, i want to remind you that eu is the region of the world with the lowest confidence in the safety and effectiveness of vaccines. one of the strongest drivers for vaccine hesitancy are misunderstandings about the benefits and risks of vaccines, the severity of vaccine preventable infectious diseases and the fear of potential side effects derived from misleading information. consequently, i stress the ongoing need to reinforce our actions, together with the commission, member states, ema and ecdc to tackle vaccine hesitancy, improve vaccination coverage, promote sustainable vaccination policies, and contribute eventually to european and global health.
in writing. it is not the first time that we are facing shortages of goods at the international level: today it is medicines, tomorrow it will be vaccines. we know that a fair distribution of these will not only ensure health, it can also avoid dramatic friction between countries. unfortunately, this has already happened within the eu, calling the institution into question. it is time to join forces, to reach agreements between countries. we call on the commission to assume its role of coordinating the member states, giving priority to the essential and forgetting ideological proclamations which only materialize resentments and accentuate division. we need to anticipate health problems, we need fair distribution based on patient needs, on actual demand, not on storage forecasts. in the face of scarcity, give priority to those most in need. when the vaccine arrives, clarity on this will be essential. we ask the ecdc to share information on the situation in each country: test results, treatment protocols, conclusions... the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work.
in writing. despite the fact that more and more member states are fortunately able to implement a gradual relaxation of the corona measures, it is also becoming increasingly clear that a treatment or a vaccine against the virus remains indispensable. the european union rightly plays a leading role in international cooperation in the search for this vaccine. the successful donor conference on 4 may, which raised eur 7.4 billion worldwide, is a good example. further cooperation across borders will be needed. however, when a vaccine or a treatment is found, it will also be essential to ensure equal access for all citizens. we cannot allow the big private companies to profit through patents on a vaccine that the whole world is craving and for which billions of euros have been spent in public research funds. research that has been publicly funded should also remain available in the public domain. the vaccine must be safe, affordable and universally available. i think it's clear that this is our social duty. every citizen, in the eu and beyond, has the right to equal access. this can only be done through international cooperation, with countries, companies, ngos, universities and other research institutions.

it is time to think seriously about the european union as it stands. the lisbon treaty entered into force 10 years ago, and the schuman declaration celebrated its 70th anniversary this year. the question of the future of europe has only become more relevant because of the corona crisis. corona has clearly exposed certain pain points in the construction of our union. despite the strong catch-up movement that followed, the eu was unable to control the divergent measures taken by the member states at the beginning of the crisis. we must not be blind to this and dare to learn from it. the social and economic consequences, the worst since the second world war, also require a high degree of resilience and a strong union able to act decisively. i am calling today for a thorough debate on the role of the european union in cross-border health crises, so that we are better prepared for the future. the european union's toolbox in this area has proved too limited for the major works expected of the union. we must seize this moment to reflect on how to make the eu more efficient and, above all, more effective.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, this pandemic highlights precarious working conditions for 17 million mobile workers in the eu, of whom hundreds of thousands are cross-border, frontier and seasonal workers. better social protection was already needed before this crisis, but the lack of safety measures on asparagus or strawberry farms, or in slaughterhouses in the netherlands or germany, opened many eyes. health and safety measures against covid-19 are not always respected at the workplace, and that is why written instructions like display notices must be used. crossing borders must also be easy for them, and a better coordination of social security systems has become a matter of high urgency. these workers are in the front line: in hospitals and nursing homes in austria for example, where many female health workers from romania and bulgaria work overtime. they provide a vital workforce. we must provide them with vital social, and health and safety, protection. this is a matter of social justice.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the adoption of a joint resolution by the european parliament is an important step towards ensuring better conditions for seasonal workers and is in fact a clear message that we will not tolerate any form of modern slavery. the messages sent by us, the social democrats, about the existence of serious problems and violations of european legislation on labour and social rights are confirmed. in the case of romania, the government itself has endangered the health of hundreds of seasonal workers by taking decisions that have not allowed the application of european legislation. the resolution takes up the solutions included in our repeated messages to the european and national institutions and these solutions are supported by over 50 000 people who have signed our petition to the european institutions. but the problem is not solved. the romanian workers who took to the streets, those who appealed to the courts, those who were crowded into planes and buses have high expectations from us. the european union must protect all workers and intervene when social rights have been violated, as it did in the case of romanian workers.
mr president, the covid crisis has shown how important seasonal workers are in meeting the food needs of all european citizens. these workers deserve our respect and recognition, and in that sense we thank the rapporteur and the contribution. it goes in the right direction of protecting them and somewhere of safeguarding them, which is part of the whole agricultural system, in quotes. in relation to frontier workers: almost one in two frontier workers in my region is a frontier worker. i think it is important to be able to make their lives easier in a crisis. there have been many difficulties, especially at the borders. it would be a good idea to produce, perhaps at european level, a kind of work passport for frontier workers to facilitate checks on them, and also to make life easier for companies which have had to multiply administrative procedures, and thus to organise this, perhaps at different levels. we could give a five-star passport, if i may say so, to health workers who cross the border to meet health needs on both sides of the border. let's work on this proposal. i think she could make a difference in everyone's life.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the coronavirus has put the survival of the european union in question, perhaps even in jeopardy. the european central bank is doing well, the european investment bank is doing well, and the esm credit line is doing well to protect our health. i believe that italy should use that 36-37 billion euros to improve health facilities, even in the face of a possible new second wave of coronavirus. but now we cannot go backwards, there can be no compromise downwards at the european council on 17 and 18 july. we must absolutely defend the original proposal of the european commission. the northern countries who think they can cut back on growth-support measures are wrong: we must continue to have investment, we must have loans to enable industry and small and mediumsized enterprises and agriculture to recover from this crisis. it would be a mistake not to do so, it would be dangerous for the very survival of the european union.

president, in times of coronavirus restrictions and economic hardship, people want solutions and not north-south conflict. the big buyers of the european recovery fund are the multinationals. they benefit from direct economic support and from investment in our economy and the internal market. yet they hardly contribute to taxes. let them therefore contribute to our recovery, let them pay for a subscription to the single market, let them pay for pollution. but above all, do not put the bill to the ordinary taxpayer, in the north or in the south. and yes, there are conditions to the spending. - i'm sure you do. for countries and for companies. european money must make us prosperous and sustainable and must never cause harm, never be accompanied by harm to people, the environment or the rule of law. so, prime minister rutte, stop the fighting. we must not keep the multinationals in the lurch and choose the dutch interest. choose a repair paid for by those who benefit and do not contribute now. choose a sustainable and prosperous europe and the netherlands.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i would also like to make it very clear that the european semester is not the right time to do this, since we are faced with a huge package of financial aid. we are currently seeing in the rule of law how member states are in some cases dealing with commission recommendations. we need concrete ways of identifying and also of checking, also as the european parliament, the respective flows of payments, the type of projects, those who receive the money - whether it is the special committees from a technical point of view or whether we are in the committee on budgetary control from a financial point of view. we must get out of a kind of post-carriage era in the field of the transmission of data concerning the financial and budgetary spheres. we need a digital information system that provides all the necessary data in real time and, in particular, that makes the economic stakeholders public or visible, so that we no longer have to wait for months for information, as is the case today, and to start out-of-the-box research operations to obtain adequate information on projects and their financing.

for the greens/efa group mr president, i would first like to thank the european commission for this ambitious action plan on combating money laundering and terrorist financing, which incorporates many of the recommendations of the green group. so the problem is not so much the absence of european rules as their effective application by our member states. and so it is indeed time for directives to be partly transposed into regulations, for the whole european legislative arsenal to be transposed into our member states and for the european commission to conduct infringement procedures against states that do not respect the rules, because the fight against money laundering is a priority for citizens. we know that the crisis has opened up new opportunities for criminals and financial criminals, taking advantage of the vulnerability and fears of citizens, and that 98.9% of criminal activity, of criminal money, is not recovered today. and so the priority is also to transpose the directive on the freezing and recovery of assets so that this money is returned first to the victims of crime and also used for investments in our public services.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, it’s actually very sad that we needed a worldwide health crisis to put health at the top of the european agenda, but let’s look forward now. let’s learn from this crisis and let’s ensure that, from now on, health remains at the top of our agenda. beyond enhancing crisis preparedness, we should also work towards a real ‘health in all policies’ approach and take action in three other fundamental domains as well. one: let’s focus on prevention, as this benefits citizens and national health budgets. two: let’s revise our pharmaceutical system to ensure access to affordable medicines for all. and three: let’s reinforce the ‘one health’ approach. any efforts to work on a healthier europe are doomed to fail unless we address the interdependencies between human health and the health of our planet. it’s now up to us. will we remember covid-19 merely as the world’s worst health crisis in recent history, or will we also remember it as the start of a better european public health policy?
in writing. any european health strategy should encourage the rebuilding of our public health systems. this means giving up the bad habit of encouraging member states to cut public health care. this is the real meaning of integrating health issues into all policies: a break with budget cuts and the commodification of health. the public sector not only offers more accessible care, but also has a huge advantage in terms of frontline care and therefore screening. let's support, restore, develop the public hospital. let's invest in prevention and screening. and finally, let's avoid the privatization of the future covid-19 treatment or vaccine. public money finances research and development. the final product should be public too. take the example of american inventor jonas salk, who discovered the polio vaccine. when asked who patented his vaccine, salk replied, "the people". there is no patent. could you patent the sun? other the polio vaccine thus entered the international market without patents. the disease has now been eradicated in many parts of the world.
in writing. – the european union is in dire need of health reform. we need increased eu cooperation to create a union-wide, future-proof health care system, set common european minimum health standards, and to use the lessons learnt from covid-19 to propose reforms. first, we must formalise the working methods established during the pandemic and ensure the creation of a health-crisis unit. we also need a european health union and health care index to track the progress of healthcare systems. second, we need to futureproof our medicinal product purchasing system. all member states should be able to fairly negotiate medicinal prices with industry and avoid unfair competition for medicinal prices, accessibility and distribution. we must also upgrade our pharmaceutical patenting rules which currently stifle innovation and need a monitoring system that ensures that national stockpiling initiatives are proportionate to countries’ needs. we should also lower our dependency on third countries via an updated eu strategy to address obstacles in the pharmaceutical supply chain and to attract back manufacturing sectors for raw materials that previously moved out of the eu. finally, we must invest more heavily in research and innovation to ensure that our health care systems are always at their strongest.
in writing. this pandemic should be a game-changer for our vision of healthcare in the eu and for our determination to put the eu in the cockpit of such a crisis. we have to seize this momentum. first of all, cooperation must be the starting point. we must exploit the european economies of scale by pooling expertise and resources. the vaccination strategy is a good test case in this respect. but let us go on with this in the context of the cancer plan, the new pharmaceutical strategy, etc. and a strong health care system is a guarantee of solidarity. affordable health care is a fundamental right, interwoven with our european pillar of social rights. the location of your crib should no longer be the determinant of how long you can live in good health. thirdly, the protection of the people on the front line must be paramount. not just the many health care workers, but also workers who may be exposed to the virus on a daily basis. prevention through a healthy working environment is also an essential part of this. our respect for those who care in care must be more than a token applause. let's learn lessons, not just for the next crisis, but also structurally. an economy of well-being puts people at the centre and health in all policies assumes public health as a criterion in policy decisions.

there's a lot of talk in this house about the rule of law by people who have no idea what it means. for me, the rule of law means freedom, national independence and democracy. the rule of law means rule by law instead of rule by man. my generation has fought for 30 years to make this not just an inherited privilege for those living in the western half of europe. we fought for it, but i'll also tell you what the rule of law is not, which is what many people here would like to use it for. it does not mean asking for unfixed, unwritten rules. it doesn't mean double standards. the one where you punish someone for the same thing that someone else is praised for. it's not about sanctioning or silencing political ideologies that some people don't like. and that's what some people here use the rule of law password for. they're being emptied and used for party politics. the rule of law is not a matter of bending the rules of the vote to get the result you want, as was done recently in the vote on the sargentini report. a man of the rule of law should not be afraid of dissent, but give the accused the floor to defend himself. in the recent debate on hungary, you refused to allow the hungarian minister to express his position. one of the oldest principles of the rule of law comes from roman law: nullum crimen sine lege, no punishment without law. in this house, however, solutions are being pushed that do not determine the violation of the rule of law on the basis of normative, legal criteria, but that impose severe financial penalties even in the case of suspicion and do not guarantee equal treatment. this is the communist model. it's an orwellian model of thought policing that wants to punish intent. it's the soviet way, the extortion of the law, to blackmail and exorcise political dissent. we've seen this before. we've been victims of similar pursuits before. we've defeated them, and we're not gonna let them come back through the back door. europe deserves a better, a much better rule of law. and madam president, if you would allow me a point of order: in this house some members like to lecture certain countries to certain politicians about observing rules in the name of the rule of law. however, this parliament is in breach itself in this moment, of its own rules of procedure rules 182 and 187 which clearly say that voting can start only after the debate on the floor is finished. we are debating still, but the voting has already started. this is a violation of our own values. so, let's sweep in front of our own house first before we condemn others.

madam president, first of all we must recognize that the agreement reached by the european council in recent days is a historic and important step in the process of european integration. this agreement is a continuation of the request made by this parliament in our resolution of may, in which we called for the creation of a financing instrument through community debt to be repaid with more own resources to help the european economy in the face of this crisis. an important, key, fundamental agreement in which some government, such as the spanish government, led by pedro sánchez, has played an essential role. but there are also some issues in this agreement that we have to question. and the first of these is the regular budget, because some items are not growing as they should. and i will make special reference to the just transition fund, because those regions - like my own, asturias - which are also facing a major challenge, namely to modernise their economy, need stronger european support. and on the other hand, for the recovery and resilience mechanism - an instrument to help countries in investment and reforms - there is a governance in the european council agreement that this parliament cannot approve. we need governance with clear democratic legitimacy; we need governance in which the citizens see their interests represented. and this parliament will have its say and we will meet the council in the tripartite negotiations before the end of the year.
president, thirteen billion euros: this is the amount that belgium has to contribute net to the european union's coronavirus recovery fund. in addition, our contribution to the european budget is increasing by more than one billion euros a year. however, belgium is already facing a 50 billion euro budget deficit and 120% of public debt due to this corona crisis. who's going to explain this to their constituents? who is going to explain to my citizens that there is no money for decent pensions, that there is no money for health care, that there is no money for tens of thousands of families on the brink of ruin, but that billions are available for the european union's st. nicholas policy? i can't explain that. or maybe it is. the more money the eurocrats get their hands on, the more power they gain over states and, therefore, the more control they gain over citizens. creating dependence, without people ever having chosen to do so. because for 15 years these euro-fanatics have lost every referendum. years of sabotage and blackmail over brexit did not work. to prevent even more countries from playing their democracy and choosing their freedom, they now want to introduce european debts and european taxes. in this way they want to build a financial and fiscal prison from which there is no escape. this european union is becoming something we never chose.
mr. chairman, please! the european council has reached a difficult compromise on a new budget - a budget that is very important for the recovery of the eu economy after the crisis that has hit us. for this, the participants - all the participants - are to be thanked. i think the role that prime minister mateusz morawiecki played in reaching this agreement deserves special recognition. the criticisms of the conclusions of the summit are not only exaggerated, but sometimes they are hypocritical. what my fellow meps, and unfortunately also those from poland, call the rule of law mechanism is an attempt to create a non-treaty tool to influence the member states, a mechanism which is not based on facts and data but on sympathy and antipathy towards national governments. the fact is that all new laws that are adopted in poland are in line with existing law and the european treaties. so poland is a country of law. there's no doubt about it, but there are some in this room who would like to use the rule of law to put pressure on unpopular prime ministers or unpopular governments. poland shall make exemplary use of european funds in accordance with the treaties and polish law. that should be the only criterion that should be used in this type of discussion. the council took its decision on the basis of precisely these types of considerations. i hope that parliament will not spoil the outcome of these difficult negotiations by making unrealistic demands. more than ever, europeans need cooperation, not idle bickering.
president. - the next item is the report (doc. with the political commitment of the heads of state and government for a european recovery fund and a multiannual budget, we can reinvest in our european future and in our european economy. the size of such a european recovery fund is unprecedented. the so-called 'next generation eu' plan consists of €390 billion in funds and €360 billion in loans. on the one hand, funds prevent the debt of the southern member states from going off the rails again, and on the other hand, loans ensure that each country pays a fair share of its own recovery. the 5 billion euro brexit fund is also necessary and good for our country belgium and the eu. but the work is not done. the multi-year budget as it stands now cuts research and development very heavily, just as we're looking for a vaccine for corona and as we're making cancer a priority. investments in research and development and the horizon europe programme are essential if the eu is to shape the future. i also strongly disagree with the large savings in health programmes, erasmus+ and defence. the eu cannot achieve strategic autonomy without full defence cooperation. the negotiations between the council and the european parliament are now beginning, as a second wave of the corona virus is coming and a hard brexit is becoming more and more likely. so time is short, but we cannot help but agree that the budget agreement can and must be improved.
in writing. the agreement reached by the european council is very hot and cold. the hot issue is the recovery plan: how it will be financed, the strong and welcome solidarity it contains, the support to the member states that need it most. the cold is the draft multiannual financial framework which gives us more than the impression that the council has compensated for the generosity and strength of the recovery plan by incomprehensible cuts in the draft european budget in key strategic sectors such as agriculture or health, for which the funds allocated are totally sufficient. it is up to us in the european parliament, in the coming weeks, to give this draft financial framework a boost and to give it as much strength and ambition as the recovery plan.
mr president, it took a crisis like this for the european union to start taking decisions that had been awaited for too long - 32 years since the last modification of the own resources system. with a 20th century income model, it is impossible to meet the challenges of the 21st century. we need more and better resources of our own for many reasons. in advance, to return the money from the recovery instrument. the alternative would be to increase national contributions, further complicating the debate on net donors and net recipients, or to reduce the union budget, i.e. less europe: unacceptable. we urgently need more own resources to give the union greater financial autonomy vis-à-vis the member states, but also to fight climate change, to promote harmonization and fiscal justice and to promote financial stability. the july council proposal is not enough. it is not enough to tax plastic waste; it is not enough to talk about the carbon border tax, the digital tax or the emission rights as a possibility: they are an imperative necessity. and, as this parliament proposes, we also need the financial transaction tax. we need, finally - and i conclude - to start building a genuine european tax system.
mr. chairman, please! it should be noted that our debate today is really about two issues that are related, but two issues. the first concerns the council's decision to launch the procedure to create the opportunity to build a new next generation eu fund. secondly, what this entails is the future of building new own income. this is important to remember, because this second debate obviously requires a different type of decision and agreement between parliament and the council, and the first debate does indeed require time. it also takes time because when we were working and discussing the budget for 2021-2027, when we had not even talked about this fund, we were planning specific expenditures. then the budget was frozen or cut, and we were told that it would be compensated by the fund. it now appears that if ratification does not proceed as planned, the fund could be seriously jeopardized. i would like to stress very clearly in this connection that if both the minister and the commissioner say that we are talking about one package, which we agree with, then in this package everything must be interrelated and everything is interdependent, which means that parliament's agreement to the multiannual budget must be linked to the creation of an opportunity for a new fund. without this fund, the budget is too small and not enough for certain actions that we have envisaged. if it's a package, then let's treat it as a package and we're ready to have this kind of conversation and start working very, very intensively.
mr president, the remedy for unpredictability lies in the ability to make and keep promises, as hannah arendt so rightly said. in july you, the commission and the council made a promise: to finance the recovery plan with new resources by making the polluters, the digital giants and the financial giants pay. now, let's do what we said. you want to make europe fair and popular, so keep that promise quickly. the alternative is to increase national contributions, to ask more of taxpayers or to cut the eu budgets even further. if you want to make the union more unpopular, this would be the way to go. but no, this is not an alternative. the effectiveness and credibility of the plan depend on it. a binding timetable for the introduction of new own resources is needed before the end of the year. finally, let us respect the treaties: the own resources are anchored there, the current ones are very outdated, so it is high time to set up new ones.
president, the corona crisis is being used to introduce a new european tax. that's what i'm worried about. it starts with a european tax on plastic waste and then there are many other forms of taxation that are in the pipeline: i would like to see a european digital tax, a european financial transaction tax, a european carbon cap tax and so on. these european taxes are actually a growth hormone for the formation of a european state - which some speakers also object to - where all power goes with money and so on. of course, it will require the approval of the various member states and also of my own: the approval of the second chamber and the first chamber. i am very curious to see the position of the cda and the vvd, because in the netherlands they say they are opposed to the european debt community, but in brussels they are happy to cooperate with it. so i ask them, where are they? and if they don't have an answer, their masks fall off.
mr president, the question you are asking us today is this: do you want a european tax, a new european tax, yes or no? personally, i say there is no quick answer to this question which is much more complex than that. the question is, in fact, who will pay for this european tax? and now, what i'm going to say is not necessarily going to please the right but the question is, is it time today to take the financial transaction tax out of the drawers? a real financial transaction tax and not a canada dry version, a coca light version, no! a real tax, which, for once, makes the big speculators pay instead of always the workers, instead of always those who make our society run. a tax that targets the financial markets and allows us to mobilise public funds and put them at the disposal of jobs, the real economy and the people. for once, instead of lying in front of the financial markets, let's tax them!
mr. president, when politicians say: i am very careful, however, because this reasoning is often used as an excuse to implement policies without considering the consequences. it's the same now. i think we can all agree on the possible introduction of new european taxes. corporations must pay their fair share of taxes, just like all citizens. but a large number of my colleagues here in parliament are convinced that new taxes, such as a financial transaction tax or a european company tax, will protect the citizens of the european union. and i can predict to you that new taxes, left or right, will eventually fall on the hard-working people, so let's just not do that. exceptional circumstances call for careful, thoughtful measures at this time.
mr president, mr minister, mr commissioner, i have to ask you to stop this. in taking part in the debate on the union's own resources, i would like to recall that the compromise reached in this respect at the council meeting in july was based on a number of principles which we in parliament should respect. first, new sources of revenue for the union budget should not be directly charged to member states' budgets but should be linked to the benefits of the common market, and thus should primarily be a digital tax or a financial transaction tax. the non-recycled plastic charge adopted by the council only achieved consensus because it was proposed at a relatively low level and a mechanism was introduced to limit its regressivity. the commission also noted that the commission had not provided any evidence that the union industry would be able to benefit from the exemption. only if parliament supports these arrangements will they have a chance of being adopted by the parliaments of the 27 member states. unfortunately, the parliament's report departs from them in many places.
mr president, by agreeing to a eur 750 billion package, eu leaders have crossed the rubicon. yet, just as caesar’s success was far from guaranteed, this package alone might not bring us the recovery that we wish and the sustainability that we need. we may run out of steam before we reach our destination, and without proper own resources we will run out of steam. to avoid cutting back on future eu spending, the eu should have income to repay its debts, and this income should derive from areas where european action is of added value. to make polluters pay a fair price, to make digital companies and large corporates pay a fair share, we need european action including a common consolidated corporate tax base, and by asking for this now, the european parliament is paving the way for a real success.
mr president, an unprecedented crisis calls for unprecedented measures. that is why we are negotiating a budget with an ambitious recovery package. through low interest rates and the high creditworthiness of the european commission, we will borrow cheaply to invest in our european economy. but the reimbursement of the recovery plan must be timely and correct. we therefore call for fair own resources and a binding timetable. importers of cheap steel, for example, that do not respect our environmental standards will be contributing to the recovery through a carbon levy. digital multinationals, which pay little tax here and make a profit in the european single market, will also have to contribute to our recovery. this european recovery plan is a historic opportunity which we must seize. if the european union succeeds in reviving the economy, we can win the confidence of the citizens. but if we fail to repay, each country will be left to fend for itself, and we must prevent this at all costs.
in writing. ladies and gentlemen, i invite you to support this text to the end because it is essential that all sectors of the economy including the maritime sector contribute and participate today in the common effort to complete the transition to achieve the goal of climate neutrality in the european union by 2050. i would remind you that shipping is currently the only sector not specifically targeted by a co2 reduction target or specific mitigation measures. if nothing changes, global emissions from the shipping sector are expected to increase by 50% to 250% by 2050. i therefore urge you to vote: for the inclusion of the maritime sector in the co2 emission trading scheme (cets); for the introduction of eu-level energy efficiency standards for ships; the commission is also proposing to regulate the access of the most polluting ships to eu ports and to make it possible to force moored ships to use electricity from the dock. i also say that an ocean fund should be created from the revenue generated. thank you .
mr president, turkey is an important strategic partner for the eu, both economically and geopolitically, but that does not negate its many misdemeanours: backsliding democracy and human rights; devastating actions in syria and libya; disregard of international law with regard to greece and cyprus; lack of respect for christian communities. and this is not the first, second, third or even fourth time we’ve had this exact same debate in this parliament. turkey has not presented itself as a worthy candidate country for years. we need a fundamental strategic rethink of our relationship. it’s time the eu accepts that at best we can only have a business relationship with turkey. accession negotiations should be stopped and the budget allocation also for this process should be put to better use. we need the money for other things. trying to turn erdoğan into a reliable and cooperative partner is like trying to flog a dead horse. i don’t want to do that.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when we talk about erdogan's actions, we are sadly accustomed to using harsh terms. however, what is happening in the eastern mediterranean is far beyond any limits, with ankara now directly threatening athens after months and months of illegal drilling and agreements contrary to international law, such as the memorandum of understanding with libya on the delimitation of maritime jurisdictions. let erdogan be clear about one thing: when he threatens greece or cyprus, he is not simply threatening his neighbours, with brussels as a mediator. it is threatening the whole european union. and we as europe must react unitedly, by keeping a channel of dialogue open, by working for de-escalation, of course, but the extraordinary european council next week must send a strong signal, with a package of sanctions, but also with an embargo on the sale of arms to a country which, in addition to openly violating international law and jeopardising relations within nato itself, continues to regress in respect for human rights, fundamental freedoms and democracy. it is time for europe to pursue its own policy in the mediterranean without being crushed by blackmail from autocrats like president erdogan.
mr president, we should not need to remind ourselves that greek and cypriot borders are european borders – european union borders. when russia invaded georgia, the donbass in ukraine, and crimea, all the european union reacted because russia was not respecting international law or international borders. now that we have this constant bullying at our own european borders, are we going to be silent? are we going to be inactive? aren’t we going to do something? turkey is not a third country. it has a customs union. it is a candidate country and a member of nato, and so it has obligations that other countries don’t have. we therefore have to be firm and clear. it’s european borders that are at stake. (applause)
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr high representative, we have a huge responsibility which comes from the awareness that it is not at all true that turkey cannot be stopped. the problem is that europe is not stopping it: in both syria and libya, russia has taken on the de facto task of carrying out this task. so what's our emergency? we must return to the defence of the mediterranean as europeans, and today the threat is from turkey. how can we achieve this? a proposal could be the establishment of a special envoy of the european union for the eastern mediterranean, precisely with the aim, on the one hand, of settling legal disputes on the law of the sea and, on the other, of guaranteeing a peaceful europe, because turkey's peace towards us, and therefore towards greece and cyprus in particular, is not a provocation: the european parliament has been the victim of a series of attacks on the european parliament, and it is an attack on what we call the neo-ottoman strategy, whose victim is the greek, latin and christian history of europe.
mr president, there is another episode of a chronic crisis in the eastern mediterranean, which has only been aggravated by erdoğan's hegemonic ambitions to fill the void left by the absence of america and the short-circuited nato, at least until the european union has matured in its foreign policy, finally assuming a leading role in the wider mediterranean region and perhaps, mr president, entrusting a european special representative for the eastern mediterranean with the task of consulting turkey at all levels in favour of a peaceful coexistence with the european union. let me emphasize the obvious here: respect for international law and the sovereign rights of greece and cyprus are non-negotiable and are not on anyone's table. europe must therefore cut off erdoğan's neo-ottoman geopolitical appetites for conquest 'because greece and cyprus are europe', stop using double standards, depending on the economic and military interests of some member states, and finally acquire a strong and long-term strategy, from libya to syria, on our wider borders.
president. - at the beginning of march, tens of thousands of migrants, mainly young men of military age, laid siege to the greek border. sultan erdogan used mass migration as a blackmail tool to extract more money for his regime. there was hardly any reaction. erdogan has since learned how weak and indecisive the european union is. today, his regime is violating the territorial waters and airspace of greece. once again, there is hesitation to take action. appeasement doesn't work with this kind of leader. we must bring the erdogan regime to its knees or we will remain at the mercy of turkish blackmail and aggression. so stop the billions for turkey. abolish the customs union and announce economic sanctions. and most of all: the european union must finally stop its accession talks, because turkey is not and will never be part of europe geographically, religiously, culturally and historically.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. mr president, before the pandemic crisis we saw in lebanon many demonstrations by young people, young people who were fighting against corruption, who were fighting against the economic crisis that has affected them, against poverty, against unemployment. but in reality, the pandemic crisis has also been accompanied by a huge explosion, and these young people are in need of our support now. not only the young lebanese, but also so many refugees who were able to settle there following the enormous generosity of the lebanese people. in this sense, i believe that the european union cannot escape this call, this call to support the lebanese population, whether through the instruments it already has, the neighbourhood trust fund, or unrwa, but to respond to the population and the refugees, more than a million syrian refugees, about half a million palestinian refugees. this is also the time to avoid temptation, to have no political leverage, to have no meddling, to have no geopolitical games. european solidarity must be the answer to lebanese generosity.

mr president, the explosion in beirut's port, which caused hundreds of deaths, thousands of injuries, more than 300,000 homeless people and the complete destruction of the city's districts, is adding to the chaos that led millions of lebanese, already in october 2019, to express their anger against an inefficient and corrupt political system. the lebanese people are demanding change: a religiously neutral country, effective and progressive institutions... above all, they want to turn the page on corruption. as we know, lebanon has been the terrain of influence for the regional powers and their allies since its independence. our duty as europeans is to stand by the lebanese. we are facing a humanitarian emergency and we must ensure that europe's resources go directly to the people. we see the urgency of political and economic reconstruction: helping civil society to embrace the reforms demanded by the imf today will give this country the opportunity to return to the economic and political scene and to be able to release €11 billion in aid. this civil society knows what it wants, we have to work with it.
mr president, lebanon is a resilient country that has endured many challenges; but after the tragic explosion in beirut’s harbour, lebanon is on the brink of disaster. the lebanese people are sick and tired of having to be resilient, and i understand them. in this dire situation, the first priority must be to form an impartial government that is able to tackle the immediate crisis. i call upon europe to support, in any way possible, the formation of such a government. decades of corruption must come to an end. the lebanese people deserve leaders who solve problems, not cause them. they deserve leaders who inspire hope, not despair. these leaders exist. i have met them. they are young, capable and visionary. they fight for social justice, human rights and sustainability. many of them are women who struggle in the face of patriarchal elites. we have to support them. europe needs to support them.
mr president, mr high representative, the lebanese people are a friendly people, they are our brothers. in the present situation we must support them, of course, through our declarations, but even more so by accompanying them in the various reforms that are so necessary: political reform, on the one hand, but also disarmament, particularly of the militias, in accordance with un security council resolution 1599, or, of course, the provision of the budgetary support necessary to rebuild the country. the lebanese are wounded, but they are standing. i ask that we can send a european parliament delegation quickly - i have already written to that effect - to be accompanied by the high representative to create, further establish and further build these important links between europe and the lebanese people and to accompany these necessary reforms in support of the lebanese people.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, many eu citizens craved holidays this summer and some did go on holidays, to spain for instance. after their holidays, belgian tourists returning from spain faced 14 days of quarantine. dutch tourists only faced 10 days, and french (tourists) zero. for us belgians, spain is a red zone in its entirety, but not so for the french authorities. we cannot explain these diverging practices to our citizens. it creates an idea of arbitrariness amongst our citizens, while it is very important that everyone keeps following the guidelines. it does not make sense from a health perspective. all eu citizens are equal, also in the eyes of the virus. a dutch person who visits a spanish village faces the same risk of infection as a greek, swedish or slovakian person who went to the same village. it also does not make sense from an internal market perspective. it creates uncertainties for businesses and cross-border workers if member states apply different quarantine lengths or definitions of essential travel. luckily, the commission indeed proposed earlier this month that member states would use common criteria and thresholds to base their travel restrictions on. as a parliament we should support this. member states should follow the ecdc’s recommendations and agree on common rules for returning travellers, identical colour coding and common criteria. i hope that all member states can agree on a common viewpoint on this matter in the interest of all eu citizens. on behalf of the group of the european right mr president, we have rightly stressed that public health is a member state competence, and in our case even a state competence. and i want to keep it that way. the policy, the choices, the funding must remain there. the eu does not have harmonisation powers, as is stated in the treaty. where can the eu add value? well, first by coordinating the color codes: the commission has already proposed a number of measures to improve the quality of the products. we have seen that the same zone is coloured red in france and orange in belgium. those codes also mean the same thing. secondly, to ensure the maximum possible freedom of movement for persons and goods. the european union is a great example of this. thirdly, it is also good that the commission is taking the lead in the purchase of vaccine programmes. i want reliable information on that: about the test, the results, the progress. but i also ask the commission to be transparent about the purchase contracts with the farm giants, to be transparent about the negotiating teams. i have asked the commissioner for information on this in the context of transparency of administration. i'm waiting for the answer to that. the commission asks for transparency, often shows transparency. well, show that you have nothing to hide: practice what you preach.
mr president, covid— 19 is still omnipresent in our daily lives, but also in the political debates. in the eu, we tend to focus a lot on economic recovery, but we must not forget that the health of every european citizen is even more important. european citizens worry about the future, about their health, about their jobs, about the climate, and about their future. this is the moment when we as europe have to show that we are ready to help them, and that we’re on their side, step by step. how will we reassure people that there will be an affordable vaccine for all of us who need it? how will we convince people that we will coordinate member states’ policies better during the next health crisis? the only way to show people we mean it is to maintain budgets that we set aside for eu for health and horizon europe. these flagship programmes are crucial for the next generation of europe and we will fight for them. but the most important call that i want to strengthen is of course to redirect investments towards a green economy. innovative companies are showing the way forward to a more circular economy and sustainable product design, as well as a socially just transition. let’s keep our targets sufficiently high. let’s listen to what the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi) decided last week. we need at least 60% or 65% to be ambitious. the more we reduce now, the more relaxed we will be towards 2050.

in writing. the messages of the president of the european commission at today's state of the union were very clear and unequivocal. the crippled world left behind by the covid-19 pandemic needs to be put back on its feet as soon as possible. this is why we need decisive action from europe. von der leyen is promising the europeans protection, stability and opportunity. and he has my full support. i welcome the construction of the health union, one of the most important political projects in post-pandemic europe. the virus cannot be stopped at the border. it is high time that the union was given more powers in the field of health policy. i support the president's initiative to strengthen the key eu agencies and to create a new one for biomedicine, research and development. i also welcome the announcement of a binding mechanism to set up a european minimum wage and the european commission's commitment to reduce emissions by 55% by 2030, although i would like an even more ambitious plan myself. we need to further transform, digitise and green our economies and europe to be truly future-ready. at the same time, we must protect the eu's strategic interest and common core values. i therefore particularly welcome von der leyen's initiative to extend the european criminal code to prohibit hatred and all forms of discrimination and to protect the most vulnerable.
in writing. i think that commission president von der leyen has the right priorities: the commission has also proposed a number of measures to improve the quality of the environment. work must pay, which makes a european framework for minimum wages a real must. together with a strong network of temporary unemployment, we are building a genuine social union. the strong emphasis on collective bargaining is crucial in this respect. the commission will also be supporting the implementation of the new eu-wide climate change strategy, which will be implemented by the member states in the context of the eu-wide climate change strategy. we must work towards a strong european health union. this will enable us to act more effectively in the next crisis. the re-examination of the distribution of healthcare responsibilities will also undoubtedly be a topic of discussion in the discussions on the future of europe. i also welcome the new european agency for biomedical research and the strengthened role for the european centre for disease prevention and control and the european medicines agency. a first major task for the european union is the development and deployment of a vaccine. by working together to develop a safe vaccine that is accessible to everyone, we can save lives. now we must all turn this ambitious 'state of the union' into action!

mr president, it is beyond doubt that diversity of company boards is an asset, because diversity means that the board members have different views, different backgrounds, different experiences. so, the question then is why was diversity of company boards then doing better than it is today? i believe that one problem is hidden criteria for the selection of board members, where still the stereotype is a certain type of white man, and this should now be broken somehow. we have seen, for instance, in finland, which has so far chosen the way of self-regulation, that you come to a certain degree: you can have 29% of women on listed company boards, but you won’t get that further with self-regulation. i believe that we should not be satisfied with that low number, and that’s why we need this european regulation. i also very much believe that, because women are barely represented in those committees in companies which actually choose board members, this has to be corrected. in finland, only 14% are women on this kind of body in companies.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the post-corona era it is of the utmost importance that we keep women on board, both literally and figuratively. figuratively, because it was women who were predominantly on the front lines of fighting the virus, but also literally bringing it on board, by giving women a vote on the boards of publicly traded companies. in 2020, women are still underrepresented. only three out of ten board members are women. in some member states, this is less than one in ten. and only 7.5% of ceos are women. countries like france and belgium show that good legislation does make a difference. more women on the board means a boost, an enrichment of companies. this is the result of numerous studies by the ilo and the oecd. this talent, this source of expertise and creativity, we must tap into now. there is an urgent need for a different corporate culture, where women and men take the lead together. we must strive for a society where quotas are not necessary and equal representation is a given. but until that happens, we need clear legislation. it is therefore time for the member states to come together in the council, to take the matter out of the freezer and to make the eu a point for gender equality, including in the executive boards.
