mr president, it has obviously not been easy to find a good balance in this report. i think this debate shows it. and from the s&d group, our perspective is that there certainly are aspects that could have been better included in the process. we need to take good care of our forests. there is no doubt about it. they are one of our green lungs. i’m from a country which has a large and thriving forest myself, and for me a good day is a day when i walk in the forest with my dog or spend time there enjoying the biodiversity. but that being said, i also believe in a sustainable forest management that it’s possible to combine, and i’m convinced that the objectives of forest protection, strengthening biodiversity and wood protection can actually be challenges that together go as a big opportunity for our member states and also for the climate and climate policy in the eu. so i look forward to seeing the commission’s proposal taking that into account.
mr president, i thank the rapporteur, mr sarvamaa, for the good work he has done. forests and forest resources are of great environmental, economic and social importance. in spain, galicia accounts for more than half of the total wood harvest in the country. the forestry sector is strategic for my region, galicia, as i said. we must work towards sustainable and multifunctional forests and woodlands, which are a source of natural and economic wealth. in galicia we are committed to a management of forest resources based on the bio-economy. in this respect, i was pleased to see that mr sarvamaa's report is in line with this and also advocates the strengthening of sustainable forest management. in addition, forests are also a way to boost the economic reactivation needed after the covid-19 pandemic; for example, the xunta de galicia is evaluating the creation of a center for the manufacture of textile fibers, from wood, within the framework of the circular economy. another very relevant issue is the prevention of forest fires, a terrible threat that spain and other southern countries face every summer and which is aggravated by climate change. fires know no boundaries. within the european union, as proposed in the report we are now discussing, there is a need to allocate more funding and develop greater cooperation and joint action capacity to combat forest fires. it is important that the european commission incorporates this element into the forestry strategy it is preparing.
mr president, forests and wooded areas currently cover 43% of the union's surface. they are true cleaners, removing 10% of all greenhouse gases emitted in europe. but in recent years there has been a marked increase in the cutting and deforestation of forests. it is therefore crucial that we do everything we can to balance the demand for wood with the need to preserve these environmentally vital ecosystems. we need effective, coherent and harmonised management of our forestry heritage at european level. the commission's proposal will therefore have to focus on the three pillars of economic sustainability, societal sustainability and, of course, environmental sustainability. the challenges are many: stepping up the fight against illegal logging, promoting the circular bioeconomy, agroforestry and building resilience to natural disasters. it is our responsibility to recognise the value and importance of forests to society and to develop effective policies to ensure that this value is protected, maintained and enhanced.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. 285,000 hectares of forest are currently destroyed in germany. the effects of climate change: bark beetles, drought, new fungal diseases. the forest has long since ceased to be a store of co2. i must say that this report does not meet the new challenges. the emphasis is still on use. yeah, nothing against a sensible use. but can we in europe be responsible for the burning of wood in power stations so that member states can improve their carbon footprints? we can't accept that anymore. that is why we must clearly reject it in the future. we must put our forests under increased protection. we point the finger at brazil, but in europe we are not even able to protect the carpathians, to protect the fsm areas. in germany, a motorway, the a 49, is being built through ffh areas. we can't accept that anymore. so we have to build a new forest, natural forests, away from the spruce, pine and eucalyptus plantations. and that includes changing hunting laws so that trophy hunting doesn't eat away at the forest in some areas.
mr president, although responsibility for forests and forestry lies entirely with the member states, we are well aware that the european union, through its policies, has a fundamental influence on the quality of forests. with europe facing desertification, forest destruction from drought, pests, fires and other impacts of climate change, we must choose what is more important to us - the health and long-term impact of a diverse forestry mix or the short-term economic benefits of intensive forest management and the expansion of forest cultivation for biomass. i think if we want forests to remain our lungs, to produce oxygen, to absorb at least 10 percent of the greenhouse gases we produce, to retain water, and to retain a number of other functions, including the production function that is talked about a lot here and that i don't appreciate, then we need to put the protection of forests first today. i expect the commission to adopt a number of new measures, including a revision of the renewable energy directive. i also think it's worth considering whether it's a good idea to replace all the disposable items that used to be made of plastic with wood on a massive scale, whether there's a better solution to reducing consumption than increasing it in the form of wood products. i personally have a great deal of respect for the work that my colleague sarvamaa has done, but still, the emphasis on environmental protection and forest protection... mr president, i am in favour - and i want to make this very clear in my speech - of an ambitious european forestry strategy which will be translated into future regulations at european level so that the national management of forestry policy today can gradually give way to european management. but now this policy is national competence. we are also facing the great fight against climate change and we must take account of our climate commitments made at the paris summit and the proposals of the european green deal. that is why this resolution states that the forestry strategy must be an important pillar within this green deal. i think it is a balanced proposal which takes into account important aspects such as the need to preserve and promote biodiversity and the importance of reforestation. that is why i am calling for a vote in favour of this proposal and against amendments nos 1 and 2.
mr. president, please! first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr sarvamay. it is clear that it comes from finland, which knows how to manage forests, has a lot of forests, so this report is also comprehensive and also coordinated with the many stakeholders we have worked with in the preparation of this report. forests have a multi-purpose function. just as sustainable development is made up of environmental, economic and social components, forest management is made up of all these components. forests also play an important role in the green deal, our new development paradigm, and it is right to highlight this protective function, these important areas of biodiversity, water storage, firefighting and reptile control. but we certainly need to talk about, in addition to the carbon sink, the economic use of forests, what farmers can actually do with forests, and what we can do with research to upgrade the bioeconomy, where forestry plays an important role. i am glad that we slovenians can be proud of our sustainable development of forest management, as mr sarvamaa also recognised in his report. farmers are the ones who will continue to ensure the sustainable development of forestry, and it is right to support it through the common agricultural policy and the rural development programme, while also enabling good management where forests are protected.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i believe that the text as voted by the committee on agriculture is a good compromise, that it is going in the right direction, that is to say, in the direction of outlining the commitment we must make in the coming years to defend our forestry heritage, taking account of course of the environmental aspects, which are fundamental, but also of the economic and social aspects which are linked to it. we are talking about an important strategy - it has been emphasized by all my colleagues - which has already led to important results in recent years. it has been said: 43% of the territory is covered by forests, which provide jobs for more than three million people: that is why it is important to have an ambitious, coordinated and coherent strategy at european level. i think that this text is going in the right direction.
mr president, i would like to thank you for your service. the eu forest strategy post-2020 can best achieve a balanced representation of the three pillars of sustainability through sustainable forest management. i am pleased that this report confirms the importance of this. hungary has almost doubled its forest area in the last century. we're aiming to have 27 percent of the world's forest cover by 2030. this is an important part of the hungarian climate and nature action plan. i would also like to emphasise that efforts to halt the decline of biodiversity are welcome. however, this can be done most effectively within the framework of sustainable forest management, and it is regrettable that the publication of the biodiversity strategy preceded the forestry strategy. this was a mistake on the part of the european commission, in my opinion. this was supposed to happen in parallel, in a coordinated way. the eu's biodiversity strategy is a key instrument for achieving the objectives of the lisbon strategy. however, in the absence of a preliminary assessment, it is likely that these will not achieve the expected effect and therefore comprehensive impact assessments are required. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the eu's environmental and climate action programmes, including the implementation of the eu's climate action strategy. this is a matter of great concern. this is why i think it is very important that the principles of sustainable forest management are the guiding principles in both strategies.
mr president, european forests absorb 10% of the carbon emissions of the european union. forests and the forestry sector have the potential to make an increasing contribution to combating climate change and to achieving the objectives of the european green deal for a climate-neutral economy. healthy forests and resilient ecosystems have natural protective functions to adapt to climate change. that is why we must first of all take care of the ecological state of forests, combat illegal and naked logging, promote forestry practices and preserve natural, old and age-old forests, which are an irreplaceable source of biodiversity. forests play an important socioeconomic role in rural and least populated areas of the european union, providing jobs and livelihoods for local people (including ecotourism, mushroom and berry gathering, beekeeping). in order to achieve the higher environmental objectives of the new cap, it is important to continue supporting the forestry sector and the development of the bioeconomy, to apply agroforestry practices and to incentivise foresters to protect, grow and manage forests in a sustainable way.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i believe that we must stop dealing with the three main issues of the forest. first, the economic issue which is decisive for three million people, families, in europe. secondly, the environmental and climate challenge, of course, to protect biodiversity, but also to ensure that the whole forest can participate in the fight against global warming by storing co2 and also to prepare our forest in terms of planting so that this forest is not harmed by global warming, as can be seen in some european countries. finally, there is the very important social issue, namely the access of the population to the forest and the related tourism issue. it is not necessary to do this, but we must find the necessary interactions between these three issues, which are obviously decisive. the report, on the whole, is a step in the right direction and respects these balances and, above all, provides for these interactions between these three essential issues. there is a weakness, though. the one of european sovereignty. today we export a large proportion of our raw materials from various member states, especially to china. we have to admit that we do not have the same reciprocity, because china prohibits the export of raw wood to other parts of the world. we must act to make this sovereignty much stronger, to ensure that tomorrow this raw material is processed at home, in short circuits, to ensure that the whole chain can develop and there, we will respond concretely to employment, the environment and the reality of the climate challenge.
in writing. it is in our interest to ensure energy independence in the european union. we should not be dependent on importing fossil fuels or energy from the burning of fossil fuels from third countries. the less we burn fossil fuels in europe, the healthier our people are and the longer our lives. achieving climate objectives is also important for europe from a security perspective. a greener europe depends less on third countries for its energy policy. energy issues are closely linked to our sovereignty.
in writing. the scientific consensus is clear: we need to get to a 65 percent reduction in emissions by 2030. and yet the commission came up with a much less ambitious objective. fortunately, the european parliament is doing better, but here too there is no majority for a climate law that would simply reflect the scientific consensus. isn't that weird? how could you do that? it's got to be economic interests. we know why the far right is doing everything it can to avoid ambitious climate targets. they want to protect the major shareholders of oil multinationals. that's why they like to spit on the young people who come out on the streets for a livable planet. but even the traditional parties are stalling, hesitating, when there is just no time. why would i? because they realise that really ambitious targets would also mean that we would ask large multinationals to contribute, and not make the average worker pay with all kinds of eco-taxes? that we should break with the logic of the market and put energy policy back into public hands, instead of leaving it to the english, electrabel or shells of this world? that we need a completely different model of production, because capitalism doesn't offer a future for our planet?

vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i thank the honourable members for all their comments and i definitely take note of all they have said. what i heard today confirms the commission’s resolve to act swiftly and address the shortcomings of the union’s anti-money laundering legal framework. there were some questions which i would like to answer, especially from mr giegold and mr urtasun. you were asking about cases where we have sent formal letters to member states, and these are publically known. the commission has opened infringement procedures against member states that failed to notify transposition of the 5th anti-money laundering directive against cyprus, hungary, netherlands, portugal, romania, slovakia, slovenia and spain. in parallel, we are continuing to check those member states that have fully and correctly transposed the 4th anti-money laundering directive. i cannot disclose here to which member states we have sent informal letters. however, rest assured, that over the course of the next years, we will address all member states. let me stress again, the enforcement and effective implementation of the existing rules remain a top priority for the commission. we are also stepping up practical work to help national authorities detect money laundering. for example, the interconnection of beneficial ownership – ownership registers – should be operational in 2021. a similar interconnection between bank account registers is also part of our action plan, as it will speed up cross—border access to bank account information. however, criminals are highly adaptive and their methods are constantly evolving. the fincen revelations clearly illustrate the ongoing challenges that the main actors face in their efforts to detect illegal financial flows and prevent criminals from using the financial system to launder dirty money. our framework must therefore continuously evolve in order to keep pace with technological developments and the growing sophistication of financial crime. we are up to the challenge. in the first quarter of 2021, we will propose an ambitious anti-money laundering package in order to further strengthen the capacity to fight money laundering and protect the integrity of the financial system. in a single market, we can no longer afford to work in silos. we need to build bridges between anti-money laundering supervisors and financial intelligence units. for this, we need not only a stronger regulatory framework but also strong european union level structures to ensure its implementation. i wanted to be fair to those who mentioned the cayman islands. i wanted to clarify. there are two lists: one list is anti-money laundering and the other is fiscal cooperation tax. so some members were mixing this up, but okay. some members were speaking about tax non-cooperative jurisdiction, but of course we have to deal with this too. thank you for this debate. the commission and my colleague valdis dombrovskis, who was not able to attend this meeting today, we look forward to continuing to work with you on these crucial issues in order to further strengthen the integrity of the financial system and to ensure that dirty money finds no place to hide in the european union. thank you once again for this fruitful debate.

in writing. the publication of the fincen files, which reveal suspicious activity reports from european banking institutions to the us anti-money laundering agency, totalling usd 2 099 billion over the period 2000-2017, demonstrates, despite the rules adopted, once again the legislative gaps in the european order in the fight against money laundering and the ease with which illicit profits obtained by criminal organisations are laundered in europe and worldwide. at the july plenary session we welcomed the commission communication on an action plan to update the fourth and fifth anti-money laundering directives. i would like to stress the need and urgency of establishing a single body of rules at community level, including the transposition of the provisions of the anti-money laundering directive into a regulation. to establish a european aml/cft supervisory authority and a support and coordination mechanism for fius; ensure effective cooperation and exchange of information between them and the law enforcement authorities. tighter rules on suspicious money movements, greater transparency by all financial operators and coordinated investigative activity between member states can stop money laundering by criminal organisations by ensuring security for citizens and healthy competition between businesses.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, what we are discussing here today is people’s lives. talking about long—term care facilities means talking about both those giving and receiving care in a particularly vulnerable situation. due to protective measures, persons in care could no longer receive family and visitors. long—term care facilities could not immediately provide the type of care required in such exceptional circumstances. at particular moments in life we also rely on the commitment and dedication of others. never has this been more visible than during the current pandemic, when carers held the hands of their residents. the staff in long—term care facilities did their best to provide not just comfort and dignity at the end of a precious life, but also a sense of tenderness and care. people working in long—term care facilities deserve more than our applause and gratitude. we can pay tribute to them, but we must also take action. we need to deliver for residents and carers in our policymaking. covid—19 brings unprecedented challenges health—wise, economically and socially. some people are more at risk than others, especially the elderly, and people with a disability. when it comes to social services, there are users and service providers; we cannot help one if we do not help the other. we have seen how care workers and those receiving care are particularly exposed to the virus and its devastating consequences. this is of great concern to us. we need to improve the protection of the most vulnerable in our societies. too many lives have already been lost. we know that social services have been under particular strain and the commission is committed to help. together with commissioners nicolas schmit, who is responsible for jobs and social rights, and stella kyriakides, in charge of health, and helena dalli, responsible for equality, we have held discussions with organisations focusing on services to the elderly, people with disabilities, and others who are in need of long—term care. we are listening to the sector and are assessing different instruments and possible solutions to support both providers and users of these services. not all of today’s problems are new. the capacity of long—term care facilities to deal with any crisis was laid bare by covid—19. clearly, there are structural deficiencies – the lack of staff, difficult working conditions, low salaries and access to services. ongoing issues with affordability and the quality of long—term care services also come to mind, as well as the availability of such services in rural and remote regions. this explains why we are assessing whether our social protection systems are fit to deal with the needs of an aging population. families of women provide a large part of informal long—term care. informal carers deserve better support such as social protection, income replacement, training, counselling and day care. the gender care gap must be addressed, including the equal sharing of informal care responsibilities. we can do this through the implementation of the directive on work-life balance, which should be transposed by 2022. long—term care systems need revamping to better prepare them for rising long—term care needs and future health crisis. funding issues must be addressed at member state level because this is where the competence lies. however, i cannot stand here as a member of the commission and say that our hands are tied. we have been very active. i will share some examples with you. the react—eu programme will continue and extend to the crisis response and repair measures by reinforcing capacities in health and long—term care. in addition, the commission is working with the european centre for disease prevention and control to protect vulnerable groups including older people in residential homes, but also in places like migrant centres and prisons. the european centre for disease prevention and control has published guidance on infection prevention, control and preparedness for covid—19 in healthcare settings as well as guidance regarding surveillance of covid—19 in long—term care facilities. this guidance is a much needed tool for use right across the european union. the joint report of the commission and the social protection committee on the preparedness of long—term care systems in the european union will provide evidence and clear mapping of the challenges that need to be tackled. the commission is committed to implementing the european pillar of social rights, including principles of access to quality health and long—term care. preparations are ongoing for the upcoming action plan for the implementation of the european pillar of social rights to be adopted in 2021 under the guidance of my colleague commissioner schmit. the elderly must fully enjoy these rights, too. it is beyond doubt that covid—19 has hit the elderly hardest. at the same time, our work in tackling demographic change shows that our union is aging. given the current and future context, we need to discuss this and factor it into all aspects of our policy action. let me be clear, dear members of parliament: aging concerns all generations, not just the elderly. in early 2021 we will present a green paper on aging with the aim of launching a wide debate to discuss the long—term impacts of aging, care and pensions, active aging and the capacity of social protection systems to deal with an aging population. we will look at inter—generational solidarity and fairness, impacts on the labour market, the economy, and health and care systems. a substantial part will deal with questions on long—term care. based on the outcome of the consultations, the commission could be in a better position to consider what further initiatives could be taken on long—term care. to conclude, dear ladies and gentlemen, to borrow a thought from dostoevsky: you can best judge a society by how by how it treats its most vulnerable members, and those who take care of them. if we learn one lesson from the covid—19 pandemic, let it be the importance of solidarity. solidarity between generations must be one of the driving forces of europe’s recovery, as set out in the commission’s proposal for a major recovery plan to repair and prepare for the next generation. only together can we successfully exit the crisis and build a future. as i said at the beginning, we can pay tribute to the service providers, but we must also take action. we need to deliver for the service providers and users in our policymaking. in this i count on your support.
i am glad that the commissioner has taken this opportunity to speak on behalf of the ppe group. at the beginning of the coronavirus pandemic, no one could have predicted the terrible tsunami that has claimed so many lives. our elders in particular were in the eye of the storm. the difficult choices in some hard-hit areas about who could be hospitalized and who could not often resulted in an age-based decision. choices that we as a society have not had to make since world war ii. and let it be that generation that has worked to build our welfare state, that generation is the one that has mr. chairman, the ongoing pandemic has shown us the enormous vulnerability of the long-term care system for the elderly and the disabled. we have seen a number of clients succumb to the coronavirus in these facilities because they were not provided with access to protective equipment, safe isolation or testing. any failures, including insufficient support of the installations, should therefore be duly and promptly investigated. the pandemic has also shown us that the repeated calls for deinstitutionalization of long-term care facilities are more important than ever. smaller family-type facilities would not only improve the clients' chance of a full life, but would also be demonstrably more resilient to epidemiological crises. in the near future, therefore, we need to focus our efforts, but also sufficient resources, to achieve the goal of moving from institutional to community-based care. last but not least, unfortunately, the pandemic has set us up. we are witnessing a loss of respect for the elderly or acceptance of the disabled. the measure of a society is how it treats its most vulnerable. but the pandemic has shown us that our society has a long way to go to live up to the values we profess.

i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission is not prepared to accept this. commissioner, in recent weeks, the documentary 'the social dilemma' has consistently been in the top ten most watched films on netflix. the message of the film or documentary is both frightening and very moving. social media platforms are no longer just a simple communication tool, but are huge profit machines that have a major impact on public discourse. they do this by not only deciding what information we get to see, but also deciding what content is allowed to be shown and what content is not - often in contravention of the law. i do not want the freedom of expression of european users to be in the hands of private companies, who act as sheriff's deputies and make decisions that only the courts can make. if we say that the law should apply online as well as offline, then we must stop interfering with freedom of expression through overblocking and arbitrary deletion by platforms. the juri report therefore proposes to establish clear procedural rules for the notice and action system in dealing with illegal content. this is important because it creates legal certainty for the platforms, but also legal certainty for users and their fundamental rights are respected. the report also calls for a strong european supervisory authority, able to ensure compliance with the rules and, above all, with the necessary enforcement powers to ensure the necessary transparency. an online platform cannot be a black box. its actions must be transparent, reasoned and understandable, especially when it makes such important decisions on the freedom of expression of users. and that brings me to a point that i'm particularly proud of in our report, which is that we proposed that we install a system that does not require prior review of uploaded content. in fact, we specifically instruct the commission that the digital services act cannot require platforms to use upload filters. the voluntary use of technical means by platforms should also not lead to content being scanned and blocked at the time of upload. and i think this is a huge success after the debate last year and a step towards an internet without any more upload filters. there is also content that is legal but can still cause great social harm. the same rules cannot apply to content such as fake news, disinformation, hate speech and propaganda as to how to deal with illegal content, as such content is often below the taste limit, but is protected by the freedom of expression. and so the problem we have with this kind of content is not so much that it exists at all, but that it's spreading like wildfire across the internet and being preferably flushed into users' timelines by platforms because algorithms believe that attention-grabbing content is more important than, say, democratic debate. thus, platforms can promise their actual customers - not us as users, but the advertisers - a particularly effective delivery of targeted, personalised advertising. but when algorithms exploit human curiosity to capture attention for profit, and fake news, hate and incitement are the gift wrapping paper for personalized online advertising, then we need to step in as legislators and take action against this business model. that's why we're calling in the report for users to have more control over their content. they have to decide what content they're going to see, and they have to decide if they want to see any targeted online advertising at all. we cannot allow user algorithms to be at the mercy of eroding our shared social information base and endangering our democracy. if this is the price that silicon valley companies must pay to make more profit from personalised advertising, then that price is clearly too high, and that business model has no place in our digital single market. that is why i am calling for the vote tomorrow to be taken on the legal affairs committee's text, despite the split vote on advertising. the split vote on paragraph 17 is intended to undermine protection against these harmful commercial practices and it is clear to us that this cannot happen, that the commercial interests of the advertising industry must not take precedence over the interests of the citizens. we are not yet at the forefront of the development of ai in the european union, ladies and gentlemen, but with the discussion we are having here today, we are a big step ahead of both china and silicon valley. in europe, artificial intelligence and digital services have to follow our principles, our values and our fundamental rights, and not the other way around.
rapporteur. – madam president, online services are everywhere, from the way we inform ourselves to the way we keep in touch with friends; from the way we buy books and clothes to the way we travel; from the way we eat to the way we make a date. some people born in a different time, like myself, might want to go back to how it was before, because it seems like a simpler time. but we can’t. we simply cannot put the genie back into the bottle. the online world with digital services, with artificial intelligence – it’s all here to stay. the future will be more digital, not less, and it brings incredible progress that we, as policymakers, should embrace. but... it also brings dangers that we cannot ignore anymore. let us be honest with each other, colleagues. the eu has done more to regulate the online world than any other continent. yet, even here, it is still the wild west! over the past decades, we have not followed up on illegal content and illegal activities online with the same rigour as we have illegal activities offline. we have not achieved any meaningful transparency over how algorithms push content to users on a massive scale. we have not grasped the impact of it on our democracies, our culture and our fundamental rights. instead, we have focused on voluntary cooperation, which is good, but not good enough. not nearly good enough. so as this european parliament discusses these six reports on the digital services act (dsa) and artificial intelligence (ai), i have one overarching message: these digital files matter a great deal more than many people think. we cannot leave this to the private sector without oversight and enforcement. we must keep this in the public debate as a priority and we must regulate, no matter how technical these files are. the difficult challenge before us is to find the balance between digital progress and respect for fundamental rights. as rapporteur for the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe) dsa report, i’m raising a few issues right now. the value of lawmaking is the real change it effects. if we start off with conflicting regulations and directives that undermine each other instead of complementing each other, we have lost the battle before it starts. we must respect the scope of each digital file. secondly, the fundamentals of the e-commerce directive were great, but its greatest issue was transparency. we must improve and enforce meaningful transparency, and i call for a clear mandate from this parliament. thirdly, we all agree on the importance of notice and action mechanisms to remove illegal content online, but it places the burden almost entirely on users. we therefore should complement notice and action with clear and appropriate obligations to remove illegal content, and it’s the public authorities who should provide the funding to follow up on this. finally, i would like of course personally to thank all the shadow rapporteurs and all my colleagues on the libe dsa report for their excellent cooperation.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. – madam president, we are in the midst of a global tech race. technology should empower us all, but we see everyday how it can be deployed for malign purposes, from deep-fake videos undermining democracy to the challenge of fully autonomous weapon systems. the eu is a regulatory power, but we must also be a tech superpower, not only for regulation but also for promoting technology and supporting it. that means developing metadata skills and partnerships with like-minded allies. the only way to ensure ethical development of ai is for the world’s democracies to win the global tech race and set the global standards. if china comes first, our ethical frameworks will be nothing more than well-intentioned empty words, and that cannot be.

mr president, whatever one may say, agriculture is, has been and will remain a risky business. to counteract this risk, she does not need to be cuddled, caressed or taken care of. to meet the many economic, health and environmental demands, agriculture will need more predictability, more capacity for innovation, political coherence and a level playing field both within and outside the european union. i said, "a level playing field". it is doubtful whether we can put an end to cultural practices which are more environmentally friendly, even if they are very praiseworthy, if they are maintained in third countries which maintain them and export their products to us. i'm told the boarder adjustment mechanism is there to put an end to this injustice. i'll wait and see, it's far from certain. in conclusion, i would ask for the farmer: that he be a little less exposed to what is, what must be; a little less sectarianism; a little more respect; a little more objectivity; and ultimately a little more feasibility. i know because i've been there.
mr. chairman, ladies and gentlemen, i want to extend greetings from the czech republic. let me start by thanking the farmers who feed the 450 million people in the european union, and they are not having an easy time of it because they are affected by climate change and now by the impact of the covid-19 pandemic. let us bear this in mind when we decide to reform the common agricultural policy. the commission's proposal is to set up a european network of experts to help the member states to develop their own national and european policies. i want, like most citizens in the european union, to support only active farmers with european funds. we do not want to continue to support the non-transparent agro-complexes that expand their businesses at the expense of family farms and small and medium-sized enterprises. i consider this a great danger for the countryside, for the protection of the countryside, but also for european agriculture as a whole. i have therefore proposed a number of amendments with my colleagues. we want to simplify the drawings for small farmers to eur 5 000, we want to limit direct payments for drawings to a maximum of eur 1 million per entity. we also propose that member states be able to bring their farmers' subsidies up to european standards. ladies and gentlemen, i would like to remind you that it is thanks to european subsidies that we can afford a high standard of food quality, but we cannot afford to continue to support the oligarchs, and therefore i ask you to support the amendments which will prevent this.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. it is necessary, ladies and gentlemen, for the survival of agriculture, for a living income for farmers, for attracting young farmers. because make no mistake, in europe a thousand farmers disappear every day. i went to talk to them. and i collected their stories in a book: "peasant pride". if you listen to them, you hear the passion, you feel their craftsmanship. but you also see the problems and challenges they face every day. then you'll realize more than ever that reform is needed. and that reform is also needed to make agricultural policy greener, more in line with the green deal. well, ladies and gentlemen, for some people the proposal is too green, for others it is not green enough. i tell you: i think it is realistic and ambitious and will combine the agricultural policy with the green deal. so be a "boer proud" and vote for this agricultural reform. i would like to present this book to you, commissioner.
mr. chairman, after the epic debates at the height of the pandemic, when the amazon rainforest and jungle were discussed here, today we reach a new high in the political debate. at a time when farmers' money is being discussed, here in this room, colleagues are arguing whether a burger is vegan or vegetarian, and whether it can be a burger if it's vegan or vegetarian. as they say, if it's important to you, gentlemen, argue! but i cannot agree to the compromises proposed by the three largest groups in the european parliament. once again, this chamber proves that when it comes to allocating funds from common taxes, there are no ideological divisions, but always the interests of the old member states are considered against the new ones. the story of the mobility package, alas, is repeating itself. we tried to introduce amendments to align the percentages proposed by the council. it didn't work out. this will lead to what is known as unreliable farmer incomes and a decline in competitiveness. in fact, we all know what the battle is here: more money for the uncompetitive farmers of the old countries. there is no way to have an effective common economic policy.
mr president, the current cap reform is the most ambitious reform to date, as it introduces both a new performance-based delivery model and higher climate targets. for the first time, eco-schemes are also being introduced and direct payments are being made conditional on compliance with certain environmental requirements. but along with environmental sustainability, we must not forget economic and social sustainability. unfortunately, we will not achieve full external convergence of direct payments in the new programming period either. and without convergence of payments, farmers in the new member states will continue to be at a disadvantage compared to their counterparts in other countries. instruments such as transitional national aid and tied aid partially compensate for lower support levels in the member states and have an important social impact in rural areas. i therefore urge you in the upcoming vote to support transitional national aid as an instrument that provides financial assistance with national funds for vulnerable sectors and farmers. as regards tied support, i regret that the compromise amendment under article 86 did not provide for greater financial flexibility to allow member states to allocate a higher percentage of the first pillar funds to tied support. in this context, and given the enormous importance of this aid to sensitive sectors in some member states, i would appeal for the support of the 13%+2% tied aid.
mr president, i should first like to thank very warmly all the farmers who feed the european population every day. we've seen through the covid crisis how important their role is every day. this nurturing function must of course remain at the very heart of the common agricultural policy. this does not prevent the achievement of new performance and objectives in the areas of climate change and biodiversity. this is already the case: if we look at europe and compare it with the rest of the world, the agricultural world has made efforts overall, which will certainly be more important in the future. but we must do so with a certain degree of trust in each other, without bureaucracy or administrative work and without additional burdens for all farmers, and i am thinking particularly of small producers. in the guidelines we must also, of course, support the smallest farms. to do this, there must be redistributive aid to support more the first hectares, to support more small family farms at european level. finally, i would like to say that i am of course in favour of all the climate and biodiversity objectives, that is obvious. no other sector has as many standards as the agricultural sector: health standards, animal welfare, biodiversity... all this is already part of the standards and conditionalities, which will be strengthened. but i would really like, once and for all, that whenever free trade agreements are discussed here, they should be given this particular attention, to avoid ruining the european economy, ruining farmers and, in some cases, winning the climate bet by means of these imported products.
chairman of the counting session! through the cap, we are sending a signal about the kind of agriculture we want and how we want to shape the future of the sector. the most important message of the strategic plan we are discussing today is that farmers are expected to become increasingly environmentally conscious. that farmers are increasingly being asked to focus on broader climate goals. but that means more rules and more restrictions on eligibility. however, the tightening of restrictions and rules may lead to a significant loss of competitiveness for many producers and to the impossibility of production. the restrictions must be based on the assumption that we are not depriving farmers of their future prospects, because the preservation of agriculture and rural life is essential for the future of europe. the specificities of the member states should be taken into account more when setting the restrictions. for example, for an estonian farmer producing in a peripheral area of the european union, market access is already associated with significant additional costs. however, our production costs are higher than average and our direct subsidies are significantly lower than average. nevertheless, i intend with a heavy heart to vote in favour of the compromises negotiated under the auspices of my good colleague peter jahr, even if they are painful. but i hope that on a number of important issues, more appropriate solutions will be found in the trilateral negotiations.
mr president, i would like to take the opportunity to congratulate the rapporteurs on reaching the compromise text, especially my epp colleagues herbert, peter and others. this has been one of the toughest negotiations we ever had and to finally get agreement on some of the key elements is extremely welcome. no, this is not a perfect text, but as always, the sign of a good compromise is when nobody is fully satisfied. however, we must focus on the positives of the agreement found. one, this is the greenest cap we have ever had and at least 35% of all rural development funding must be used for environmental and climate measures. 30% of investments must be beneficial for the environment. it is more ambitious than the commission’s proposal and strikes a good balance between ensuring farmers get a fair income, preserving our rural communities and ensuring we secure the production of top quality european food. i additionally welcome the reduction of needless bureaucracy. coming from a rural constituency i know very well how badly we need simplification and the cutting of red tape. i think this compromise will help in this regard. most of all this is a good deal for farmers, the people who have devoted their lives, the people who know best how to protect our rural environment, preserve biodiversity and produce sustainable quality food. i hope we will agree with the compromises to put us in a strong position to go with council for negotiations.
mr. chairman, please! direct payments are one of the pillars on which the common agricultural policy is based. they are what help european farmers to produce high-quality, safe and healthy food. direct payments also offset some of the risks associated with agricultural production, which, unlike any other sector, is subject to additional risks linked to weather or climate change. such measures are also important for consumers, as they guarantee continuity of supply and affordable prices. unfortunately, although the challenges faced by european farmers and the costs associated with agricultural production are similar across the european union, direct payments are still different, creating unequal competition in the single market. of course, thanks to the mechanism of external convergence or transfers between pillars, these differences are reduced. and it's good that those records are also in the current proposal. however, direct payments between member states have not been equalised. i would therefore like to call for support for the amendment that the process of convergence of direct payments should be completed as soon as possible and at the latest by the end of the next funding period. i am firmly convinced that only by treating all european farmers equally and fairly can we achieve fair competition in european agriculture.
mr president, we can spend months debating in this house figures and eco-schemes, financial allocations, the green deal, capping, climate measures and many other issues directly related to the cap. but i ask you: have we stopped to think seriously about whether this cap is the one that best serves our farmers? is that what they need? is it the cap you're waiting for? is this the reform that protects the hard work of those who, despite so many difficulties, continue to cultivate the land? the one that gives answers to farmers who don't know how to cope with water shortages? is this the cap that meets the expectations of farmers and livestock farmers in the most disadvantaged areas, in the outermost regions, such as the canary islands, who are extremely concerned about the fact that the posei index is being reduced to zero? is the cap promoting agricultural employment and generational renewal? i honestly don't think so. i think it's not the best possible cap. because it is not the cap they need, nor is it the cap they expect, nor is it the one that suits them, nor the one that protects them. and, more importantly, not the one they deserve. it is a political cap and a very distorted one. and we cannot reform the cap by looking at other issues and forgetting about farmers. because they're the ones who can be left without a future. and we cannot and must not leave them without hope, not from this seat of mine at least.

president, commissioner, if a cleaning lady is to be quarantined, she will lose her job or part of her salary. i know it's hard to imagine. because if you have to quarantine, you'll just keep your 20,000 euros salary. but this virus is a real class virus, and the working class is hit much harder than the millionaire class. the working class cannot retreat to an estate. he has to go to work, he has to take public transportation, and he risks salary and job. while the shareholders continue to receive dividends, the ordinary employees lose their jobs. that is reality! and what kind of programme is the european union coming up with? especially with hundreds of billions for the big corporation. for that, the big company doesn't even have to pay fair taxes or stop paying dividends. you don't have to do all that. even securing jobs is not a condition. you can't be! free ivs while the common man loses his job! no, that's totally unacceptable to us. i would like to suggest that you reverse that logic. instead of giving taxpayers massive amounts of money to big business for free, let's do the opposite. let's get the very richest, the millionaires, to contribute and then let's invest that money in public services and our public sector. it's good for all of us. i think that's a much better deal for our entire society, both socially and ecologically.

madam president, i think it is undeniable that the recovery fund has been and remains a great necessity in order to support the economies of the member states and to be able to cope with this unprecedented and unprecedented pandemic which is sweeping everything in its path. but it's also common knowledge that the recovery fund was decided with different data and under different circumstances, when the pandemic was in its infancy and no one could have calculated and estimated the enormous socioeconomic impact and the depth that it would have. i therefore believe that today, given the present situation, we need to review our objectives and targets. on the one hand, the recovery fund can become the fund for the economic recovery of the economies of the countries, and on the other hand, there is a need today, here and now, for a new strong fund of this kind, which can be called the 'social recovery fund'. this fund will be able to directly channel money into the market for our unemployed, since there is a risk that the unemployment funds in all countries will collapse, for small and mediumsized enterprises, farmers, vulnerable groups, pensioners, the socioeconomic whole. and this is especially important because the economic recovery fund (1.4 trillion euros) will take too long to bring money to the market. i come from a country, cyprus, which is heavily dependent on tourism, like other countries in the south. if these countries do not receive direct support for the tourism sector, i can assure you that in the coming years we may have tourism but we will not have tourism businesses because they will be driven into bankruptcy. the social fund is therefore essential to prevent the bankruptcy and bankruptcy not only of companies but also of the economies of our countries.

taken in these weeks in our member states could further challenge the recovery. so this is my first point. the crisis is not behind us because this crisis is not like a war that finishes at a certain sentiment, but also from a common interest: the common interest to preserve the single market, because all we are seeing now shows that our concerns about the fact that this crisis could have uneven consequences among different sectors and member states have been confirmed. so we are seeing that distances are there. again, this common interest is not behind us. this is one of the reasons why we should continue to support the recovery with our fiscal policies in the next period. i think that several interventions mentioned the need to make the best possible use of the recovery and resilience facility. what the commission is committed to is finding the right balance because this is not a memorandum written in brussels that we have to impose on member states. these will be proposals coming from member states and the commission will work to make sure that these proposals are coherent with our common priorities and with countless specific recommendations, and not only linked to tactical or short-term goals. but i repeat that the starting point will be national decisions, national governments and national parliaments. several honourable members rightly say that we need stronger coordination, but we all know — and you know better than me — that it’s always necessary to find the right balance between the demand for stronger coordination and the demand to preserve a national role. we are not a federal state. this could be a good thing or a bad thing, according to political and cultural points of view, but the fact is that we are not. so we have some sectors and some areas where the coordination is easier and others where it is more difficult. i think on this occasion we should go further in coordination, especially on health care safety measures, border coordination overall in this crisis our social model showed its difficulties, but also its strengths. i think we can be proud of our social model if we compare the social consequences of this crisis in other parts of the world where we don’t have social services or even where we have authoritarian models in government. in my view, the european social model confirms its strength, but of course we have to reinforce it. we have to confirm the commitment to make the social pillar crucial in our semester. we have to be aware that the crisis is increasing social inequalities and that we have winners and losers. we have winners from this crisis, not only rich people in general, but also sectors of our global economy. for example, the digital global platforms are winners in this crisis. we will speak about this when we speak about digital taxation, own resources, etc. but, for sure, they are the winners in this crisis and other sectors that were mentioned here — culture, entertainment, travel, tourism, smes, and i am sure that i am forgetting something — are the losers in this crisis. this means that they will need support for a longer time than others. we can’t change and phase out the support in these sectors too soon. finally, i think that there is broad consensus on the fact that our goal, the goal of this unprecedented common response, is not only to revive our economies and go back to where we were at the end of 2019. we are all aware of the difficulties and some of them were mentioned here. climate transition is not easy in many countries, but we are also aware of the tremendous responsibility that we have during this crisis to seize the opportunity to change our societies and our economies for the better.
in writing. the crisis caused by the covid-19 pandemic has huge economic, social and geopolitical implications. i am pleased that the commission has decided to give maximum budgetary flexibility and to provide member states with support on an unprecedented scale. the increased coordination to avoid new bottlenecks in the internal market and in health is another positive development. however, in recent days, the situation in europe has deteriorated again. further tightening of social and economic life is being introduced. therefore, rapid action is necessary to prevent further effects of the pandemic. i therefore call on the representatives of the commission, the parliament and the council to reach agreement on the eu's multiannual budget as soon as possible and to follow the principles of cooperation, solidarity and flexibility. support for smes is crucial to prevent mass redundancies and rising unemployment, as any fluctuations in this area affect the entire internal market. by finalising the budget negotiations, we will be able to support our european businesses before it is too late.
in writing. many member states are facing a second wave of covid-19. at the moment, 60 million europeans are at risk of losing their jobs. so far europe has avoided the mass unemployment that has struck elsewhere. this is largely due to schemes to reduce working hours (such as the sure instrument) used by some 45 million people, which are intended to help people pay rent or meet basic needs. but we also have to think about the future of our jobs. the health crisis has made it clear that we need to do more to promote quality jobs and decent working conditions, in line with the sdgs and the european pillar of social rights. our employees deserve a fair wage and a safe and healthy work environment. this requires a strong involvement of the social partners. i therefore ask the commission and the member states to maintain the current measures to protect employment until a real recovery has started, to launch a proposal for a permanent european unemployment reinsurance scheme and a european framework for fair minimum wages. as the epp's shadow rapporteur, i am also in favour of a substantial increase in the budget for the european globalisation adjustment fund.
mr president, the european commission has adopted an ambitious green deal for europe. the european parliament voted, just 15 days ago, the climate law which provides for a 60% reduction in our emissions by 2030. and yet we are stuck in texts that date back to the 1990s that do not incorporate the climate emergency. we must stop procrastinating and get out of the energy charter treaty, which provides for the protection of investments in fossil fuels and the mechanism for settling disputes between investors and states, which also provides for a supremacy clause, so that this text is superior to any later text, such as the paris agreement, and which also provides for unanimity in decisions between 50 members. just over 50 members who cannot agree on sustainability issues. this is particularly problematic with a country like japan, with whom we have concluded a trade agreement and negotiated sustainable development chapters, which should allow us to have common positions on the international stage. so, commissioner, after two rounds of negotiations with no end in sight and given the hostility to the modernisation of this treaty, what is the union's plan b to get out of it and to protect itself from the effects of the treaty?
the fact that we reached agreement yesterday on the multiannual budget and the recovery plan is not only good news in these difficult times, but is indeed historic. the agreement should enable us to rebuild europe after the covid19 crisis and make the eu even better to better secure the future of our citizens and businesses. in short, the agreement means more money for research and development, more money for european health challenges and better protection of the rule of law. the budget for research and development will be increased substantially by eur 4 billion. this is good for europe and for the member states, including belgium and in particular the belgian province of flanders. the funds are allocated according to the principle of excellence and flemish research institutions and companies generally participate with great success in these calls for projects: some 2,6% of european r & d funds are channelled to flanders. the european budget for health challenges will also be tripled. this will be used for the provision of medical supplies and for cross-border teams of health workers. the current crisis has shown that european solidarity can indeed save lives. however, the principle 'for what, belongs to what' applies: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. this is how we protect the democratic values of europe. my compliments to everyone who made this agreement possible.
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, i would like to welcome the political agreement reached on the multiannual financial framework and the recovery plan, as well as the exceptional work of our negotiating team. the european parliament has managed to move the council's lines: this is a first, this is a real victory and we must welcome it. by voting on our 2021 budget proposal, we are responding concretely to the concerns of european citizens who face every day an unprecedented, long-term health and economic crisis, with the economy contracting by more than 7% this year. we cannot disappoint them with european programmes that have been scrapped when they have not yet recovered from the first wave of this crisis and are already facing the second. when member states take decisions on major health policy decisions, the transfer of these competences must go hand in hand with adequate financial and human resources. that is why the european parliament has been advocating for months for an ambitious budget. we are calling for a substantial additional allocation for the 2021 budget to finance the european union's flagship programmes: erasmus+, eu4health, horizon europe and investeu. we want to be able to make citizens understand that european solidarity is not just slogans, that european values are not empty words, that only real solidarity from 2021 onwards will ensure the financing of structural reforms and the effectiveness of the union's policies. our budget, which corresponds to the first year of the multiannual financial framework, must be able to support a four-year sustainable and resilient recovery facility. the council has chosen an arithmetical approach to the amounts proposed by the commission for 2021 for several programmes, based on the position of the european council and without any real political priorities. the cuts in the union's flagship programmes, which are clearly oriented towards the future, such as the climate and digital transition, health, research and youth and culture, are already undermining the very foundations of this recovery. in these particularly difficult times for entrepreneurship, youth, the hospital sector and civil society, europe offers a way out of the crisis, through collective and solidarity projects. only a common political will has enabled us to reach an ambitious, fair and balanced political agreement on the next multiannual financial framework. the principle of fair return is outdated. the conciliation which will open on 17 november is crucial and it is equally crucial to make the most of it: 21 days of conciliation in a constructive and open spirit so that, in terms of frank and honest exchanges, we can meet the legitimate expectations of european citizens as from 2021. we must learn from this major crisis and build the foundations of european sovereignty in health, digital, industrial and even security, so as to be able to deal with current and future challenges by generating european decisions that bring sustainable investment and human cooperation to the service of all european citizens. ladies and gentlemen, the 2021 budget is also the budget of our institution, the european parliament, and indeed of the other institutions. it is clear that we cannot build policies for the future without institutions that are operational, efficient, modern, environmentally friendly and interactive and therefore capable of functioning even in the event of force majeure. that is why, with my colleagues from the five main political groups, we decided to restore the council's cuts, at the request of the institutions, when they were justified, because here too the council made proposals for horizontal and systematic cuts, without taking account of the specificities of the institutions. such an approach would have undermined their functioning and, in the same spirit, would have diminished the vital contribution they make to the proper functioning of the union. and based on that same approach, we restored the headcount table. finally, with regard to the commission's proposed cuts, we considered in a limited number of cases that it was necessary to restore the estimates of these institutions. and i will conclude, madam president, by recalling that a euro spent at the level of the european budget saves far more than a euro in our various capitals, on the basis of the principle of budgetary subsidiarity. member states must not regard payments to the european union as expenditure but rather as investments in a globalised world, where europe must be able to deal on an equal footing with china, russia and the united states, thanks to a european autonomy which is essential to defend the interests of our citizens and future generations.

in writing. environmental and budgetary issues dominate much of the agenda of our plenary session. and we need votes that are up to the challenge. that is why, in the context of the vote on the report on 'the investment plan for a sustainable europe - financing the european green deal' launched by the president of the european commission, ursula von der leyen, i will support the following provisions which i believe have become imperatives today: first, that investments made under the european recovery plan respect the principle 'first, do no harm' (to ensure that the eu meets its commitments under the paris agreement) and, second, that the union's multiannual financial framework/budget does not support or fund activities that could harm climate and environmental objectives. i also support the commission's assessment and identification of activities which significantly impair environmental sustainability.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the most efficient use of products and services is the way to ensure sustainable development. efficient resource management and a commitment to the circular economy will help bring us closer to the goal of carbon neutrality. it is therefore imperative that a comprehensive and coherent strategy be developed, one based on scientific research, which increases the durability, reuse and repair of products. this is also an approach which will help to protect the interests of consumers. however, it is important that it does not result in undesirable consequences such as increased bureaucratic burden and loss of jobs, growth and competitiveness of companies. we must make this transition with the companies and never on their side. i would also like to draw attention to the need to maintain the high standards in public procurement, particularly in the criteria of sustainability and the durability of products. i ask the european commission for the courage, the political ambition, to implement these measures for the good of our economy, our consumers and society.
mr president, this report is committed to promoting a more sustainable single market for producers and consumers by applying the principles of the green deal in a pragmatic, consensual, realistic but firm and decisive way. we are thus laying the foundations for healthy and responsible economic and trade relations which are the first condition for the transition to a circular economy. this proposal therefore sends a clear, firm and consistent message to the commission, the council, the member states, industry, services and consumers. the transition from linear to circular starts with the design of products themselves, with the possibility they offer to be reused and to last over time. it continues the guarantees we offer users to access accessible, quality and non-monopoly spare parts and repair processes, extends that philosophy to upgrades in the digital realm and ends premature or planned obsolescence. this commitment requires commercial transparency and implies traceability. both qualities should enable us to see the origin and the expected durability of a product, from the origin of the materials from which it is made to the possible destination of the same at the end of its useful life. and on that story must be based product assurance, sound and reliable trade relations and sustainable development. at the beginning of the 20th century, when the world seemed to have no limits, in many cities of my country you could find shops called "the three beans": good, nice and cheap. this report is committed to the three r's: resistant, repairable and reusable. encouraging this form of doing things to be binding does not harm any manufacturer who bases his success on honesty, innovation and quality.
mr president, the pandemic we are all facing together has forced us to think about the future shape of our european single market. we must always have in mind first and foremost the interests of citizens as consumers and also the interests of small businesses, which account for up to two thirds of jobs. i would like to highlight three areas of this report that i consider important: digitalisation, public procurement and simplification. the first area: digitization. in digital technologies, which also offer new possibilities for transparency, i welcome the initiative of the so-called 'digital product passport' to improve traceability and access to information on the conditions of production, shelf life, composition, reuse or repair of a product. i would stress, however, that this development must take account of the needs of small firms, which are the largest employers. in the second area, public procurement, which accounts for up to 16% of european gdp, we should send a clear signal for reliable sustainability criteria. i believe that the most important thing is to shorten the supply chain and to take account of the needs of small firms, which are often discriminated against in public procurement today and therefore need to be better positioned. as regards simplification, i would like to stress the need for it, and i regard the common connector for electronic mobile devices as a typical example. it is important that the european commission implements our initiative and the european parliament's decision on the common terminal of connectors. this will not only contribute to the simplification of the equipment, but also to better recyclability and to meeting environmental objectives. but we must always strike a balance between consumer protection and the sustainability of the manufacturers' capabilities. therefore, on the other hand, i would like to draw attention to the following: there is no need to tighten the requirements in the area of spare parts. i believe that forcing companies to produce and store large quantities of spare parts for which there is not necessarily demand would not only harm the environment but also the competitiveness of our companies in the union. the second area i want to highlight is the service sector. it has a very important role to play in sustainability. we need to complete the internal market in services as soon as possible, as this can make a decisive contribution to a sustainable internal market from which consumers can benefit. last but not least, i want to emphasize the area of standards. for digital technologies in particular, we need to put in place standards and protocols to improve interoperability and data sharing between firms, investors and institutions, which will undoubtedly bring further development opportunities. in this area too, we need to invest much more in research into new technologies within our budget. i believe that, while maintaining these principles, our common market will continue to be a driver of prosperity for our citizens.
