the president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are experiencing a great many changes which are having an effect on our consumption patterns. the covid-19 crisis, digitalisation and the green transition are some examples. consumers want tailored rules that meet their needs. they need to be empowered to contribute to the green transition. this means that consumers need to be better informed and that there is a right to redress that facilitates the transition to a circular economy. artificial ageing must also be tackled. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet its own needs. it is important that the new rules provide an incentive for consumers to make sustainable choices. citizens also want to enjoy the same high level of protection online. consumers are increasingly buying products and services online. in this context, we see an increase in illegal activities such as online fraud, the dissemination of false information and certain forms of profiling. these practices affect the freedom of choice of consumers and have no place in europe. in addition, there are specific groups of consumers with specific needs. children, people with limited digital literacy, families in difficult financial situations are some examples of this. they ask for tailor-made protection. the covid-19 crisis also exposed some gaps in european consumer law. let us learn from this and take this lesson into the future. finally, we must not forget to ensure a level playing field in the implementation of measures. the new consumer agenda is ambitious but necessary. consumer choice plays a crucial role in the post-covid-19 recovery, the green and digital transformation. we must therefore build a high-quality consumer protection system for the future, and i am sure that you will succeed in doing so, commissioner.
member of the commission. – madam president, i should like to thank ms walsmann very much. i’m delighted you mentioned christmas, because a lot of us have fears around christmas, but it’s nice to look on it as an opportunity, and of course when we’re buying gifts to think of safety. so thank you for your good work on this. it’s both important and timely. buying a safe product is the utmost important thing – it protects consumers and also the functioning of the single market, and all products sold on the market should be safe, but you have raised a number of very important issues when it comes to non-food products. most of these issues correspond with the main objectives of the revision of the general product safety directive, which is planned for the second quarter of next year. and this includes the safety of consumer products, linked, as you said, to new technologies, like connected products or products powered by artificial intelligence and the need to address the growth in online sales. these issues need to be tackled from different angles and we’ll carry out the revision of the general product safety directive in conjunction with other initiatives such as the digital services act, the revision of the machinery directive and initiatives on artificial intelligence. the commission is also working on delegated acts under the radio equipment directive. in your report, you underline the importance of the recently adopted regulation on market surveillance and compliance. this new regulation is a major step forward for the 70 pieces of harmonised product legislation, many of them on product safety, such as the low voltage directive and the toy safety directive. it will provide better protection for consumers, it will greatly help national inspectors, and the implementation of this regulation is ongoing. the commission will also support the eu product compliance network, providing funding from the single market programme, and we thank parliament for emphasising the importance of market surveillance in this programme. this report also calls on the commission to encourage more online marketplaces to join the product safety pledge. just two weeks ago, two new online marketplaces joined the pledge, which brings the total number of signatories to nine major online actors. the report also raises issues linked to ineffective recalls of dangerous products from consumers, and i think this is an area of deep concern. most consumers are not sometimes even aware that they own a recalled product and many others continue to use dangerous products despite seeing a recall notice. i think that is deeply concerning but important to stress. we are currently working on developing guidance on product recalls and we will also look at this issue during the revision of the general product safety directive. so thank you for this opportunity this evening, for your work and i look forward to tomorrow’s vote.
the case is closed. the amendments will be voted on tomorrow, 24 november 2020, and the final vote will be delivered on wednesday, 25 november 2020. the same applies to the previous ferber report on financial instruments markets. written declarations (article 171) the president of the court of justice shall be entitled to give a reasoned opinion.
in writing. i am very pleased about this debate today. the development of the european system on product safety is a demonstration of the essential role played by the european union: to protect our citizens, our consumers, from the dangers of the modern world. at the same time, the set of rules established by the union is also a demonstration of our capacity to exert a beneficial influence: products meeting the standards of the european union are also found on the markets of third countries. however, the stakes remain high. these may be new developments such as the development of new chemicals, the increasing importance of digital technology, software and artificial intelligence. they can be related to crime as the danger of counterfeit products not meeting standards. or they may be linked to the increasing division and diversification of supply chains. finally, it is essential to strengthen controls at the union's borders on imported products, especially from china, which is still the country of origin of more than half of the products reported in the european rapex system.
mr president, this report is a breath of centralism. this report is contrary to subsidiarity, it is contrary to unity in diversity. transnational lists as well. they will also damage public confidence: you don't know these people, you can't follow them for five years, and you don't speak the language of these people. and the election of the president of the commission on these transnational lists paves the way for a presidential regime. this is contrary to the foundations of the partnership which is the european union. 'unity in diversity' is the motto. centralism and gigantism are the opposite. you do not have to be a centralist to believe in the added value of the european union, based on the values of the enlightenment.
for the greens/efa group mr president, you know that we greens are intransigent when it comes to defining the challenges of european trade policy, and that is because, along with agricultural policy, trade policy is probably the area where the frictions with the european green deal are the most important and where the prevailing mindset is still that of the 20th century, when the objectives of sustainable development and the paris climate agreement did not exist. in recent years, the leitmotif seems to have been 'everything but china' and this has made us lose sight of the big societal issues. it is therefore time for a decisive change in european trade policy. this is what the european parliament is asking for: that trade policy contribute to the green and just transition. we therefore support this text, which can be improved but is the result of a compromise, and i thank my colleagues for the openness they have shown in putting the european parliament on the path to ambition and in giving the european commission the necessary input in defining the union's trade strategy for the next five years. what are we asking for? first, to ensure that all free trade agreements are compatible with the green deal, including by revising existing agreements already negotiated and in progress. second, to ensure that the sustainable development provisions are binding and that non-compliance by a partner is subject to sanctions. then, adopt a binding legislative framework on business due diligence throughout the supply chain for eu and foreign companies that are active within the single market. therefore, prohibit the market access of products resulting from forced labour and child labour. this would be a strong signal from the european union in the context of the international year for the elimination of child labour in 2021. this is also a recommendation echoed in the news revealed by the guardian, which shows that china, in addition to the uyghurs, uses north korean forced laborers to produce personal protective equipment for european countries in particular. finally, mr commissioner, involving civil society in the implementation of free trade agreements is essential to ensure compliance with these provisions. this is the role of the domestic advisory groups, whose recommendations often seem to be ignored by the european commission. it would therefore be an honour for the european parliament to hear at least once a year the members of these groups, whose report would undoubtedly help to ensure that our free trade agreements are in line with the green deal and contribute to a fairer, more equitable and more respectful of the planet globalisation.

madam president, thomas jefferson said: where the press is free and every man able to read, all is safe. but today, in too many countries, including in european countries, the press is not free and journalists therefore are not always safe when they express what they think. hate speech online, intimidation, the spread of disinformation, arbitrary detention and sexual violence towards female journalists all create a shrinking space for free speech. in the past 15 years, close to 200 hundred journalists have been killed. in nine out of ten of these cases, there is no conviction. impunity emboldens perpetrators. it also points to a bigger problem within our societies and justice systems. a free press speaks truth to power. it criticises, satirises and holds us to account. it sheds light where others have sought to shore up corruption, wrongdoing and mistruth in darkness. a free press is one of the great pillars that hold us up and our democracy safe, and by protecting journalists we help protect the path to truth and freedom.

in writing. the most relevant debates were the response to the covid-19 pandemic, climate change, the fight against terrorism and violent extremism, the situation in the eastern mediterranean and relations with turkey, the eu's relations with the countries of the southern neighbourhood, the eu's trade policy. the main issues for the socialists are brexit, the negotiation of the 2021-2027 multiannual financial framework (mfp) and own resources. the spanish delegation supports parliament's position on the need for a legislative instrument linking the budget to the defence of the rule of law and therefore calls on the council to confirm the agreement reached without changing it. the negotiations on the european recovery fund are of paramount importance to us and we hope to reach an agreement as soon as possible. we also expect an agreement on our future relationship with the united kingdom, and we cannot give up a reliable and stable system of governance, competition rules that protect our internal market and an agreement that safeguards the interests of our fishing industry. and to manage the covid situation we need to move forward in creating a european health union and ensure a transparent plan for vaccine procurement and distribution.
in writing. in april, commission president ursula von der leyen solemnly promised that a vaccine against covid-19 would be a universal, collective good for us. that was important, because everyone, everywhere in the world, deserves protection. so if there's going to be an effective and safe vaccine, we're going to need massive amounts of it. that means that we cannot accept that one company gets a monopoly on it. because no company can produce enough to feed everyone. but since then, you've been backing down more and more. in june, you forgot to mention the promise in your vaccine strategy. then you narrowed your engagement to international initiatives that wanted to share health technologies globally. you then replied that your promise of april was not legally binding and that you had bought up all the vaccine doses very quickly. solidarity pledges were a joke, in other words. today you're going one step further. now, when 100 countries, including india and south africa, want to suspend patents to make the vaccine widely available, including in poorer countries, you actively oppose it. how dare you? this is about people's lives. support the initiative of india and south africa. make the vaccine really good for a global audience.
on behalf of the id group. mr president, commissioner, you want to build a pharmaceutical strategy for europe, which aims to guarantee the quality and safety of medicines. but it is precisely the same reason that led to the creation, 16 years ago, of the european medicines agency, which already sets out community procedures for the authorisation, supervision and pharmacovigilance of medicines for human and veterinary use. you add that you want to improve and speed up access to medicines for patients at affordable prices and in a safe manner. but how are you going to get the support of mps, almost all of whose groups rejected a report on access to medicines that i brought in 2016 and that was written in exactly the same terms? you also say that we must support innovation in the pharmaceutical industry, that is obvious, but first we must restore the rules of fair competition which no longer encourage our large groups to outsource 80% of the active ingredients of generics to china or india. the covid crisis was only the reveal of an untenable and long-standing situation that has had the effect of multiplying the shortage of medicines in france by 20 in less than 10 years. and that's a direct result of your globalist policies. to control stocks, you are calling for even more europe, through europe of health, by expanding the powers of the european medicines agency, in particular through new technologies, and by creating an interconnected health system. but who can guarantee that this data will not be immediately retrieved by insurance companies or by foreign providers of digital services? you also address the fight against cancer. although a special committee has just been set up, we have been waiting since a resolution in 2016 for the paediatric regulation to be revised in terms of the fight against childhood cancer. you are right to want to accelerate research and innovation through the horizon europe, artificial intelligence and digitisation. but how can you ensure that the results and publications are up to date, when one year after the launch of covid research funding, we still have no actionable feedback to reorient states in their health strategy? so once again, in the face of an a priori acceptable project and the challenges ahead, you are overstepping your bounds and, in the case of what is only one therapeutic option among others, you are setting up a genuine tool for shaping the european health care sector, which will take away all autonomy from the member states in their regulatory management. that is why, instead of building this fragile system, we propose cooperation between states that makes it possible to use the tools that already exist. the aim is to enable the relocation of generic production to europe and france, to ensure that all stages of production are carried out locally, in sufficient quantities and production, to ensure good manufacturing practices, to ensure transparency of stocks, to ensure tracing and labelling of boxes at all stages, to finally put an end to the policy of quotas and to limit, as far as possible, parallel trade, to make regulations more flexible, to allow a policy of prices and costs which stabilises margins, and thus markets, and ultimately to save our national systems.
for the group gue/ngl. mr president, although i do not do this very often, let me first commend the commission. personally, i welcome the fact that one of the four main objectives is to ensure that patients have access to affordable medicines and to address the gaps in treatment needs for children and patients with rare diseases. we need this revision as much as we can, and i have been calling for it in the european parliament for six years. i therefore strongly hope that the review will include the joint procurement of medicines for rare diseases at the level of the european union as a whole, because in recent months it has been shown that joint procurement is possible when the commission wants it to be possible, and i see no reason why this should not be the case for medicines for rare diseases. however, i would expect the new strategy to include a reference to patent law at the level of the world health organization, because as we've found, a lot of the current rules of global trade are simply not helping us solve any major health crisis. i would therefore like to call again on both the commission and the world health organisation to be as active as possible in tackling this problem in the fair pricing forum. if we don't make a significant change in this regulation, very little will improve access to medicines and their prices, and the pharmaceutical companies will continue to make money and the public will be forced to pay. colleagues, i am so far positive about the proposal, but i see a lot of white space. however, we have a lot of work to do, and i look forward to it.

the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. the present resolutions highlight some problems in the protection of this diversity, including the definition of 'national minority'. the experience of my own country, belgium, which is home to two peoples, shows that the concept of 'national minority' is not a mere numerical notion referring to population. this purely mathematical element is qualified by the fact that a dominant position is being occupied or not. the institutional balance of power within the belgian federal government and the constitutional framework for language legislation in belgium means that the two major communities co-dominate. the dutch and french-speaking peoples cannot be considered a national minority within the meaning of the council of europe framework convention for the protection of national minorities, either in the whole or in part of the territory of belgium. the smaller german-speaking community can be considered as such if it so wishes. the proposals for legal acts that the commission may submit following the european citizens' initiative minority safepack should take into account that the case of the dutch and french-speaking people in belgium falls outside the scope of protection rules for traditional national minorities.
mr president, the values of respect for human dignity, freedom and democracy, as well as the rights and freedoms of national minorities, are undoubtedly just some of the fundamental values of the european union. the european citizens' initiative minority safepack can be an instrument to help protect the rights of national minorities, but any policy approach, especially at european level, must have a well-founded legal basis. according to the european union treaties, the protection of persons belonging to national minorities is part of the political and constitutional structure of each state, which is obliged to adopt national legislation to this effect. there is thus no legal basis in the treaties to allow the european commission to adopt legislative acts which may concern the rights of persons belonging to national minorities. i urge all relevant actors to follow the good examples of good practice in romania, where 17 national minorities have legal representation in parliament under special conditions, without meeting the electoral threshold.
mr. president, hilaria feels safer in the doctor's office if she's treated in her native language. aurelio too, when they explain a problem with his pension. that language was forbidden for half their lives. today, the basque language is official in the basque country, but so many years of persecution left their mark. that's why hilaria and aurelio, my parents, myself, we can't always use our language, sometimes or where we miss it most. that's why we need to protect our right to live in basque. diversity that generates development and strengthens the union. that is why we need and support the minority safepack, a european protection for the fundamental rights of more than 50 million europeans. it doesn't matter the strength of the language, the number, or the will of its speakers. the selective and narrow view of the diversity of some member states is imposed. only in this way can it be explained that languages which are more widely spoken than others are not official in this parliament, simply because they have a state. the commission has already made a number of proposals. we're here today to talk about minorities. i myself belong to the german-speaking minority in belgium. so i am a german-speaking belgian, i am belgian, i am european, and my identity is therefore multifaceted. one identity does not exclude the other, it even conditions it. protection of minorities is therefore not a matter of political attitude. protection of minorities is a fundamental right and the proper treatment of minorities is a matter of survival for the european union. but i want to focus on one specific element today, and that is geo-blocking. if i want to see a german film in my constituency, it is almost exclusively possible to do so on live television. in the media library, in the streaming services like netflix or in the platforms like apple, i'm simply blocked from accessing them for copyright reasons. unfortunately, the geo-blocking regulation has not improved this situation - quite the contrary! we must therefore urgently use the revision of the geoblocking regulation to draw up an exception for defined linguistic minorities. in my region in belgium or in south tyrol, for example, this would be possible without any problems, because these regions are clearly demarcated geographically. these areas could very easily be assigned to the immediately adjacent language area. ladies and gentlemen, we have succeeded in breaking down barriers in our border areas. we cannot allow these very borders to be artificially raised again on the internet.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, let me thank you for a very rich debate this evening, with very interesting perspectives and thought-provoking statements from different speakers. and i may add, i have heard something definitely new in certain areas, and this is definitely something we should look into and reflect on. but let me start by saying that for the commission, inclusion and respect for the rich cultural diversity of europe is one of its main priorities and objectives. ‘united in diversity’ is the motto of the european union. the commission confirms its commitment both to policy support and funding. respect for the rights of persons belonging to minorities is part of the founding values of the european union and its, if i may say so, dna. the principle is explicitly mentioned in the treaty on the european union. it’s not only compatible but part of our european identity. moreover, any discrimination on the basis of membership of a national minority is explicitly prohibited under article 21 of the union’s charter of fundamental rights. the adoption of the eu anti-racism action plan 2020-2025 is a clear example of the role the commission aims to play in this regard. another good example of concrete policy action involving minorities is the recently-adopted eu roma framework. as i indicated, this debate is timely, as the college has another few weeks to finalise the commission’s formal answer to the initiative. thank you again for this very fruitful, interesting and inspiring debate.
in writing. - my fellow members of parliament! i am pleased that the european parliament has put on the agenda the citizens' initiative minority safepack, which has been supported by over one million citizens across europe. it is important that the european union finally addresses effectively the initiative that affects more than 50 million indigenous citizens belonging to national minorities. in some european countries and regions, successful guarantees for the protection of minorities are already in place and can serve as an example. the european union has so far treated minority protection as a matter of subsidiarity, but unfortunately it is often the member states that do not guarantee equal rights for indigenous minorities, and the eu must set a good example and take on the role of protector. to this end, it is important that the european union develops a legal framework that promotes the survival of national minorities on its territory, the preservation of their mother tongue, culture and national identity. by developing such a legal framework and applying an appropriate control mechanism, the eu would demonstrate that it is truly acting to protect the rights of minorities. i am confident that over time, the initiative will have a positive impact on neighbouring and associated countries, and that the legal framework developed will ensure that citizens living in their territories are also granted their rights.
in writing. - my fellow members of parliament! we have reached a very important moment in the case of the rights of indigenous national minorities in europe. the success of the minority safepack european citizens' initiative, supported by over one million signatures, has given hope to the more than 50 million european citizens who feel they belong to a national, ethnic or linguistic minority. with such legitimacy, the european commission cannot afford to remain silent on a matter that affects more than 10% of the eu's population. the ostrich policy must end. in recent decades, the legal frameworks, policies and programmes developed at european level have become so interconnected that they can now directly affect the situation of citizens and the realisation of their rights. the european parliament resolution aims to encourage the european commission to take account of the situation and development of the rights of indigenous national minorities, among other factors, in the design of european policies and programmes. this is a unique opportunity, not only for the current european union and its citizens, but also a message to the candidate countries on the path to european integration that the european union takes the development of the rights of national and linguistic minorities seriously. here in the european parliament we have spoken out on many occasions to protect the rights of indigenous national and linguistic minorities, now it is the european commission's turn to act! thank you, sir.
in writing. europe's strength lies in its linguistic and cultural diversity of nations and national minorities, in addition to its christian-jewish cultural heritage. we should view this as a value, not a burden. linguistic diversity, multilingualism, is a huge advantage, not a disadvantage. i can attest to that as a bilingual deaf person. we have many positive examples in europe of the legal position of historical minorities, such as the swedes in finland or the german-speaking minorities in south tyrol. however, as an active member of the committee on petitions, i could also inform you about many of the abuses perpetrated by member states against national minorities, especially in terms of language. there is a classic double standard: while the copenhagen criteria are being closely monitored, monitored and held accountable in the candidate countries, there is no clear legal framework for the situation of indigenous national minorities in the eu member states. therefore, the initiative, as expressed in the minority safe pack, signed by over 1.2 million citizens, must be taken seriously and respected. i ask my colleagues to vote in favour of this resolution and to urge the european commission to respond to the legitimate demands of citizens with an ambitious legislative proposal.

in writing. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the council has reached a number of important agreements. i would like to join in the celebration of the fact that we have reached agreement on the future multiannual perspective. the european parliament has made a great effort to ensure that the mff documents are ready for adoption once agreement on the mff is reached. i am the rapporteur for the european globalisation adjustment fund myself and we will have the final multilateral negotiations on this fund this evening. i am very pleased that the member states see a common interest in better coordinating their actions during this difficult period. indeed, we all need more clarity and coherence in how we will proceed, what restrictions we can expect, what the requirements for travel will be. so far, we have seen only the actions of individual member states, which have created confusion and uncertainty. we need to have more or less the same rules and more information. i fully agree that covid-19 vaccines should be considered a public good and available to everyone without exception. i congratulate those member states that have already announced free vaccination, and that is a very responsible approach. in this difficult situation, we must remain focused and united and ensure the safety of our people so that we can return to normal life as soon as possible.
in writing. this new seven-year budget, if we were to evaluate it in the light of what the parties on the right wanted or what the european council's proposal was, we could say that it is a glass half full. the health budget is being cut back. research is less helpless. unfortunately, this is not how a budget is evaluated. the question cannot be: is this budget less bad than another? no, i'm not going. the question must be: does this budget respond to the social and climate emergency? does this budget take full advantage of the lessons of the health crisis? but on the social front, we have no guarantee that the new european tax will not be borne by the workers, nor will it break with the market. on the climate front, the targets have been raised, very well, but the means are not keeping up. and from a health point of view, in the midst of a pandemic, we are faced with a budget that gives more to the military than the health program. really, is this the budget that people need for the next seven years? i don't think he did.
in writing. mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the saga of this regulation introducing a rule of law conditionality in access to european budgetary resources is finally over. i am not the only one to say that the european union is not a money-changing machine. it is a club with strict rules, especially and above all for respecting a set of values: the rule of law, i.e. an independent and efficient judiciary, objective administrations, a free media that is not subject to the dictates of power and an effective fight against corruption, especially when it concerns the highest-ranking people, are essential pillars of european democracy. if these pillars are in danger, the european citizen cannot understand how the financial means can fuel the policies of the leaders who do not fight against, who are at the origin of, or even who benefit politically and financially from these practices. the veto of the polish and hungarian governments, whose countries are now strongly challenged in these areas, was certainly as revealing of their practices as any in-depth report on them. i am satisfied that the compromise reached, while regrettable, preserves the heart of the regulation intact.

in writing. mr president, this multiannual budget agreement proved to be a complex process, and its preparation and coordination required a great deal of time and patience. at the same time, the difficulties caused by the covid-19 pandemic made it more difficult than ever to agree on specific budget lines. we have indeed faced unprecedented challenges this year, and i am pleased that we can look forward to 2021 with some clarity on this issue. the budget implementation will present its own difficulties, but the negotiations were a great opportunity to secure additional funding for some union programmes. in particular, erasmus+ and horizon projects, both of which are highly relevant to latvia's interests. moreover, the budget will also have a positive impact on the rail baltica project, which will connect latvia with its baltic neighbours. i call on my colleagues to support the implementation of this budget and its swift implementation.
the agreement between the european council and the european parliament, which allowed the adoption of a powerful recovery stimulus package with a european union solidarity dimension in the face of the impact of the pandemic, is a sign of the vitality of the european project. i would highlight in this agreement the overall volume of eur 1.8 billion allocated to the various programmes, the eur 16 billion with which parliament was able to strengthen flagship programmes in the last round of negotiations, the inclusion of a formal and mandatory plan for the introduction of own resources as a key tool of the solidarity-based financing model, the establishment of ambitious cross-cutting targets for the allocation of funds to climate and biodiversity policies, the strengthening of the european parliament's monitoring capacity and its ability to act as a budgetary authority, and the adoption of a rule of law guarantee mechanism. a rapid and effective implementation process is now essential to tackle inequalities, create wealth and jobs and position the european union as a positive reference in the new global geopolitical framework through the energy transition, digital transition and economic modernisation.

(video presentation released in the media) ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, presidents of the institutions, authorities, i am very honoured and pleased to welcome the winners of the sakharov prize of the european parliament, who represent the democratic opposition in belarus. i welcome sviatlana tsikanouskaya and veronika tsepkala, who are with me in the gallery. i also welcome siarhei dyleuski, ales bialiatski and stisapan putsila, who have been appointed to the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights. and i also warmly welcome svetlana alexievich, who is following the ceremony from a distance, as many other members of the european parliament are doing. unfortunately, not everyone was able to attend. sergei tikhanovsky is still in prison for the sole offence of wanting to exercise his right to stand for election, as are mikola statkevich and maria kalesnikava, who are represented by their relatives maria adamovic and tatiana khomich. i invite you to think about this prize. i am very honoured to have you with me in the european parliament today. over the last six months, you have shown the world, ladies and gentlemen, what it really means to not give up. you have stood up for your rights and you have not given up the fight, despite the pain, the suffering, the fear. as nelson mandela said, courage is not the absence of fear, but the triumph over fear. your courage and strength of spirit paved the way for the revolution that took power in the country in 2020. we honor you, we pay tribute to you today, for your endurance and perseverance. for us, the sakharov prize ceremony is an important moment. human rights defenders and democracy activists are our role models and, as a sign of the utmost respect we have for them, we have organised this prize, despite the current difficulties. members of parliament from all 27 countries of the union are partly connected to us by remote means and partly present in the chamber to welcome your testimony. in the age of social media, it is difficult to keep information hidden and i want to convey to you, but also to all citizens of belarus, the message that we, as well as the whole world, are fully aware of what is happening in your country. we see your courage, the courage of women. we see the suffering of your people. we see the unspeakable abuses. we see violence. your aspiration and your determination to live in a democratic country, in freedom, and all this inspires us. we support you in your struggle. i'd like to pay a special tribute to our winners and the strength you've shown. however brutal and ruthless the methods of the government and however strong the fear they instill, this cannot withstand the protests of the citizens of all the people of belarus. "we have woken up and we will not sleep again", you said during the unity marches. it's true, you know. belarus is not afraid anymore. and that's something that the authorities, the government, have understood. the belarusian people have shown that no authoritarianism can block change and the opening of a new chapter in the history of your country. ladies and gentlemen, your struggle is our struggle and this is not the first time that we have awarded a collective prize to sakharov prize winners. in 2004, the european parliament awarded the sakharov prize to the belarusian association of journalists for defending press freedom. i would also like to congratulate them on their constant commitment and their spirit of sacrifice in informing and informing us and on the spirit of sacrifice they have shown over the past few months. in 2006 we awarded the prize to aliaksandr milinkevich, who was then the leader of the united democratic opposition in belarus, and we remember him fondly. the eu's message is clear: the belarusian people must fully enjoy their democratic rights and live in freedom. we in the european union have a moral duty to support them. we also have a duty to support all those around the world who are fighting the same fight as you. before i give the floor to sviatlana tsikhanouskaya, i would also like to congratulate and send a message of support to this year's finalists: environmental activists from guapinol and berta cáceres from honduras and monsignor najeeb michaeel, archbishop of mosul. i can't finish without mentioning our sakharov prize finalists who are still in prison and calling for their release: ilham tothi, raif badawi, nasrin sotoudeh, who has just been imprisoned again by the iranian authorities, despite her fragile, very fragile health. we greet them all and we are always and constantly close to them. (applause) thank you for your attention and participation and i now turn to sviatlana tsikhanouskaya for her speech.
winner of the 2020 sakharov prize for freedom of thought. – mr president, members of the european parliament, ladies and gentlemen, this year the world has seen belarus awakening. we stood for our freedom, dignity, national identity, but we faced the brutal nature of the regime. two realities confronted. peaceful bright people with flags and flowers against armed men in black hiding their faces behind balaclavas. we feel humbled that our story of resistance touched so many people across the globe. on your way to the european parliament, you could see a shocking photo on the façade of the building. a woman in white falling down to her knees before the riot police. it’s not a wartime movie scene, it’s a ‘peaceful’ belarus in 2020. people locked inside a cathedral by special forces. it’s a ‘peaceful’ belarus. detainees marked with different colours to determine how severely they should be beaten by the police officers, and it is a ‘peaceful’ belarus. deliberately infecting prisoners with covid—19. it is a ‘peaceful’ belarus – in the heart of europe. fear is what one feels hearing those stories. i am one of the thousands of belarusians who used to be afraid. an invisible wall of fear was built around us. this wall of fear held us back for almost three decades. but this year everything has changed. this year we united in our aspiration for change. united, we believed that this wall of fear can be taken down, brick by brick. united, we overcome our fear on the way to freedom, day by day, week by week, month by month. the photo on the façade of the european parliament is not a story of fear, it is a story of hope, courage and freedom. for the third time in 20 years, belarusians have been honoured with the sakharov freedom of thought prize. what is better recognition that we are freethinkers? what is better motivation for us to keep going? we are bound to win, and we will win. a simple wish to walk freely in the streets of our cities and towns keeps us going. desire to celebrate holidays with our families keeps us going. the dream of a better future for belarus keeps us going. a belarus where justice and truth prevail. a belarus that our children can be proud of. i am endlessly honoured to speak in front of you. we receive this prize together with the brave belarusians maria kalesnikava, veranika tsapkala, sviatlana aleksievich, volha kavalkova, ales bialiatski, mikola statkevich, siarhei tsikhanouski, siarhei dyleuski, stsiapan putsila and all the members of the coordination council. (applause)but we are not alone here today. beside us are thousands of belarusians who inspire us, give us strength and hope. many of them are not here today. our courageous women, workers, students, doctors, teachers, lawyers, writers, journalists and activists – all of them are in belarus fighting for our freedom. it is to them that this prize belongs. (applause)last sunday, i met with our distinguished belarusian writer sviatlana aleksievich, who is with us remotely today. sviatlana received the nobel prize for telling the stories of a ‘little big man’ in the light of historic events. this is how she created ‘a monument to suffering and courage in our time’. for me, such a ‘little big man’ is each and every belarusian who takes part in this peaceful protest against violence and lawlessness. each of them is a hero. each of them is an example of courage, compassion, and dignity to me. freedom is made of simple things and often requires fearless resistance. for aliaksandr taraikouski it was protesting against the falsified election. he stood raising his bare hands in front of men with rifles and he was shot dead. fearless he was. for the artist raman bandarenka it was going out to protect the national symbols in the courtyard where neighbours gather. he was kidnapped and beaten to death in the police office. fearless he was. the journalist katsiaryna barysevich and the doctor artsiom sarokin dared to unveil the truth about this murder by the regime cronies. this bravery cost their freedom. fearless they are. natalia hershe could not stay on the sidelines and came back to belarus after living abroad for many years. out of solidarity, she participated in peaceful protests and was sentenced to two and a half years in prison for taking the mask off the police. fearless she is. freedom is victory over fear. seventy-three-year-old nina baginskaya …(applause) … has become a symbol of our struggle. despite countless detentions and intimidation, she has been protesting against injustice for 30 years already, often alone. truly free she is. mikola statkevich …(applause)… was a presidential candidate in the 2010 election. he has been imprisoned and recognised as a political prisoner for the third time in 15 years. his commitment to the values of freedom is something that no prison sentence can take away. truly free he is. my friend maria kalesnikava tore up her passport and chose prison over exile to remain in belarus, with our people. truly free she is. my thoughts are with her every day. my husband siarhei tsikhanouski decided to tell the truth no matter the cost. for that, he was thrown in jail on trumped—up charges. he has not seen his family for seven months already. but these are only some of the courageous people our european nation is extremely proud of. freedom is also universal. belarusians have been marching every week since the 9 august election. they march for their future and the future of those who cannot be there. they march for the freedom and dignity of belarusians, of europeans – yours, and ours. without a free belarus, europe is not fully free either. we ask europe and the whole world to stand with belarus. we call on europe to be braver in their decisions and support the people of belarus now, not tomorrow, and not sometime in the future. standing for human rights and democracy is not interference but is the duty of each self—respecting country. your solidarity and your voice are important but it is actions that matter. please, be there for the belarusians and be assured that belarusians will do the same for you in the future. i have only one wish this year. i want every belarusian who is now in jail or was forced to live in exile to return home. the third sakharov prize will mark a new beginning for belarus. nelson mandela, who received the prize in 1988 together with the soviet dissident anatoly marchenko, said: ‘i learned that courage was not the absence of fear, but the triumph over it’. he believed that brave are those who conquer their fear. only united we can fully conquer fear on our long path to freedom and build a new belarus. every country has its own path to democracy, and this is ours. long live europe! long live belarus! zhiv’e bielarus! (sustained applause)
the president of the council. the commission has already made a number of proposals. we are currently experiencing a health crisis of enormous proportions worldwide - but especially in europe. in the debate this morning we sent a clear signal that we want to emerge from this crisis in solidarity and together, that we are above all trying to mitigate the serious social and economic consequences of the pandemic as much as possible, with a large programme, with a multiannual financial framework - with a lot of money, but also with a lot of commitment. we all know, of course, that's not enough. in these days and weeks we are witnessing a second, sometimes even a third wave in many member states of the european union. in my own country - germany - we have more dead than ever before. today, a sad high point: 950 people. several hundred thousand people have already died, millions of people are infected, some seriously ill. we have introduced restrictive measures in our member states to protect citizens as much as possible. but one drug, hopefully soon, will help us say we've contained this pandemic, and that's a vaccine. only a vaccine can defeat the virus. but there is still a lot of work to be done. since this virus has no passport, since this virus does not care about national borders, it was necessary from the very beginning to develop european strategies. we've done that in the vaccine business. first of all, i would like to express my great respect and gratitude to the researchers and companies in europe who helped us develop such a vaccine in a very short time. two vaccines are due to be approved in the eu soon. because we know that only the development, manufacture and deployment of safe and effective vaccines will be a lasting and sustainable solution in the fight against this serious health crisis. and we've decided to take a common approach. i know that there is great concern among our people about the fact that it has already been approved in some countries - in the united kingdom, in israel, but also in the united states of america. we have not focused on national strategies, but have said that we are going to follow a common european path through the ema. we do not want to enter into a competition between member states, but we want the vaccine to be made available to all citizens of the european union at the same time. this will not be easy, because of course, at the beginning, after the approval, which hopefully will be very soon, possibly as early as december 21, there are not enough vaccines to vaccinate all the people who need it right away. this too is disputed. there are discussions in all member states about who should be vaccinated first. and there are states that are debating whether to introduce mandatory vaccination. the priority in vaccination plans is always the elderly, health care workers, and people with pre-existing conditions. these are the main target groups that are given priority in member states' vaccination strategies. i am very grateful to the commission for having succeeded in getting us to generate an option for almost two billion doses of vaccines. it is clear that, wherever people live in the european union, with our joint efforts coordinated by the commission, they will really get the protection to which they are entitled. i hope that we will also be able to make these accesses available as free of charge as possible. let me mention another important point that has been raised a little bit in the debate about who's first and who's fastest: communication. i don't know about you, mr. member. i'm sometimes amazed at campaigns - including social media - that scare people into getting vaccinated. the disease itself is certainly far more dangerous than any vaccination. but we must be careful not to give the impression that the relevant approval and licensing procedures are subject to political influence. we must give citizens confidence. we have to convince them that getting as many people vaccinated as possible -- 60 to 70 percent -- is the only guarantee that an entire society can be protected. finally, post-authorisation surveillance data collection and formal surveillance studies are key elements of a vaccination programme, so that everyone knows what can happen in the event of side effects and so that there are no new fears. we have to take this very, very seriously. but, ladies and gentlemen, we have not only been concerned about the safety and protection of our own citizens in the eu, but we have also, of course, taken into account the global dimension of this crisis, and i am grateful for that too. the commission has joined and contributed to the so-called covax facility. that means we are practicing international solidarity. we know that the only way to protect our citizens permanently from this pandemic is to ensure that our neighbours, the countries with which we cooperate and exchange, can also successfully combat this serious pandemic. it is therefore in our interest to provide financial and organisational support to those countries which do not have the financial capacity that the european union has, and i am grateful for that. so our overarching common goal is to accelerate the development, manufacture and delivery of vaccines so that all people everywhere have access to vaccines at affordable prices. yes, vaccinations are not a miracle weapon that will allow us to return to normal life immediately. the old normal will no longer exist. there will have to be a new normal. we must continue to work together for the common good and in our daily lives, we must continue to follow the rules we have agreed to: wear masks, keep distance, observe the hygiene rules and, especially now, during the christmas holidays, even if it is difficult for us, take responsibility for our neighbours, for our loved ones. we should also set a good example as politicians.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, we are living in turbulent times. the health situation across europe is extremely uncertain. the impact of the pandemic on our economies and societies is unprecedented and the threat unfortunately remains very real. as we approach this end-of-year holiday season, it is only natural that families and friends wish to travel and spend time together. but make no mistake. we are still very much in the middle of this pandemic and we cannot afford to be complacent. yet the prospect of a covid—19 vaccine provides some light at the end of the tunnel. allow me to stress once again the three golden principles that are guiding the commission’s work in this field, namely equal access, affordability and safety. in recent months, the commission has been negotiating intensively to build a diversified portfolio of vaccines at fair prices and is working at full speed to review their safety and make them available. as you know, in europe the decision on the authorisation of these vaccines is not in the hands of politicians nor the hands of institutions, but in the hands of independent regulatory agencies, in our case the european medicines agency (ema). as i think you know by now, we are aiming to authorise the pfizer—biontech vaccine by the end of the year. the ema has already announced its intention to provide for an authorisation next monday. if this is the case, the commission is ready to provide the formal authorisation and placing on the market in supersonic fast procedures. in two days we can do the formal authorisation that normally, for this type of authorisation for placing on the market, can take two months. this means that this vaccine will be the christmas gift for all europeans if everything goes well. we also know that, before mid—january, we will also have an opinion from ema on the other vaccine by moderna. ema is also now currently conducting rolling reviews on the vaccines produced by astrazeneca and johnson & johnson. once authorised, we will secure access to these vaccines for all eu citizens at the same time. but, for this to happen, member states need to have all preparations in place and activate their national vaccination plans. we already provided guidance in october in that respect and this is being done in a coordinated manner. all our member states are making great efforts to put in place robust vaccination plans. these include identification of priority groups to be vaccinated first, ensuring solid supply chains, logistics and infrastructure, training staff or developing electronic vaccination registries. there is still much to do and certainly many challenges to overcome, but the commission stands ready and committed to help. we are also providing support to member states on communication around covid—19 vaccines. as someone who fought against the virus, not only as a member of the college, but also from a hospital bed, i wish to say that nobody should underestimate or negate the threat we are facing nor the usefulness of the vaccines. it is important that our citizens understand that we have not cut corners on safety and the vaccines do not pose a risk. europe will only authorise vaccines for our citizens under the sole condition that they are safe. building trust is of course key to ensure a high vaccination uptake and everybody knows by now that this is, and should be, our collective priority, because at the end of the day vaccines in themselves do not save lives. vaccination does. we must also remember that the european union has a duty to the world. we know that no one is safe until everyone is safe and that is why we aim to ensure universal and equitable access not only to vaccines, but also to diagnostics and therapeutics for all, including the most vulnerable populations living in the poorest and most fragile countries. no one should and no one will be left behind. europe has been leading the international solidarity efforts to establish the covax facility. team europe, our institutions and our member states, is now the largest contributor to covax to purchase and deliver vaccines to low-income countries as part of a collective objective to buy two billion vaccine doses by the end of next year. the european union is deeply and firmly committed to multilateralism and joint coordination with multilateral institutions. we are the european union and in our values we are guided by the principles of solidarity and unity of purpose. as such, we will donate under the team europe approach – we and our member states – a proportion of our vaccines to our western balkan partners in our neighbourhood, as well as for humanitarian assistance and to frontline health workers in the poorest countries. this is a temporary solution to bridge the time gap until vaccines from covax are broadly available and we are currently working on a methodology to make this happen. we will also ensure – and i know that this is an issue of interest to many of you – that intellectual property rights are not an obstacle to universal access to vaccines, including in developing countries that have no production capacity or have limited financial resources. last but not least, as the european union, we need to advance rapidly on getting our own european health union off the ground. several proposals are already on the table. others will follow. i am grateful to this house for your consistent support for all these initiatives from the commission. i wish to emphasise the importance of remaining vigilant. whilst we advance our work on vaccines – and we are now getting to the later stage – it is essential to keep reducing transmission of the virus through effective testing, contact tracing and isolation until the immediate danger is behind us. responsibility, both collective and individual, remains essential to get our lives and businesses restarted, but also to reduce the pressure on our heroic healthcare workers, whose immense efforts in the last months need to be not only acknowledged, but compensated. our european way of life has been severely tested in these eight months, but it did not collapse. it resisted. our resilient societies, our resilient health systems and the universality of our schools and education proved that, although we are tested we can manage, and we did manage because we are in this together. (applause)
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i'm very angry today, but i'm also very grateful. i am very grateful to the intensive care workers across europe who are fighting for the lives of people with covid-19 and other illnesses who are really at the edge, physically and mentally. they deserve our solidarity, and we owe it to them that we all follow the rules and that we as politicians make rules that help. and i think we should look not at britain, but at ireland. ireland has managed to have the lowest numbers in europe and in some cases, in some days, no patients have died from covid-19. i'm very angry at the people who are still downplaying the coronavirus and saying that you don't need a vaccine at all. but i am also angry at some politicians and unfortunately also some journalists who are discrediting the process of approval in europe, mostly without any knowledge of the facts - unfortunately not only the tabloid media, but also the mainstream media. and i'm grateful to those who are working day and night at companies like biontech and the european medicines agency to get us a safe vaccine as quickly as possible. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. keep going! please help us! we are here to support you, dear businesses and dear staff of the european medicines agency!
the second wave of the pandemic has already caused more deaths than the first. since 1 august, the virus has claimed the lives of more than 152 000 people in europe, up from 136 000 in the first wave. our thoughts are with the victims and their families. after months of suffering and pain, we see the light at the end of the tunnel. thanks to the concerted action of the commission and the support of the member states, we have achieved equitable access to vaccine doses. this achievement demonstrates what we are capable of when we speak and work with one voice. we now need to articulate a common vaccination process. and as we continue to fight the virus, we must have an equally ambitious response in the realm of international cooperation. if we want vaccines to be effective, they have to be produced at scale, distributed equally around the world, and affordable for everyone. ensuring that all people can be immunised at the same time is a humanitarian imperative and a precondition for ensuring well-being. the european union's support for the covax global consortium, which is promoted by the world health organisation, is essential for vaccination in the most vulnerable countries. as long as the disease resists in one place, it will remain a threat to all other places. the establishment of strong and inclusive health systems must be a priority of the health union, but also a fundamental pillar of our development cooperation programmes. now more than at any time in the last 70 years, we need to be inspired by the principles of international solidarity and cooperation. let us make the story of the european union's vaccine a success story that can restore hope to european citizens and to the rest of humanity.
on behalf of the renew group. madam president, vaccines have indeed been what has allowed us to fight infections and prevent millions of deaths, and it will be the vaccine or vaccines against covid-19 that can really end this pandemic. i would therefore like to say to the commission that the common vaccination strategy is a success story, because thanks to this strategy we have been able to make pre-contracts, we have been able to make joint purchases; i hope we will be able to make joint vaccinations in that week of vaccination that was discussed in the council. and this has been because we have never in history seen public-private funding on this scale to accelerate drug discovery. we have invested a lot of public money to accelerate the technical knowledge that already existed, and it's important that european citizens know this, because the best vaccination strategy is for citizens to get vaccinated, and for citizens to get vaccinated, they have to have confidence. and the european union has been far from the 'marketing coup'. we haven't made any political career, because we haven't eliminated any safety requirements for vaccines to be approved. that is why the european medicines agency works on scientific and technical criteria, outside of pressure or any other political time. when the european vaccine is authorised in the member states, it will be a safe vaccine. no one is safe until we're all safe. that is why, commissioner, covax is good, but it is not enough. when there's that much public money, we can really make this vaccine a public good, global, universal, for everyone.
for the greens/efa group madam president, i have several questions for you today. the first concerns europe. when will you allow members to consult the contracts signed with industry so that we can at least learn about the liability clauses before the start of the vaccination of citizens? as for the external dimension of the strategy, by refusing within the wto the possibility for some countries to lift intellectual property rights for covid-19-related medical products, the union reneged on its commitment to help the lowest-income countries. so i ask the commission to justify this position and, to avoid any misrepresentation, while the commission is advocating voluntary mechanisms, can it tell us whether any of the companies that have signed advance purchase contracts for the production of the vaccine, and thus benefited from european funds, have committed themselves to support the union's initiative for non-exclusive licences?

madam president, the start of vaccination does not offset the huge shortcomings in public health systems and inadequate government measures. the european union and governments, in the context of commodity health, are treating the vaccine as just another source of profit for the monopolies. pharmaceutical companies were subsidized for all stages of research, manufacturing, pre-marketing, and marketing of the vaccine. they hide research results from each other, preventing interdisciplinary cooperation which is not allowed in capitalist competition. it is questionable why the issue of treatments and drugs that seem to help against the virus is not being promoted. the commission's refusal to submit documents from the european parliamentary group of the communist party of greece to the european parliament to reveal the contracts concluded by the european union with the companies, in particular to make the people aware of what has been agreed with their own money, raises questions. vaccines must be safe and free. against conspiracy theories, they must be a conscious act and a requirement of the people to protect their health according to the recommendations of scientists and as advised by doctors.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the vaccines are coming. that's the good news. but will they be available to every man and woman? will they be able to be distributed everywhere? will we be able to provide the medical and practical conditions everywhere in the world? the question marks behind these sentences that's the negative message that we can't answer that yet. and let me emphasize one thing: yes, we can beat this virus! and we are full of gratitude to all those who help us to do this in our home countries, to do this in europe. but we're only going to do it if we do it globally, if we do it with a global strategy, with a global vaccination policy. and to do that, we need an additional $5 billion next year to get our foot in the door in the global south and help the people there. no, it cannot fail on patents! we urgently need agreements with the companies - yes - and where necessary, exceptional rules, so that the vaccines can be made available everywhere. and for the european commission, commissioner, it is a great opportunity to include a global health policy as a core element in the work programme for next year and to turn this crisis, with the guidance and active assistance of the european union, into a great opportunity for a global health policy in the interest of us all. that's the moral, that's the medical imperative.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, europe has been suffering from the effects of the coronavirus for almost a year. to end this disease, we need a health policy that is protective, effective and transparent - the opposite of what the commission is proposing. firstly, while europeans have never been so opposed to vaccination, you have decided to make the negotiations with the laboratories opaque and not to make public the clauses exempting them from liability in the event of side effects. you have prevented us from consulting these numerous contracts except in a room without a telephone, paper or pen. i'm still waiting for permission to go in. this decision prevents us from carrying out our mandate as democratically elected members of parliament, which you are not. secondly, you are ready to validate the marketing of the messenger rna vaccines, which have never been used in a mass vaccination campaign, and this after only a few weeks of testing. in view of the urgency of the situation and the precautionary principle, we should, like the chinese and russians, give preference to more conventional vaccines, such as the flu vaccine, for example, in order to protect the health of europeans, and also to avoid any conflict of interest. i hope, madam president, that we will not be the butt of the joke, by being the last to receive and deploy the coronavirus vaccine.

for the greens/efa group mr president, being and feeling safe in the anxious and upsetting context we are currently experiencing is fundamental and implies, in particular, resilience in the face of terrorism and all forms of crime, but also trust in our institutions and law enforcement, which guarantee our fundamental freedoms and rights in the face of arbitrariness both online and offline. inequalities, gender and domestic violence, police violence, racist and xenophobic crimes, respect for the rule of law and corruption are among the issues that a union security strategy must tackle. security challenges must be seen through the lens of education, culture and socio-economic policies, in short, articles 2 and 3 of the treaty on european union. parliament called on the commission to include these dimensions in its strategy. in this context, the reform of europol, as well as the strengthening of the counter-terrorism agenda without an assessment of security policies and their impact on fundamental rights and without enhanced democratic control are not acceptable. an ever more secure agenda, which satisfies the populist appetite for increased border controls, whose human consequences are europe's shame. the european union is also committed to the development of a common defence policy, which is based on the principles of the treaty of rome and the treaty of rome. this agenda is taking the path towards which terrorists and extremists want to push us despite respect for individual freedoms and fundamental rights. let us be clear: we do not accept that the european union should go down this road.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, the threats around us evolve very fast, but our response is way too slow. the terror attacks of recent months and years were mostly perpetrated by islamic extremists acting alone. we need, of course, a stronger role for europol and more effective cross-border information sharing but this is not enough. it must be matched with measures that tackle the heart of a dangerous ideology that permeates our society – radical islam. this is why the commission and member states are right to strengthen their efforts to tackle radicalisation. we must take steps to root out those who use religion and ideology to incite acts of violence. in order to do that we must cast aside political correctness and understand that defending our values must be central to any action. we need the resources, political will and policies, but above all, we need courage – the courage to be proud of who we are, the courage to defend our society and way of life. this renewed security union must deliver that.
author. – madam president, can you imagine what it must be like to be forced to be sterilised, to be forced to undergo abortion, to be tortured, to be used as a slave, to have your children taken away from you? because that is the reality for so many uyghurs and other muslim minorities in the xinjiang uyghur autonomous region. twenty percent of the world’s cotton production originates from xinjiang, often harvested with the use of forced labour. the supply chain for textile products, including masks sold in our pharmacies here in europe, are part of an economy that is based on forced labour and a lot of exploitation and suffering. our companies cannot simply turn a blind eye and claim ignorance. they have a duty to better monitor their supply chains. china is not simply an economic partner; it is a country edging towards the definition of carrying out genocide. the eu and member states must put in place effective controls and place an import ban on chinese products made using forced labour. the eu should enforce targeted sanctions against chinese officials responsible for overseeing these shocking crimes. it’s time to stop talking and practice what we actually believe.
president. - i should like to begin by thanking the vice-president for getting the withdrawal agreement back on track and making it operational. i would also like to thank mr barnier and his negotiating team - he has left us for understandable reasons - for their months of commitment and exhausting negotiations which are very demanding on a human being. he has done so with great tenacity, with lucidity, with the necessary pragmatism and with flexibility. my question is that everything would be done to reach an agreement in those last days, those last hours. the consequences, ladies and gentlemen, of a no deal are terrible for the european union. in total, we will lose 1.2 million jobs. all of this is on top of the coronary disaster. the united kingdom will lose more than half a million jobs. in my little flanders we are losing 2.6% of our gdp and 30,000 jobs. i would therefore ask the commission to make the brexit fund work quickly for the most affected regions, for the most affected sectors. i would also ask that the negative effects of brexit be offset somewhat and that new trade agreements be accelerated, which we will absolutely need. i think, ladies and gentlemen, that it must be possible to reach an agreement with pragmatism, with flexibility, to strike a balance between the sovereignty of the british and the integrity of our internal market, which they must respect. there are mechanisms for state aid, for the rules of competition, for maintaining our standards, for governance. the same applies to fisheries, where we recognise sovereignty, but where we must have sufficient access, sufficient quotas and, above all, legal certainty for our fishermen. i agree with mr barnier that we must think about the future, that we must go for a post-brexit partnership not only on defence and security, but also on research and development, on erasmus+, university cooperation and cooperation in and around the north sea, for the blue economy: the 21st century will be the century of the oceans. i think, ladies and gentlemen, that we should also work on interparliamentary cooperation with the british once brexit is over. we too are going to have to learn from this brexit. this can't happen again, this misery. article 50 will have to be revised. i hope it will never be used again. but whoever leaves must come with a proposal, with a blueprint. we ourselves will have to do more to counter populism, and the eu will have to make progress on the internal market, jobs and prosperity, trade agreements and a comprehensive migration policy.
mr president, on the eve of the real brexit, perhaps three quick lessons. firstly, the workers in britain, tired of social dumping, saw no alternative in this european competition union. the social europe remained a slogan. secondly, boris johnson, who promised them better working conditions and better wages if they were to gain independence, has been fighting for the right to lower wages and lower working conditions for british workers for a year now. can you imagine that? and finally, a lesson for flanders. in flanders, smes will suffer. it may cost us thousands of jobs and the working conditions in the port of antwerp will put pressure on the dock workers. and yet the flemish belang applauded this brexit as loud as the loudest idiots we could say. but no, they know what they're doing. their nationalism, your nationalism, mr annemans, is leading to social breakdown, to the breakdown of social security and to lower wages. and i 'll tell you straight up: it won't pass! no way, mr. annemans, not a chance!
